                                                             Spectral Dual Fitting for k-Means
                                            Aditya Anand1 , Moses Charikar2 , Vincent Cohen-Addad3 , Ruiquan Gao2 ,
                                         Fabrizio Grandoni4 , Euiwoong Lee1 , Amatya Sharma1 , and Ernest van Wijland5
                                                1
                                                    University of Michigan, {adanand,euiwoong,amatya}@umich.edu
                                                      2
                                                        Stanford University, {moses,ruiquan}@cs.stanford.edu
                                                               3
                                                                 Google Research, cohenaddad@google.com

                                                          5

arXiv:2607.14654v1 [cs.DS] 16 Jul 2026




                                                                                    Abstract
                                           We give a new dual ﬁtting algorithm which gives improved approximation ratios of 3 +
                                           ln 2 + ε (≈ 3.694) and 4.9 + ε for k-Means in (high-dimensional) Euclidean and general
                                           metrics respectively, improving upon the previously known ratios of 4+ε [Charikar, Cohen-
                                           Addad, Gao, Grandoni, Lee, and van Wijland STOC’26] and 5 + ε [Byrka, Guo, Hu, Li,
                                           Wan, Wang FOCS’26], resp. In particular, our result for Euclidean k-Means breaks the
                                           hardness barrier of 1 + 8/e ≈ 3.94 for Metric k-Means. Prior to our work, no such
                                           separation between general and Euclidean metrics was known for k-Median, k-Means, or
                                           Facility Location in terms of their approximability.
                                               Unlike prior dual ﬁtting approaches for k-Means, our new dual ﬁtting algorithm tightly
                                           accounts for dual payments while still facilitating an eﬀective dual feasibility analysis. We
                                           introduce a new framework that uses spectral analysis for determining the approximation
                                           factor of our algorithm.

1        Introduction
Clustering is one of the most fundamental tasks in data science with wide-ranging appli-
cations across various domains, including unsupervised learning, text and image analysis,
recommender systems, and bioinformatics. (Metric) k-Means is one of the most well-studied
clustering objectives. Given a collection of n clients D, a collection F of potential center
locations in a metric space deﬁned by distances d(., P .), and an integer k ∈ [n], the goal is
to select k centers S ⊆ F such that conn(S) := j∈D d2 (j, S) is minimized, where d(j, S)
is the distance of point j to its nearest center in S. A very important special case is Eu-
clidean k-Means, where D are points in a Euclidean space, and F is implicitly given by any
point in the Euclidean space.1 By standard techniques [Mat00; DKKR03] and losing only
a (1 + ε) factor in the approximation ratio for any constant ε > 0, we can restrict F to a
polynomial-time-computable subset of points. W.l.o.g., we will also assume that D ⊆ F .
    From a theoretical standpoint, the study of clustering problems through the lens of
(polynomial-time) approximation algorithms has been extremely successful, yielding new al-
gorithms and analysis techniques.
                           P          In particular, k-Means and the closely related k-Median
(whose objective function j∈D d(j, S) is the sum of non-squared distances) are two problems
that have received the most attention. For k-Median, there have been gradual developments
over the last three decades using various algorithmic techniques, including primal LP round-
ing [CGTS99; CL12], primal-dual [JV01], greedy algorithm with dual ﬁtting analysis [JMS02;
JMM+ 03; CGLS23], local search [AGK+ 04], and bipoint rounding [LS13; BPR+ 15; GPST23].
The current best approximation ratio is (2 + ε), achieved by [CGL+ 25]. It is known that one
cannot improve the approximation ratio beyond 1 + 2/e ≈ 1.73 [GK99].
    For k-Means, the picture has been much more elusive. While new algorithmic techniques
for k-Median often translate to k-Means, it takes nontrivial technical eﬀort, and the approxi-
mation ratios usually end up being much larger: this is because only approximate versions of
triangle inequalities are available for squared metrics, which present technical challenges for
some k-Median algorithms, most notably the ones based on dual ﬁtting.
    For Metric k-Means, [GT08] gave a 25-approximation using local search, which was im-
proved to a (9 + ε) approximation by [ANSW20] via primal-dual. Recently, the approxi-
mation factor was improved to 5.83 [CCG+ 25] via a novel dual ﬁtting algorithm. For Eu-
clidean k-Means, [KMN+ 04] gave a (9 + ε)-approximation using local search, followed by
improvements using primal-dual methods [ANSW20; GOR+ 22; CEMN22] and most recently
to 4 + ε [CCG+ 26] via dual ﬁtting. The hardness for the metric case is 1 + 8/e ≈ 3.94, while
for Euclidean metrics the hardness is 1.17 [CK19], and under a stronger conjecture called the
Johnson Coverage Hypothesis (JCH), the hardness can be strengthened to 1.73 [CSL22]. We
remark that we focus on the high-dimensional Euclidean case here, while better algorithms
are known for the low-dimensional one [FRS16; CKM16; Coh18].
    In this work, we give a new dual ﬁtting based algorithm that gives signiﬁcantly better
approximation ratios for both Euclidean k-Means and Metric k-Means. Our algorithm leads
to a 3 + ln 2 + ε (≈ 3.694) approximation for the Euclidean case and a 4.9 + ε approximation
for the metric case. Our result for Euclidean metrics is the ﬁrst algorithm that gives an
approximation ratio below the 3.94-hardness barrier for the metric case, showing that the
optimal approximation ratios for these two versions will be diﬀerent assuming P 6= NP. Such
a separation between general metrics and high-dimensional Euclidean metrics, even after
extensive recent work [CEMN22; CSL22; LS25], has not been achieved for k-Median and
    1
        Sometimes this is called the continuous Euclidean k-Means problem in the literature.



                                                         1
Facility Location.

Theorem 1.1. For any ε > 0, there is a polynomial-time algorithm that yields a (3+ln 2+ ε)-
approximation for Euclidean k-Means.

Theorem 1.2. For any ε > 0, there is a polynomial-time algorithm that yields a (4.9 + ε)-
approximation for Metric k-Means.

     Our main technical contribution is an improved Lagrangian Multiplier Preserving (LMP)
approximation for the following (Uncapacitated) Facility Location problem (with squared
distances and uniform opening costs), which we simply call Facility Location throughout
the paper: the input consists of a set of clients D, a collection of centers F , a (possibly
Euclidean) metric d on D ∪ F , and a facility opening cost f , and the goal is to select S ⊆ F
that minimizes f |S| + conn(S). In particular, S ⊆ F is called an LMP c-approximation if
c · f |S| + conn(S) ≤ c · OPTLP (f ), where OPTLP (f ) denotes the optimal cost of the standard
LP relaxation for Facility Location deﬁned in Section 3. Our algorithms yield LMP 4.9 and
(3 + ln 2)-approximations for Facility Location in general and Euclidean metrics, respectively.
     Our improved LMP approximation is obtained via a reﬁned dual ﬁtting algorithm that
tightly accounts for the cost incurred, while still being able to facilitate an improved one-
cluster based dual feasibility analysis. We also introduce a novel spectral analysis for k-Means,
departing from the usual factor-revealing linear programs. The spectral analysis provides a
more accurate method to analyze the squared objective in k-Means compared to the previous
approaches. More speciﬁcally, upper bounding the approximation factor reduces to upper
bounding the largest eigenvalue of a family of matrices. The latter task is highly non-trivial,
but it leads to substantially lower approximation factors compared to known methods.
     This is then combined with the two-step framework recently used in [CGL+ 25; CCG+ 25;
CCG+ 26] to obtain our approximation algorithm. For the ﬁrst step, building on our LMP
approximation, we use the idea of log-adaptive algorithms to obtain a good approximate
solution for k-Means that opens k + O(log n/ε3 ) centers. Let OPTk be the optimal cost for
an instance of k-Means.

Theorem 1.3. For any constant ε > 0, there is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given
an instance of k-Means, ﬁnds S ⊆ F with |S| = k + O(log n/ε3 ) satisfying conn(S) ≤ (Γ +
  √
O( ε)) · OPTk , where Γ = 3 + ln 2 for Euclidean metrics and Γ = 4.9 for general metrics.

    The second step of the framework studies stable instances. A k-Means instance is β-stable
if OPTk−1 ≥ (1 + β)OPTk , where OPTk−1 denotes the optimal cost of (k − 1)-Means with the
same clients and centers. We show that a slightly more careful analysis of the same algorithms
as in [CCG+ 25; CCG+ 26] yields the following results.

Theorem 1.4. For any constants ε, ζ > 0, there is a polynomial-time randomized algorithm
for Metric k-Means that with high probability returns a solution of cost at most (4 + ε)OPTk
assuming that the input instance is (ζ/ log n)-stable.

Theorem 1.5. For any constants ε, ζ > 0, there is a polynomial-time randomized algorithm
for Euclidean k-Means that with high probability returns a solution of cost at most (2+ε)OPTk
assuming that the input instance is (ζ/ log n)-stable.

   These results improve the (5 + ε)-approximation for general metrics and the (4 + ε)-
approximation for Euclidean metrics [CCG+ 25; CCG+ 26]. Similar to previous results [CGL+ 25;


                                               2
CCG+ 25; CCG+ 26], given Theorems 1.3 to 1.5, we can easily prove our main results The-
orem 1.1 and Theorem 1.2; given a k-Means instance, if it is not Ω(ε4 / log n)-stable as a
k ′ -Means instance for each k ′ = k, k − 1, . . . , k − O(log n/ε3 ), then OPTk−O(log n/ε3 ) ≈ OPTk
and we can use Theorem 1.3 with k ← k − O(log n/ε3 ). Otherwise, the instance is stable for
some k ′ in the above range, and we can use Theorems 1.4 and 1.5. The formal proof appears
in Section A.

Concurrent work. Concurrently and independently, Byrka, Guo, Hu, Li, Wan, and Wang [BGH+ 26]
gave an iterative randomized rounding framework for k-clustering with ℓpp -type assignment
costs. For Metric k-Means, their framework gives a (5 + ε)-approximation, improving the
previous 5.83-approximation. For Euclidean k-Means, they give a (4 + ε)-approximation and
prove an 11/3-LMP approximation. Their approach is LP-rounding based and is conceptually
diﬀerent from our dual-ﬁtting/spectral analysis. Our results have better approximation guar-
antees for both settings, giving (4.9 + ε) for Metric k-Means and (3 + ln 2 + ε) for Euclidean
k-Means.


2     Technical Overview
We start by revisiting the dual ﬁtting algorithm for Facility Location of the two papers by
Charikar et al. [CCG+ 25; CCG+ 26] for Metric and Euclidean k-Means, respectively. The two
algorithms are very similar, except for one diﬀerence that [CCG+ 26] lowers certain dual values
which can lead to further improvements. In this overview, this diﬀerence is not important,
but we will use the stronger algorithm from [CCG+ 26] which is still as good as the older
algorithm [CCG+ 25] for the metric case as well.
    We provide a description of their algorithm below.

Greedy Algorithm. The algorithm maintains a set A of active clients, a set S ⊆ F of open
facilities, and a partition of clients into three classes: active clients A, indirectly connected
clients IC, and directly connected clients DC. Intuitively, active clients are not yet connected
to an open facility, and they are actively growing their dual variables. Indirectly connected
clients are connected to an open facility, but they do not directly contribute (a strictly positive
amount) to the opening cost of that facility. On the other hand, directly connected clients are
connected to an open facility and they directly contribute to the opening cost of that facility.
Initially, A = D, IC = DC = S = ∅, and αj = 0 for every client j ∈ D. Fix a parameter
ρ > 1. We let fˆ = Γf where Γ is some constant that depends on ρ to be speciﬁed later, and
f is the (uniform) facility cost.
    As long as A 6= ∅, the algorithm increases uniformly the (dual) values αj for all clients
j ∈ A until one of the following two events occurs.

    1. A facility becomes tight. There exists an unopened facility i ∈ F \ S such that
                      X                      + X                          +
                            αj − ρ · d2 (j, i) +      ρd2 (j, S) − ρd2 (j, i) = fˆ.             (1)
                    j∈A∪IC                        j∈DC

      In this case, the algorithm opens facility i. Every client j ∈ DC with d2 (j, S) > d2 (j, i)
      is reconnected to i. For every client j ∈ A ∪ IC, the algorithm acts as follows:

         • if αj ≥ ρ · d2 (j, i), then j is connected (or reconnected) to i and moved to DC;


                                                 3
        • else, if αj ≥ d2 (j, i), then j is connected (or reconnected) to i, its value αj is
          lowered to d2 (j, i), and j is moved to IC if it was in A.

   2. An active client reaches its current service cost. For some client j ∈ A, we have

                                             αj = d2 (j, S).

      In this case, the client j is moved to IC and connected to its closest facility in S.
Intuitively, the quantities of type [·]+ above are bids that client j is willing to pay to open
facility i. Via these bids, j can contribute to the opening of multiple facilities. At the same
time, the residual dual αj of j after subtracting these bids is suﬃcient to connect j to the
closest open facility. The algorithm naturally deﬁnes a notion of time, such that at time θ,
all the active clients have α-value θ.
    Then, they show the following two cost accounting lemmas (similar to [JMM+ 03]) for this
algorithm (considering the values of α and S at the end of the algorithm), that lead to an
LMP Γ-approximation.

    Note that the RHS of this inequality is the cost of the computed solution (with facility
cost fˆ = Γ f ). The proof of this lemma follows from the fact that a facility is only opened
when it is fully paid for (by the bids), and the fact that for any client j, after subtracting any
bid to open facilities, the remaining part of αj can still pay for its connection cost d2 (j, S).

   Lemma 2.2 implies that (α/Γ) is dual-feasible for the Facility Location LP (see Dual FL
LP) with opening cost f . Combining this with Lemma 2.1, one gets

concluding that S is an LMP Γ-approximate solution.
    While the dual payment lemma more or less follows directly from the algorithm description,
the key challenge in [CCG+ 25; CCG+ 26] has been proving dual feasibility given the complexity
of the algorithm due to the presence of both IC and DC clients and the non-monotonicity of
the dual variables.

The barrier of previous works. Our ﬁrst observation is that there is a barrier for the
dual feasibility analysis of the algorithm of [CCG+ 26] for Euclidean metrics — a very simple
example shows that the LMP 4-approximation analysis of Lemma 2.2 can be tight for some
facility i. As illustrated in Figure 1, we will consider points on a line (i.e., 1-dimensional
Euclidean space). Let i be a facility and D∗ = {1, 2} located at xi = 0, x1 = −1, x2 = 1.
Then, d2 (1, i) + d2 (2, i) = 2. Let fˆ = 20. The algorithm simultaneously increases α1 and α2
until [α1 − ρd2 (1, i)]+ + [α2 − ρd2 (2, i)]+ = [α1 − 2]+ + [α2 − 2]+ = 20, i.e., when α1 = α2 = 12.

                                                 4
At this time point, suppose that client 1 gets directly connected to the only open facility at
xi′ = −3 (which was opened via bids from client 1 and other clients not in D∗ ). This leads to
d2 (1, S) = 4. From now on, the bid of client 1 to facility i becomes 8 − 2 = 6. We will keep
increasing α2 until one of the following holds:
   1. α2 ≥ d2 (2, S) = (x2 − xi′ )2 = 16. In this case, we have client 2 indirectly connected to
      i′ .
  2. 6 + [α2 − 2]+ = 20, which means α2 = 16. In this case, we have i opened and client 2
     directly connected to i.
                   P                                                      ˆ      2
Therefore, we have       ∗ αj = 12 + 16 = 28. On the other hand, we have f + 4(d (1, i) +
                               j∈D
d2 (2, i)) = 20 + 4 · 2 = 28. Hence, the dual feasibility is tight for this example, and we cannot
hope for an LMP approximation ratio below 4 using this algorithm.

Figure 1: Barrier example for the previous dual-feasibility analysis. The facility i is at 0, the
two clients are at −1 and 1, and an already open facility i′ is at −3. Thus d2 (1, i) = d2 (2, i) = 1,
while d2 (1, i′ ) = 4 and d2 (2, i′ ) = 16.


An issue with the payments. Our new algorithm is inspired by the fact that while the
dual feasibility
       P         can be tight, the dual payment Lemma 2.1 does not tightly account for the
costs: j αj may actually be larger than the cost of the algorithm. In particular, we observe
that for any client that is directly connected at the end, the algorithm always overpays the
connection cost by a factor of ρ. To see this, let DC end denote the set of clients which
are directly connected at the end. Let j ∈ DC end , and suppose through the execution of the
algorithm, j was connected to facilities i1 , i2 ... . . . it . For simplicity, assume that j was directly
connected to each of these facilities. Then the total bid (dual contribution, see Equation (1))
of j to these facilities is at most
                                       X
                                       t−1
                (αj − ρd (j, i1 )) +
                               2
                                             ρd2 (j, iℓ ) − ρd2 (j, iℓ+1 ) = αj − ρd2 (j, it ) .
                                       ℓ=1

This means that after accounting for facility costs, αj pays for ρd2 (j, it ), where ρ > 1, leading
to an overpayment of the connection cost by the factor ρ. Unfortunately, one cannot set ρ = 1
as it would not give even an LMP O(1)-approximation, as already observed in [CCG+ 25].
This means we could simply subtract (ρ − 1)d2 (j, it ) in the dual feasibility analysis.
    However, while this overpayment is not ideal, it seems challenging to account for this natu-
rally in the analysis: dual feasibility analysis is highly local, which cannot rely on the sequence
of connections a client has: in particular, it is not clear how to meaningfully incorporate the
subtraction (ρ − 1)d2 (j, it ) of client j, which is a global quantity, into the local one-cluster dual
feasibility analysis. The previous example of Figure 1 illustrates this diﬃculty as well; when
α2 = 16, the events triggering the actions of “having client 2 indirectly connected to i′ ” and
“opening i and connecting client 1 and 2 to i” happen simultaneously, but depending on how
the algorithm breaks the ties (which can be easily manipulated by the position of the external
facility i′ ), the amount of the overpayment substantially changes. In the former case, it is
(ρ−1)·d2 (1, i′ ) = 4 just from client 1, and in the latter case, it is (ρ−1)·(d2 (1, i)+d2 (2, i)) = 2.

                                                          5
Our new algorithm with tight dual payments. To resolve this issue, we propose a new
algorithm (Algorithm 1) that allows us to get the best of both worlds. Our new algorithm
still has an overpayment, but we subtract this overpayment at the end, while still being able
to incorporate this subtraction in the dual feasibility analysis. In our new algorithm, each
client reserves a part of its ﬁrst direct connection cost, that it does not oﬀer to other facilities
for subsequent (direct) connections. The key is that clients only move to facilities where their
connection cost is lower, so the ﬁrst direct connection cost is an upper bound for every future
direct connection.

  Algorithm 1 (Greedy Algorithm).
  Initialization: A = D, IC = DC = S = ∅, and Rj = αj = 0 for every j ∈ D.
  While A 6= ∅, uniformly grow the αj of every client in A until:

     1. For some unopened facility i ∈ F \ S, we have
                    X                      +  X                         +
                          αj − ρ · d2 (j, i) +        d2 (j, S) − d2 (j, i) = fˆ.
                      j∈A∪IC                                 j∈DC


        In this case, we open facility i. For each client j ∈ DC, if d2 (j, S) > d2 (j, i), we
        reconnect j to i. For each client j ∈ A ∪ IC,

           • If αj ≥ ρ · d2 (j, i), we (re)connect j to i, move j to DC and set Rj = d(j, i).
           • Else, if αj ≥ d2 (j, i), we (re)connect j to i, and move j to IC (if j ∈ A), and
             set αj = d2 (j, i).

     2. For some client j ∈ A, we have

                                                     αj = d2 (j, S).

        In this case, we move j to IC and connect j to the closest facility to it in S.



    Notice that until the ﬁrst direct connection, an active or IC client j oﬀers αj −ρd2 (j, i), but
subsequently, instead of oﬀering the natural ρd2 (j, S)−ρd2 (j, i), it only oﬀers d2 (j, S)−d2 (j, i).
Thus if Rj = d(j, i1 ) is the ﬁrst direct connection distance, then (ρ − 1)Rj2 is reserved by the
client and never used for any subsequent bid to facilities.
    Following the same analysis as before, consider any client j ∈ DC end . If it goes through
a sequence of direct connections i1 , i2 ... . . . it , then it oﬀers

                               X
                               t−1
         αj − ρd2 (j, i1 ) +         d2 (j, iℓ ) − d2 (j, iℓ+1 ) = αj − (ρ − 1)d2 (j, i1 ) − d2 (j, it ) .
                               ℓ=1

Since the connection cost at the end is only d2 (j, it ), this leads to an overpayment of (ρ −
1)d2 (j, i1 ), where i1 is the ﬁrst facility j was directly connected to. But now we deﬁne
Rj = d(j, i1 ), and subtract (ρ − 1)Rj2 in the dual feasibility analysis to make this accounting
exact (we will deﬁne Rj = 0 if j is never directly connected). This turns out to be helpful
in the dual feasibility analysis because we know Rj = d(j, i1 ) ≥ d(j, i2 ) ≥ ... . . . ≥ d(j, it ), as
clients only move to closer facilities as the algorithm progresses. Rj is thus an upper bound on

                                                         6
all these distances, which facilitates a reﬁned dual feasibility analysis, as discussed in greater
detail in the following subsections.
    Going back to the example from Figure 1 with ρ = 2, we set R1 = d(1, i′ ) = 2 when i′ is
open at time 12, and the new algorithm indirectly connects client 2 to i′ at time 16 (which
now happens regardless of the tiebreaking
                                      P      rule as the bid of client 1 toward i becomes smaller),
                                                                                              
leading to R2 = 0. Thus we have j∈D∗ αj = α1 + α2 = 28 = fˆ + 4 d2 (1, i) + d2 (2, i) just
like before, but because of the subtraction of (ρ − 1)R12 = R12 , the dual feasibility
                                                                                      is no longer
tight. In particular we have α1 − R12 + α2 − fˆ = 4 = 2 d2 (1, i) + d2 (2, i) , and consequently,
P                                  ˆ
   j∈D ∗ (αj − 2d (j, i) − Rj ) ≤ f , which improves the dual feasibility factor to 2 instead of 4
                 2          2 +

for this example.

Analysis. From the above discussion, our modiﬁed payment and dual feasibility lemmas
can be stated as follows. Let α and S be the respective values at the end of the algorithm.
The following dual payment lemma almost directly follows from the algorithm description and
the discussion above.
Lemma 2.3 (Dual Payment for our new algorithm, Lemma 4.5).
                    X                               X
                       (αj − (ρ − 1)Rj2 ) ≥ fˆ|S| +   d2 (j, S)
                           j∈D                             j∈D

   The crux of our analysis is now to prove a dual feasibility lemma for a small enough
constant Γ (depending on ρ). In particular, we prove the following.
Lemma 2.4 (Dual feasibility of our new algorithm, Lemma 4.6). Fix any facility i. Let
ρ = 2.5 (resp., ρ = 2 for Euclidean metrics). Then
                            X                               +
                                 αj − Γd2 (j, i) − (ρ − 1)Rj2 ≤ fˆ,
                             j∈D

where Γ = 4.9 (resp., Γ = 3 + ln(2) for Euclidean metrics).
    Intuitively, the above dual feasibility lemma shows that the variables αj′ := Γ1 (αj − (ρ −
1)Rj2 ) induce a dual-feasible solution for the Facility Location LP. An LMP Γ-approximation
follows by the same argument as before.
    Furthermore, note that we can interpret our algorithmic idea as having smaller bids for
clients who are directly connected. The intuition behind our new algorithmic idea (for the
Euclidean case) is that these directly connected clients are “bad” in the dual feasibility analysis
of [CCG+ 26] only when all clients and facilities are “almost” on a line (i.e., one-dimensional
Euclidean space) and its maximum direct connection cost Rj2 is close to the squared distances
between it and other clients j ′ with large dual variable αj ′ (compared to d2 (j ′ , i)). As we
have this additional −(ρ − 1)Rj2 term in the ﬁnal dual variable αj′ , we can make the ﬁnal dual
variables of these “bad” directly connected clients much smaller.
    However, to apply this intuition to the analysis (for the general Euclidean cases), we
confront several technical challenges:
   • We need to deal with high-dimensional Euclidean spaces.

   • We need to transform these −(ρ − 1)Rj2 terms into −d2 (j, i) terms (because, intuitively,
                                             P                               P
     the dual feasibility constraint compares j∈D′ αj − (ρ − 1)Rj2 with f + j∈D′ Γd2 (j, i)
     for every D′ ⊆ D).

                                                7
                                                                                 P
   • Finally, in the Euclidean space, [CCG+ 26] uses the pairwise distance |D1′ | j<ℓ∈D′ d2 (j, ℓ)
                                                             P
     as an intermediate quantity, which is upper bounded by j∈D′ d2 (j, i) (see, e.g., Lemma B.3).
     However, this inequality may not hold if the coeﬃcients before each d2 (j, i) have an av-
     erage of 1 but are “unbalanced”. After we transform the −(ρ − 1)Rj2 terms into the
     −d2 (j, i) terms, we observe that the coeﬃcients of the new terms have an average Ω(1)
     in the cases [CCG+ 26]’s analysis is tight. However, the coeﬃcients are “unbalanced”,
     and some of them can even be zero (due to Rj = 0 for the corresponding client). To
     prove a ratio strictly better than 4, the most signiﬁcant challenge is to show that the
                      Pterms2combined with these new −d (j, i) terms can be upper bounded
     pairwise distance                                     2

     by (1 − Ω(1)) · j∈D′ d (j, i).
As an illustration on how we resolve these challenges, we will provide a framework of our
spectral analysis in Subsection 2.1, and provide simple proofs that obtain improved ratios
(which are slightly worse than our ﬁnal results) on the worst cases for the analysis of [CCG+ 26;
CCG+ 25] in Subsections 2.2 and 2.3.
    We remark that slightly smaller values of Γ (hence slightly better approximation factors)2
can be obtained, for both the Euclidean and the metric case, with diﬀerent values of ρ and
a substantially more technical and longer analysis. We decided to omit this for the sake
of clarity (and given that the presented analysis is already very complex). We also remark
that our approach cannot yield signiﬁcantly better approximation factors. Our numerical
simulations suggests that our spectral analysis framework has a barrier of 3.51 for Euclidean
metrics and 4.67 for general metrics. See the end of Subsection 2.1 for a brief discussion of
this.

2.1    Spectral analysis: the general framework proving the dual feasibility
In this part, we will introduce the general idea of our new spectral analysis of the dual
feasibility, which is the key to improving the approximations for both Euclidean and Metric
k-Means. For simplicity, we will focus on the special case where at each point in time each
considered client is active or directly connected (without ever being indirectly connected).
This case is already non-trivial, and allows us to illustrate the main ideas. To simplify the
notation, we also assume that the ﬁnal dual values of the mentioned clients are all distinct.
We start with some general considerations. Then, in Subsection 2.2 and Subsection 2.3 we
will detail the analysis (for the mentioned special case) in the Euclidean and metric case,
respectively. The analysis for arbitrary instances is given in Sections 4 and 5 for the Euclidean
case, and in Sections 4 to 6 for the metric case.

The general idea of the spectral analysis. We will consider a similar set of constraints
on the dual variables αj as in the original analysis of [JMM+ 03] as the starting point of our
spectral analysis. Similar constraints are also used in the analysis of [CCG+ 25; CCG+ 26].
   Fix a facility i, and let D∗ = {j | αj − (ρ − 1)Rj2 ≥ Γd2 (j, i)}. We need to show that
                            X                            X
                                αj − (ρ − 1)Rj2 ≤ fˆ + Γ     d2 (j, i) .                    (2)
                            j∈D ∗                          j∈D ∗

Let us assume by renumbering that j ∈ D∗ is sorted in increasing order of αj , and let us
denote D∗ = [s]. Recall that we assume that no two αj are equal.
  2
   For example, we know that ρ = 1.96, Γ = 3.692 works for Euclidean metrics, and ρ ≈ 2.430, Γ = 4.865
works for general metrics.


                                                  8
    Then we consider the contributions to i at time αt − ε for some arbitrarily small ε > 0 for
each t ∈ [s]. Observe that each client j < t is already directly connected to an open facility
in our special case. Let S t be the set of open facilities at time αt − ε, and rjt = d(j, S t ) be
the distance at which j is connected at time αt − ε. Then, we get the following inequality.
                         X                       X
                               2
                             (rjt − d2 (j, i)) +   (αt − ρd2 (j, i)) ≤ fˆ.                      (3)
                           j<t                   j≥t

This inequality follows from the fact that clients j < t are already (directly) connected at the
time αt − ε, and hence they bid [rjt  2 − d2 (j, i)]+ to i, and clients j ≥ t are still active, hence

they bid [α − ρd2 (j, i)]+ to i, where α is their current α value (which at time αt − ε, is αt − ε).
We relax the terms of type [x]+ to x, which can only make the LHS smaller. Taking ε → 0
and since the algorithm ensures that at all times, every facility receives a total bid of at most
fˆ, implying Equation (3).
     Next, we eliminate rjt2 via triangle inequality. Since we know that at time α − ε, t was
                                                                                         t
not connected, we must have

                                  αt ≤ d2 (t, S) ≤ (d(j, t) + rjt )2                             (4)

See Lemma 4.9 for a formal proof. To get started with the spectral analysis, we will plug this
lower bound on rjt into Equation (3) and obtain a new inequality that upper bounds the sum
of αj − (ρ − 1)Rj2 in terms of fˆ and the sum of d2 (j, i) (see Lemmas 4.11 and 4.12 for the
formal statements we used for general and Euclidean metrics respectively).
    We will use a slightly diﬀerent approach to use the inequality αt ≤ (d(j, t) + rjt )2 for the
metric case and the Euclidean case.
   • Consider the Euclidean space of dimension dim. We will assume w.l.o.g. that the
     facility i is located at the origin of the Euclidean space and use the coordinate of
     the clients, (xj )j∈[s] ∈ Rdim , to rewrite d(j, t) = kxj − xt k and d(j, i) = kxj k. Using
     Equations (3) and (4), some quadratic inequalities and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality
     (to makeP every d(·, ·) appear in a quadratic form in the inequalities), we can upper
     bound j αj − (ρ − 1)Rj2 by the sum of fˆ and a quadratic form of x = (xj )j∈[s] ∈ Rs·dim ,
                                            P              P
     denoted as Q̂(x). Because we have j d2 (j, i) = j kxj k2 = kxk22 , it suﬃces to upper
      bound Q̂(x)
             ∥x∥22
                   . As an (s · dim) × (s · dim) matrix, Q̂ is a block-diagonal matrix with dim
      many identical s × s matrices on the diagonal, so an upper bound on the maximum
      eigenvalue of this s × s matrix yields a bound on the approximation ratio.

   • In the metric case, we use the metric triangle inequality d(j, t) ≤ d(j, i) + d(t, i). Then,
     we will consider each d(j, i) as a variable aj , and use Equations (3) and (4) and some
                                              P
     quadratic inequalities to upper bound j αj − (ρ − 1)Rj2 by a sum of fˆ and a quadratic
                                                                        P               P
     form of (aj )j∈[s] , denoted as Q̂(a). Similarly, because we have j d2 (j, i) = j a2j =
     kak2 , it suﬃces to upper bound the maximum eigenvalue of Q̂(a) (again a quadratic
     form on s variables) to prove the bound on our objective Equation (2).
   Numerically, we found the following barriers for our approach:
   • Euclidean case: After the Cauchy-Schwarz step (which makes the spectral analysis
     work), the underlying matrix Q̂(x) has an eigenvalue ≥ 3.51 in the case where there are
     300 clients: the ﬁrst 260 clients are immediately directly connected when they become
     inactive; while the last 40 clients are never directly connected.

                                                  9
   • Metric case: The underlying matrix Q̂(x) has an eigenvalue ≥ 4.67 when the number
     of clients is large. More details can be found at the end of Subsection 2.3.

    Dividing by the outer s and using j ∗ ≤ s, the total coeﬃcient is at most 5, which gives
the desired inequality Q̂(a) ≤ 5aT a, and hence Γ ≤ 5.
    However, it is possible to do better. By running experiments for various values of ρ, one
can observe that the largest eigenvalue of the symmetric matrix describing this quadratic form
is (numerically) minimized when ρ ≈ 2.5. With this value of ρ, we numerically estimate the
top eigenvalue to be 4.67783 as s → ∞. It can be shown that the matrix corresponding to
the quadratic form Q̂(a) is the sum of a diagonal and a rank one matrix, and hence it is not
diﬃcult to prove an analytic bound that reaches close to this constant for any s. Thus using
our new algorithm with the new spectral analysis, we get an approximation ratio of 4.68 for
this special case when no client is indirectly connected.
    Thus all that is left is to generalize this to the case when there are indirectly connected
clients. Let DCt and ICt be the sets of directly and indirectly connected clients at time αt − ε.
Then the number of such valid sets3 is exponential in s. Thus, we need a way to bound the
general case directly.
    For this purpose, we write down the equations for the most general IC/DC case, and
try to solve them all at once, similarly to [CCG+ 25]. However, this analysis becomes more
technically challenging as the matrices involved are no longer simple sums of diagonal and
rank one matrices. Nevertheless, we show that the quadratic form in the general case can
always be bounded by 4.9. Our proof uses the Collatz–Wielandt Formula which says that
given a non-negative matrix M , if we ﬁnd a non-zero vector h such that maxi (Mhh) i
                                                                                     i
                                                                                       is at most
Γ, then the top eigenvalue of M is at most Γ.

2.4     Turning LMP approximations to bicriteria approximations
To turn the LMP approximations to bi-criteria approximations (opening k + O(log n) centers),
we employ the framework of [CGL+ 25; CCG+ 25; CCG+ 26]. The only technical improvement
is as follows. The most recent work [CCG+ 26] can only turn an LMP Γ-approximation to a
Γ + ε bi-criteria approximations for any constant Γ ≥ 4. We remove this condition, and now
this step works for any constant Γ > ρ (which is a clear lower bound for our LMP algorithm).


3       Preliminaries
For a client j and a set of facilities S, let d(j, S) := mini∈S d(j, i). For any real number x, let
[x]+ := max{x, 0}. For a positive integer n, we write [n] := {1, 2, . . . , n}, and more generally,
for integers l ≤ r, we write [l : r] := {l, l + 1, . . . , r}. We use R>0 and R≥0 to denote the sets
of positive and non-negative reals, respectively.

Facility Location with uniform opening costs and squared distances. In the Facility
Location with uniform opening costs and squared distances problem, we are given a set of clients
D, a set of facilities F , and a facility opening cost f > 0. The goal is to ﬁnd a set S ⊆ F
minimizing                                      X
                                        f |S| +   d2 (j, S).
                                                   j∈D

We write OPTFL (f ) for the optimum value of this problem. We will refer to this problem
simply as Facility Location throughout the paper.
   The only condition for validity is that DCt ⊆ DCt+1 for all t, since the algorithm moves clients only from
    3

IC and A to DC, while never moving from DC to IC.


                                                     13
LP relaxation.      The standard LP relaxation of Facility Location is
                      X       XX
            min     f    yi +      d2 (j, i)xij
                     i∈F       j∈D i∈F
                    X
             s.t.         xij ≥ 1                        ∀j ∈ D,
                                                                              (Primal FL LP)
                    i∈F
                    xij ≤ yi                             ∀j ∈ D, i ∈ F,
                    xij ≥ 0, yi ≥ 0.
Its dual can be written as
                             X
                    max            αj
                             j∈D
                             X
                     s.t.          [αj − d2 (j, i)]+ ≤ f   ∀i ∈ F,              (Dual FL LP)
                             j∈D

                            αj ≥ 0                         ∀j ∈ D.
We write OPTLP (f ) for the optimum value of the Dual FL LP.
Deﬁnition 3.1 (LMP approximation). Given Γ ≥ 1, an algorithm is Lagrangian Multiplier
Preserving (LMP) Γ-approximation for the Facility Location problem if, for every instance
with facility opening cost f > 0, the output S ⊆ F satisﬁes
                                       X
                            Γf · |S| +    d2 (j, S) ≤ Γ · OPTLP (f ).
                                          j∈D


Bounding distances. Thanks to standard reductions (see, e.g., [CCG+ 25]), in the metric
case we will assume that distances are integers in [1, n3 /ε] while losing a factor 1 + O(ε) in
the approximation.
Lemma 3.2 ([CCG+ 25]). For any constants ε > 0 and α > 1, given a polynomial-time
α-approximation algorithm for k-Means on instances with distances in {1, . . . , n3 /ε}, there
exists a polynomial-time α(1 + O(ε))-approximation algorithm for Metric k-Means.
   In the Euclidean case, we will assume that non-zero distances are in [1, n2 /ε2 ] thanks to
the following reduction in [CCG+ 26].
Lemma 3.3 ([CCG+ 26]). For any given ε > 0 and α > 1, given a polynomial-time α-
approximation algorithm for Euclidean k-Means on instances with non-zero distances between
1 and n2 /ε2 , there exists a polynomial-time α(1+O(ε))-approximation algorithm for Euclidean
k-Means on general instances.

Triangle Inequalities for Squared Distances. We will use the following simple inequal-
ities taken from [CCG+ 25]. Given three distances d, d1 , d2 with d ≤ d1 + d2 , the following
lemma provides a useful upper bound on d2 as a function of d1 and d2 . The next lemma
provides a similar upper bound in an analogous setting where d ≤ d1 + d2 + d3 .
                                                                              γ
Lemma 3.4 ([CCG+ 25]). Let γ > 1 be any constant. For any x, y we have γx2 + γ−1 y2 ≥
       2
(x + y) .
Lemma 3.5 ([CCG+ 25]). Let γ > 1 be any constant. For any x, y, z, we have γx2 +

       2
  2 + γ−1 (y 2 + z 2 ) ≥ (x + y + z)2 .

                                                    14
4      Our Improved Greedy Algorithm
In this section, we present our improved greedy LMP approximate algorithm for the Facility
Location problem with uniform facility cost f . As in the previous works [CCG+ 25; CCG+ 26],
our algorithm is based on the well-known greedy algorithm by Jain et al. [JMM+ 03].
    In our algorithm, we use parameters ρ, Γ > 1, which will be deﬁned (diﬀerently for the
metric and Euclidean cases) later. We also use fˆ = Γ · f , where f is the uniform facility
opening cost. The following Algorithm 1 is our new greedy algorithm achieving the improved
LMP approximation. As standard in the area, we present the algorithm in a continuous
version where there is a variable θ (interpreted as time) that grows continuously from 0 to
+∞. Initially, all clients are active. We grow the α-values of active clients at the same speed
as θ grows, until one of two conditions is met: (1) a new facility becomes openable, or (2) a
client’s α-value reaches its connection cost to some open facility. It is easy to discretize this
algorithm. Here, we say one facility is openable if the total bid of the clients to this facility
reaches fˆ, where the bid of client j to a facility i is deﬁned as [αj − ρ · d2 (j, i)]+ when j is in
A or IC, or as [d2 (j, S) − d2 (j, i)]+ when j is in DC, at the time i is opened. In addition, for
each client j ∈ D, we explicitly track its ﬁrst direct-connection cost, Rj2 . We set Rj = d(j, i∗ )
where i∗ is the facility that caused j to be moved to DC for the ﬁrst time. For a client that
remains only indirectly connected or active, we keep Rj = 0 as it is initialized.

    Algorithm 1 (Greedy Algorithm), restated.
    Parameters: ρ, Γ > 1; fˆ = Γ · f where f is the uniform facility opening cost.
    Initialization: A = D, IC = DC = S = ∅, and Rj = αj = 0 for every j ∈ D.
    While A 6= ∅, uniformly grow the αj of every client in A until:

      1. For some unopened facility i ∈ F \ S, we have
                     X                      +  X                         +
                           αj − ρ · d2 (j, i) +        d2 (j, S) − d2 (j, i) = fˆ.
                      j∈A∪IC                           j∈DC


         In this case, we open facility i. For each client j ∈ DC, if d2 (j, S) > d2 (j, i), we
         reconnect j to i. For each client j ∈ A ∪ IC,

            • If αj ≥ ρ · d2 (j, i), we (re)connect j to i, move j to DC and set Rj = d(j, i).
            • Else, if αj ≥ d2 (j, i), we (re)connect j to i, set αj to be d2 (j, i), and move j to
              IC (if j ∈ A).

      2. For some client j ∈ A, we have

                                               αj = d2 (j, S).

         In this case, we move j to IC and connect j to the closest facility to it in S.



Theorem 4.1. Algorithm 1 is a LMP Γ-approximation for the Facility Location problem with
uniform facility cost f if
    • d is a (general) metric, ρ = 2.5 and Γ = 4.9; or

                                                  15
  • d is a Euclidean metric, ρ = 2 and Γ = 3 + ln(2).

Observation 4.2. At any time during the algorithm, for any j ∈ IC, we have αj = d2 (j, S).

    Because we freeze αj when a client j becomes directly connected, we can show that the
α values are non-increasing after connection. For some intermediate time θ, we will use αj (θ)
to denote the α value of client j ∈ D at that time. Further, for each j ∈ D, we deﬁne α̂j to
be its α value at the ﬁrst time when j is connected to some facility. Let (αj∗ )j∈D be the ﬁnal
vector of α-values.

Observation 4.3. For any client j ∈ D, αj (θ) is non-increasing in θ ∈ [α̂j , +∞).

Proof. This follows from the fact that we only modify αj after time α̂j when some facility i
is opened and αj > d2 (j, i). Then the resulting value of αj is lowered to d2 (j, i).

Corollary 4.4. For each client j, we have αj∗ ≤ α̂j .

    Our goal is to show that (αj∗ − (ρ − 1)Rj2 )j∈D can characterize the total cost of Algorithm 1
and can be scaled to a feasible dual solution to the Dual FL LP.
    First, we prove that the sum of (αj∗ −(ρ−1)Rj2 )j∈D is an upper bound of the total cost. The
proof relies on the following observation: for each client j that is ﬁnally directly connected,
its α-value can be written as the sum of its ﬁnal connection cost d2 (j, S), ρ − 1 times its ﬁrst
direct-connection cost Rj2 , and the bids of j to the open facilities.

Lemma 4.5 (Dual Payment, Lemma 2.3 restated, replacing α by α∗ ). At the end of the
execution of Algorithm 1,
                          X                     X
                            d2 (j, S) + |S|fˆ ≤   (αj∗ − (ρ − 1)Rj2 ).
                          j∈D                    j∈D

Proof. First, observe that it is suﬃcient to prove that at any point in the execution, we have
                         X                         X               X
                              (αj − (ρ − 1)Rj2 ) ≥     d2 (j, S) +   fˆ.                    (6)
                        j∈DC                        j∈DC           i∈S

Indeed, at the end of the execution, A = ∅, and for every j ∈ IC, we have αj∗ = d2 (j, S) and
Rj = 0 (since j never became directly connected). Thus, their contribution to the left-hand
side is exactly their connection cost, making (6) imply the lemma.
    To prove this, we proceed by induction and show that (6) is maintained throughout the
execution.
    The inequality is initially true since DC = ∅ and S = ∅. Furthermore, since whenever the
α-value of a client j reaches d2 (j, S) it stops growing and moves to IC, no client is added to
DC except when a new facility is opened (i.e., added to S).
    We now consider what happens when we open a facility i, i.e., add it to S. Let (α, S,
A, IC, DC) be the state right before opening i, and set DC ′ = {j ∈ A ∪ IC : αj ≥ ρd2 (j, i)},
and X = {j ∈ DC : d2 (j, S) > d2 (j, i)}. The change of cost of the right-hand side of (6) is
exactly
                                 X                X
                           fˆ +       d2 (j, i) +   (d2 (j, i) − d2 (j, S)) .
                                j∈DC ′        j∈X




                                               16
Since the algorithm decided to open i, we know that the total oﬀer reaches fˆ, so we have
                          X                       X
                    fˆ ≤      (αj − ρd2 (j, i)) +   (d2 (j, S) − d2 (j, i)) .
                          j∈DC ′                          j∈X

Substituting this bound into our expression, we get that the change of cost of the right-hand
side is at most
                  X                                   X
                      (αj − ρd2 (j, i) + d2 (j, i)) =   (αj − (ρ − 1)d2 (j, i)).
                 j∈DC ′                                    j∈DC ′

Because each client j ∈ DC ′ is becoming directly connected for the ﬁrst time, the algorithm
sets its penalty variable Rj = d(j, i), meaning Rj2 = d2 (j, i). Therefore, the change of the
                            P
right-hand side is at most j∈DC ′ (αj − (ρ − 1)Rj2 ). Because we do not change the α-value or
Rj value after a client is moved to DC, this is exactly the change of the left-hand side of (6).
The invariant is thus maintained, completing the proof.
                                                 
    Second, we prove that (αj∗ − (ρ − 1)Rj2 )/Γ j∈D can be scaled to a dual feasible solution for
an appropriate choice of ρ, Γ. Since the proof of this part is very long, we will ﬁrst present a
general framework for both the metric case and Euclidean case in Section 4, where we establish
a set of constraints for the variables (αj∗ )j∈D and (Rj )j∈D . Then, we will use the constraints
to establish the following lemma for the metric case and the Euclidean case separately in
Sections 5 and 6, using signiﬁcantly diﬀerent techniques.

Lemma 4.6 (Dual Feasibility, Lemma 2.4 restated, replacing α by α∗ ). For any facility i ∈ F ,
we have                 X                                 +

                            j∈D

where

  • if d is a (general) metric, we choose ρ = 2.5, Γ = 4.9; or

  • if d is a Euclidean metric, we choose ρ = 2, Γ = 3 + ln(2).

Dual feasibility: the analysis framework
Next, we show the analysis framework to establish Lemma 4.6. We will use [n] to denote the
set {1, 2, . . . , n} and [l : r] to denote the set {l, l + 1, . . . , r}. From now on, we ﬁx any facility
i ∈ F . Let D∗ = {j ∈ D : αj∗ − (ρ − 1)Rj2 > Γ · d2 (j, i)}. Suppose that |D∗ | = s and we index
them by [s] and order them in the increasing order of the time they become inactive.
    That is, we have α̂1 ≤ α̂2 ≤ · · · ≤ α̂s . Then, our goal (which will be formally shown at
the end of this subsection) becomes proving
                                X                                    X
                                    (αj∗ − (ρ − 1)Rj2 ) ≤ fˆ + Γ ·        d2 (j, i) .                  (7)
                            j∈[s]                              j∈[s]

    Consider the time α̂t − ε for each client t, where ε > 0 is suﬃciently small so that neither
condition in the algorithm is satisﬁed between time α̂t − ε (inclusive) and α̂t (exclusive). For
simplicity, we deﬁne DC t as the set of directly connected clients in [t − 1] (i.e., DC ∩ [t − 1])
at time α̂t − ε, and IC t as the set of indirectly connected or active clients in [t − 1] (i.e.,
(A ∪ IC) ∩ [t − 1]) at time α̂t − ε. Moreover, we use S t to denote the set S at time α̂t − ε.
Because the sets IC, DC, A are disjoint and the set DC only grows, we have the following
observation.

Observation 4.7. For each t ∈ [s], we have DC t ∩ IC t = ∅ and DC t ∪ IC t = [t − 1]. For
each t ∈ [s − 1], we have DC t ⊆ DC t+1 .

    Suppose that rjt (for t ∈ [s] and j ∈ DC t ) is the distance of client j ∈ DC t to its closest
opened facility at time α̂t − ε (i.e., d(j, S t )). One simple observation is that rjt ≤ Rj for each
j ∈ DC t , as the set S only grows in the algorithm.

Observation 4.8. For each t ∈ [s] and j ∈ DC t , we have rjt ≤ Rj .

    Next, we will establish two sets of constraints for the variables (αj∗ )j∈[s] , (rjt )1≤j<t≤s , and
(Rj )j∈[s] . The ﬁrst set of constraints is as follows. The proof of the ﬁrst set of constraints
relies on the fact that for each client t ∈ [s], its α-value at time α̂t − ε is strictly less than its
distance to any open facility.

Lemma 4.9. For each t ∈ [s], j ∈ DC t , ℓ ∈ IC t ∪ {t}, we have the following constraint:

                                        αℓ∗ ≤ (rjt + d(j, ℓ))2 .                                   (pt,j,ℓ )

Proof. Consider any client t ∈ [s]. Consider the time α̂t − ε. Consider any client ℓ ∈ IC t ∪ {t}.
As ℓ ≤ t, we have α̂ℓ ≤ α̂t by our assumption. Next, we prove that αℓ∗ ≤ d2 (ℓ, S t ) by discussing
two cases:

   • If α̂ℓ < α̂t (since ε is suﬃciently small, we have α̂ℓ ≤ α̂t −ε), then ℓ is indirectly connected
     at time α̂t − ε. Because of Observations 4.2 and 4.3, we have αℓ∗ ≤ αℓ (α̂t − ε) = d2 (ℓ, S t ).


                                                   18
   • Otherwise (i.e., if α̂ℓ = α̂t ), we can prove α̂t − ε < d2 (ℓ, S t ) by contradiction. Suppose
     that α̂t − ε ≥ d2 (ℓ, S t ). Then, ℓ will be connected to some opened facility using the
     second condition in Algorithm 1 at time α̂t − ε, contradicting that ℓ is active at time
     α̂t − ε. Since ε can be arbitrarily small here, we have α̂ℓ = α̂t ≤ d2 (ℓ, S t ). Because of
     Corollary 4.4, we get αℓ∗ ≤ d2 (ℓ, S t ).

Because the triangle inequality holds for the metric d, we have d(ℓ, S t ) ≤ d(j, S t ) + d(j, ℓ).
Therefore, αℓ∗ ≤ d2 (ℓ, S t ) ≤ (rjt + d(j, ℓ))2 , completing the proof.

    Our second set of constraints is as follows. The proof of the second set of constraints relies
on the fact that for each client t ∈ [s], the total bid to the ﬁxed facility i at time α̂t − ε is
strictly less than fˆ, as otherwise t will be connected to i at time α̂t − ε and become inactive,
contradicting the deﬁnition of α̂t .

Lemma 4.10. For each t ∈ [s], we have the following constraint:
                        X       X                 X                     X

our assumption that α̂t denotes the time client t is connected for the ﬁrst time. Therefore, the

5       Establishing the LMP Approximations
In this section, we will prove Lemma 4.11. For convenience, we restate the lemma below. Using
the proof of Lemma 4.11, we will also prove a generalization of it, Lemma 5.20, which will be
used in the later sections for the log-adaptive and robust analysis of the greedy algorithm for
Euclidean metrics. Throughout this section,
                                         P     we will assume that ρ = 2. For simplicity, in the
rest of this section, we will deﬁne Φ = j∈[s] w(j) · d2 (j, i).
    Readers can assume w(j) = 1 for all j ∈ [s] in the ﬁrst reading, which is enough for
understanding the main ideas and establishing the LMP approximations.

Lemma 4.11. Let ρ = 2. Let fˆ be any non-negative real number. Let ([s]∪{i}, d) be a general
metric. Let w : [s] → R>0 be any positive weight function. Let (IC t )t∈[s] and (DC t )t∈[s] be
two families of sets satisfying:
  (a) For each t ∈ [s], DC t ∩ IC t = ∅ and DC t ∪ IC t = [t − 1].
  (b) For each t ∈ [s − 1], DC t ⊆ DC t+1 .
Suppose that the variables (αj∗ )j∈[s] , (Rj )j∈[s] , (rjt )1≤j<t≤s satisfy the following constraints

                                                        26
5.3.3   Step 3: A key property on the conditional column sum of P
The third key property is an upper bound for the conditional column sum of P in terms of
d+
 j and w(ℓ), which will be used to upper bound the second and the third term in Lemma 5.7.
Intuitively, consider the ℓ-th column (corresponding to client ℓ) in the matrix P . The condi-
tional column sum of P is the sum of all rows j in this column whose corresponding client
j has d+                                         +
        j > θ. For a better understanding, dj can be interpreted as a parameter indicating
whether client j becomes directly connected early in the process of the greedy algorithm (ac-
cording to Observation 5.6). If d+         +                    ′
                                     j > dj ′ for other client j , it means that client j becomes
directly connected earlier than j ′ . Hence, the conditional column sum of P can be interpreted
as the sum of the coeﬃcients of a set of clients j < ℓ that become directly connected at some
time point before ℓ becomes directly connected in the process of the greedy algorithm. The
reason we only need to take care of those clients j is because we only charge d2 (j, ℓ) for those
clients j in the upper bound of the extra cost term EC in Lemma 5.7 (i.e., Pj ′ ℓ = 0 for other
clients j ′ ).

Lemma 5.11. For any θ ∈ (0, 1) and any ℓ ∈ [s], we have
                       X
                           1(d+j > θ) · w(j)Pjℓ ≤ w(ℓ)(1 − θ).
                             j<ℓ

lary 5.10. However, if θ ≥ dℓ , this lemma gives a stronger bound than Corollary 5.10.
                            +


   To prove this lemma, we start with the following simple observation, which is a direct
consequence of the deﬁnition of πt (j).

any t ∈ [k − 1].

5.4   A generalization of Lemma 4.11 for the Euclidean case
In this subsection, we will prove a generalization of Lemma 4.11, where we only have some
approximate version of the constraints. We will show that the same guarantee can be achieved
up to a small additive factor. Later, we will use this generalization to obtain the bicriteria
approximation guarantee for the Euclidean case. Because the proof is highly technical with
dealing with the small additive factor and mostly follows the spirit of the earlier derivations,
we defer it to Subsection B.2.
Lemma 5.20. Let ρ = 2 and Γ = 3 + ln(2). Let fˆ be any non-negative real number. Let ε ≥ 0
be any suﬃciently small non-negative real number. Let ([s] ∪ {i}, d) be a Euclidean metric.
Let w : [s] → R>0 be any non-negative weight function. Let a ∈ [s] be any positive integer
such that a ≤ s. Let (IC t )t∈[s] and (DC t )t∈[s] be two families of sets satisfying:

                                                           32
  (a) For each t ∈ [s], DC t ∩ IC t = ∅ and DC t ∪ IC t = [t − 1].
  (b) For each t ∈ [s − 1], DC t ⊆ DC t+1 .
  (c) [a + 1 : s − 1] ⊆ IC s .
Suppose that the variables (αj∗ )j∈[s] , (Rj )j∈[s] , (rjt )t∈[s],j∈DC t satisfy the following constraints
  (1) For any t ∈ [s], j ∈ DC t , we have rjt ≤ Rj .
  (2) For each t ∈ [a], j ∈ DC t and ℓ ∈ IC t ∪ {t}, we have αℓ∗ ≤ (rjt + d(j, ℓ))2 .
  (3) For each t ∈ [a] ∪ {s}, we have
       X             X              X                            X                              X
           w(ℓ)αt∗ +      w(ℓ)αℓ∗ +        2
                                      w(j)rjt ≤ fˆ+                          ρw(ℓ)d2 (ℓ, i)+            w(j)d2 (j, i).
      ℓ∈[t:s]            ℓ∈IC t       j∈DC t                 ℓ∈IC t ∪[t:s]                     j∈DC t


  (4) There is a set L ⊆ [a] ∩ IC s such that:
           P                 P
        •     ℓ∈L w(ℓ) ≤ ε ·   ℓ∈[a+1:s] w(ℓ);
         • for any j ∈ DC s , ℓ ∈ (IC s ∪ {s}) \ L, we have αℓ∗ ≤ (1 + ε) · (rjs + d(j, ℓ))2 ;
         • for any j ∈ DC s , ℓ ∈ L and ℓ′ ∈ [a + 1 : s], we have αℓ∗ ≤ (rjs + d(j, ℓ′ ))2 .

Then, the following holds:
               X                                                 X
                   w(j) · (αj∗ − (ρ − 1)Rj2 ) ≤ fˆ + (Γ + 10ε) ·   w(j) · d2 (j, i) .                          (20)
                j∈[s]                                                    j∈[s]

Remark 5.21. This is a generalization of Lemma 4.11, because Lemma 4.11 is a special case
when ε = 0, a = s − 1 and L = ∅.


6    An Improved LMP 4.9-Approximation for the Metric Case
In this section, we will prove Lemma 4.12, restated below for convenience.
Lemma 4.12. Let ρ = 2.5 and Γ = 4.9. Let fˆ be any non-negative real number. Let
([s] ∪ {i}, d) be a metric. Let w : [s] → R>0 be any positive weight function. Let (IC t )t∈[s] and
(DC t )t∈[s] be two families of sets satisfying:
  (a) For each t ∈ [s], DC t ∩ IC t = ∅ and DC t ∪ IC t = [t − 1].
  (b) For each t ∈ [s − 1], DC t ⊆ DC t+1 .
Suppose that the variables (αj∗ )j∈[s] , (Rj )j∈[s] , (rjt )t∈[s],j∈DC t satisfy the following constraints
  (1) For any t ∈ [s], j ∈ DC t , we have rjt ≤ Rj .
  (2) For each t ∈ [s], j ∈ DC t , we have αt∗ ≤ (rjt + d(j, t))2 .
  (3) For each t ∈ [s], we have
       X             X              X                            X                              X
           w(ℓ)αt∗ +      w(ℓ)αℓ∗ +        2
                                      w(j)rjt ≤ fˆ+                          ρw(ℓ)d2 (ℓ, i)+            w(j)d2 (j, i).
      ℓ∈[t:s]            ℓ∈IC t       j∈DC t                 ℓ∈IC t ∪[t:s]                     j∈DC t


Then, the following holds:
                   X                                         X
                       w(j) · (αj∗ − (ρ − 1)Rj2 ) ≤ fˆ + Γ ·   w(j) · d2 (j, i) .                               (9)
                        j∈[s]                                    j∈[s]


                                                    33
6.1     Unweighted
In this subsection, we prove Lemma 4.12 in the special case w(ℓ) = 1 for all ℓ ∈ [s]. Deﬁne
  def
at = d(t, i) for all t ∈ [s]. The ﬁnal proof of this lemma for the unweighted case was obtained by
GPT 5.2/5.4 Pro/Thinking, after several rounds of exchange with the authors implementing
various theoretical ideas and experiments, and revised by the authors.
    As brieﬂy discussed in the overview in Subsection 2.1, the proof has four steps. First, from
the inequalities at time αt − ε given in condition (3) of Lemma 4.12, we derive a family of
constraints (Pt ) by using the triangle inequality to lower bound the terms involving rjt . Second,
we choose coeﬃcients (β)t∈[s] so that, in the weighted combination of these constraints, the
coeﬃcient of each αt∗ is exactly 1. Third, after collecting terms, we obtain a quadratic form
in the distances to the facility i. Finally, we bound the top eigenvalue of the corresponding
matrix by the Collatz–Wielandt Formula, which yields the claimed constant.

                                                                                                def
Step 1: From (It ) to (Pt ). For each t ∈ [s], given w(t) = 1 and at = d(t, i) for all t ∈ [s],
rewriting and rearranging the condition (3) gives
          X         X          X                     X                  X
              αt∗ +      αj∗ +            2
                                    w(j)rjt ≤ fˆ +         ρd2 (j, i) +    w(j)d2 (j, i).
           j∈[t:s]          j∈IC t        j∈DC t                         j∈IC t ∪[t:s]          j∈DC t
                                                                                                             (Condition (3))
            X                          X                         X
      =⇒               2
                     (rjt − a2j ) +            (αj∗ − ρa2j ) +         (αt∗ − ρa2j ) ≤ fˆ.                              (It )
           j∈DC t                     j∈IC t                     j≥t


Fix t ∈ [s]. For each j ∈ DC t , conditions (1)–(2) and the triangle inequality imply

            αt∗ ≤ (rjt + d(j, t))2 ≤ (rjt + aj + at )2 ≤ rjt
                                                          2
                                                             + (aj + at )2 + 2(aj + at )Rj .

Rearranging and using 2aj at ≤ a2j + a2t , we obtain
                                      2
                                     rjt − a2j ≥ αt∗ − 2a2t − 3a2j − 2(aj + at )Rj .

Substituting into Equation (It ) gets us
                 X         X           X

6.2     Weighted
We now prove Lemma 4.12 in its full generality for any weight function w : [s] → R>0 .
    To extend the argument to the weighted setting, the overall structure is the same as in
the unweighted case: given the condition (3) of Lemma 4.12, we derive inequalities (Ptw )t∈[s] ,
choose coeﬃcients so that the αt∗ terms sum up to w(t) for each t ∈ [s], rewrite the resulting
bound as a quadratic form, and then upper bound its top eigenvalue. The new issue is the
bookkeeping. The cardinalities of clients are replaced by weighted masses, the normalization
of the coeﬃcients changes accordingly. After introducing
                                                p         the appropriate weighted analogues
of the unweighted parameters and rescaling by w(t), however, the same spectral argument
goes through.
    Since the proof is a weighted analogue of the unweighted subsection, we mention only the
diﬀerences in the proof of the coeﬃcients and in the quadratic form, and defer the full proof
to Subsection C.2.

7     Log-Adaptive Algorithm for Weighted Facility Location
Let ε ∈ (0, 10−100 ) be a suﬃciently small constant. We choose δ = ε/2. In weighted
                                                                                  P facility
location, each client j ∈ D has a weight w(j) > 0 and the objective is to minimize j∈D w(j)·
d2 (j, S) + |S|f .
     We present the modiﬁed greedy algorithm for weighted facility location that works in
O(log n/ε3 ) phases.
Deﬁnition 7.1 (openable). A facility i is openable with respect to the algorithm’s state
(α, S, θ) if there are increased dual values (τj )j∈A of active clients that satisfy the following
conditions:
                                                                           √
   • Only nearby clients are increased: τj = αj for every j ∈ A \ B(i, εθ).

    • Nearby clients are only increased slightly: αj ≤ τj ≤ min{(1 − δ)d2 (j, S), (1 + ε3 )θ} for
                         √
      every j ∈ A ∩ B(i, εθ).

    • Facility i is paid for:
                    X                                      X
          (1 − δ)        w(j) · [d2 (j, S) − d2 (j, i)]+ +   w(j) · [αj − ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, i)]+
                   j∈DC                                       j∈IC
                                                              X
                                                          +         w(j) · [τj − ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, i)]+ ≥ fˆ.
                                                              j∈A


    • Dual feasibility: for every facility i0 :
              X                                               X
                   w(j) · [(1 − δ)d2 (j, S) − d2 (j, i0 )]+ +   w(j) · [αj − ρd2 (j, i0 )]+
              j∈DC                                                j∈IC
                                                                  X
                                                              +         w(j) · [τj − ρd2 (j, i0 )]+ ≤ fˆ.
                                                                  j∈A


    The openability can be checked by solving a linear program.
Lemma 7.2. There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given the state of the algorithm and
a facility i, either outputs (τj )j∈A that satisfy all conditions of openability or certiﬁes that i
is not openable.

                                                     43
Proof. Fix i and (α, S, A, IC, DC, θ) and consider a linear program with variables (τj )j∈A .
We claim that each of the four bullets in Deﬁnition 7.1 can be expressed as linear   √ constraints.
The ﬁrst two bullets are immediate. Indeed, as τj = αj for every j ∈ A \ B(i, εθ), we can
treat them as constants and only have (τj )j∈A∩B(i,√εθ) as the true variables.
    Then, the third bullet,
                X                                     X
        (1 − δ)     w(j) · [d2 (j, S) − d2 (j, i)]+ +   w(j) · [αj − ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, i)]+
               j∈DC                                   j∈IC

becomes a linear inequality in the true variables, since     √ the only terms involving the true

and τ ≥ θ.
     Finally, for the fourth bullet, we can use an additional variable zi′0 ,j ≥ 0 for each j ∈ D.
We add the constraint zi′0 ,j ≥ (1 − δ)d2 (j, S) − d2 (j, i0 ) for j ∈ DC, the constraint zi′0 ,j ≥

                                         X

    Algorithm 2 is our log-adaptive algorithm. It works in O(log n/ε3 ) phases where, in the
ith phase, every active client j has αj = (1 + ε3 )i = θ and the algorithm opens openable
facilities in an arbitrary order by increasing
                                           √ αj ’s to τj ’s. However, when i is open, the only
scenario j with τj > αj is when j ∈ B(i, εθ), so j becomes immediately put into DC after i
is open. Unlike Algorithm 1, we don’t lower the α-values for clients in IC immediately after
a facility is opened. Instead, we only lower the α-values in the second stage of each phase.
    For√ each client j ∈ D, we explicitly track its ﬁrst direct-connection cost, Rj . We set
Rj = 1 − δd(j, i∗ ) where i∗ is the facility that caused j to be moved to DC for the ﬁrst time.
For a client that remains only indirectly connected or active, we set Rj = 0.
    In the rest of this subsection, let (αj∗ )j∈D be the ﬁnal dual solution produced by the
algorithm.

7.1    Dual Payment
Lemma 7.3. At the end of the log-adaptive algorithm, the ﬁnal dual solution α∗ satisﬁes:
           X                                        X                    X
               w(j) · (αj∗ − (ρ − 1)Rj2 ) ≥ (1 − δ)   w(j) · d2 (j, S) +   fˆ
                j∈D                                         j∈D                    i∈S

Proof. First, let us show that it suﬃces to prove that at the end of the algorithm, we have
                X                X                                      X
                    w(j) · αj ≥      w(j) · (1 − δ)d2 (j, S) + (ρ − 1)R2 +     fˆ
                                                                               j
               j∈DC              j∈DC                                               i∈S

Indeed, at the end of the algorithm, A = ∅. For every j ∈ IC, we have αj ≥ (1 − δ)d2 (j, S) by
the construction of the update steps in Stage 2. Because Rj = 0 for j ∈ IC, their contribution

                                                 44
                                                                    √
  Algorithm 2. Parameters: ρ, Γ > 1, ε ∈ (0, 10−100 ); fˆ = (Γ + 201 ε) · f where f is the
  uniform facility opening cost.
  Initialization: Set θ = 1, S = ∅, A = D, DC = ∅, IC = ∅, and αj = θ for every j ∈ D.
  While A 6= ∅:

      1. While there is an unopened facility i that is openable:

          (a) Compute (τj )j∈A so that i is openable with (τj )j∈A .
                                                              √
          (b) Add i to S. Set αj ← τj for every j ∈ A ∩ B(i, εθ).
          (c) For every j ∈ A ∪ IC:
                                                                         √
                 • If αj ≥ ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, i), move j to DC and set Rj = 1 − δd(j, i).
                 • Otherwise, if j ∈ A and αj ≥ (1 − δ)d2 (j, i), move j to IC.

      2. Perform the following step and move to the next phase:

            • For every j ∈ IC, set αj ← (1 − δ)d2 (j, S).
            • For every remaining j ∈ A, set αj ← min{(1 + ε3 )θ, (1 − δ)d2 (j, S)} and move
              it to IC when αj = (1 − δ)d2 (j, S).
            • Update θ ← (1 + ε3 )θ.



naturally satisﬁes the required bound. Therefore, proving the DC client inequality implies
the lemma.
    To this end, we prove by induction that at any point in the execution of the algorithm,
the DC invariant holds. The base case is at the beginning of the algorithm, where DC = ∅
and S = ∅. Next, we show that this invariant is preserved in each stage of the algorithm.
    Stage 1. In each step of this stage, we open a facility i, i.e., adding it to S. Suppose that
(α, S, A, IC, DC, θ) denotes the state right before opening i. Let A′ and IC ′ be the clients
that are moved to DC from A and IC respectively by the opening of i. This means that
A′ = {j ∈ A : τj ≥ ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, i)} and IC ′ = {j ∈ IC : αj ≥ ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, i)}.
    For these √newly directly connected clients, their initial direct-connection parameter is
exactly Rj = 1 − δd(j, i). Therefore, their combined contribution to the right-hand side of
our invariant is w(j) · ((1 − δ)d2 (j, i) + (ρ − 1)Rj2 ) = w(j) · ((1 − δ)d2 (j, i) + (ρ − 1)(1 − δ)d2 (j, i)).
Notice that since ρ > 1 and δ > 0, we can bound this contribution:

         w(j) · ((1 − δ)d2 (j, i) + (ρ − 1)(1 − δ)d2 (j, i)) = w(j)(1 − δ)d2 (j, i)[1 + (ρ − 1)]
                                                              = w(j)ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, i)

    Let X = {j ∈ DC : d2 (j, i) < d2 (j, S)} denote the clients in DC whose distance to i is
smaller than their distance to S. For clients in X, their ﬁxed penalty Rj does not change,
meaning the change in cost on the right-hand side is strictly driven by the distance dropping
to d2 (j, i).
    Using the bound established above, the change of cost of the right-hand side is at most:
                   X                              X
              fˆ +     w(j) · ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, i) −   w(j) · (1 − δ)(d2 (j, S) − d2 (j, i))
                 j∈A′ ∪IC ′                            j∈X



                                                      45
    Since i is fully paid according to the third bullet of the openability condition, we also have
the capacity bound:
     X                                X                                X
fˆ ≤     w(j)·(τj −ρ(1−δ)d2 (j, i))+       w(j)·(αj −ρ(1−δ)d2 (j, i))+      w(j)·(1−δ)(d2 (j, S)−d2 (j, i))
      j∈A′                           j∈IC ′                             j∈X

   Substituting this capacity bound back into our change of cost, the distance and capacity
terms cancel out, and we get that the change of the right-hand side is at most:
                               X                X
                                    w(j) · τj +     w(j) · αj
                                  j∈A′             j∈IC ′

Because we do not change the α-value after a client is moved to DC, this is exactly the change
of the left-hand side of our invariant. Thus, the inequality holds through Stage 1.
    Stage 2. No facility is open at this stage, and no client is added to DC either. Because
DC is unaltered and no new fˆ is introduced, the invariant remains trivially preserved through
the continuous updates of A and IC clients.

7.2     Dual Feasibility
Lemma 7.4. We have for any i ∈ F ,
              X                                      √
                  w(j) · [αj∗ − (ρ − 1)Rj2 − (Γ + 201 ε)d2 (j, i)]+ ≤ fˆ,                       (36)

where
   • if d is a (general) metric, we choose ρ = 2.5, Γ = 4.9; or

   • if d is a Euclidean metric, we choose ρ = 2, Γ = 3 + ln(2).
Claim 7.5 (No over-bidding). At any point in the algorithm, for every facility i0 ∈ F, we
have
       X                                              X
           w(j) · [(1 − δ)d2 (j, S) − d2 (j, i0 )]+ +   w(j) · [αj − ρd2 (j, i0 )]+ ≤ fˆ.
         j∈DC                                         j∈A∪IC

Proof. Consider an arbitrary facility i0 .
   Stage 1: Suppose that a facility i′ is opened with dual increases (τj )j∈A . Because i′ is
openable, the proposed state satisﬁes the fourth bullet of Deﬁnition 7.1 (dual feasibility) for
any i0 ∈ F :
 X                                        X                            X
     w(j)·[(1−δ)d2 (j, S)−d2 (j, i0 )]+ +   w(j)·[αj −ρd2 (j, i0 )]+ +   w(j)·[τj −ρd2 (j, i0 )]+ ≤ fˆ.
j∈DC                                     j∈IC                          j∈A

    Let us analyze how the left-hand side of our actual state constraint diﬀers from this
openability bound after i′ is added to S and clients are processed. First, observe that for
every active client j ∈ A, its updated dual value
                                               √ eﬀectively becomes τj . This is because Step
1(b) explicitly sets αj ← τj for j ∈ A ∩ B(i′ , εθ), and for active clients outside this ball, the
ﬁrst openability condition guarantees τj = αj . Thus, going into Step 1(c), the duals are τj
for A and remain αj for IC.
    For a client j that was already directly connected (DC) before opening i′ , its contribution
can only decrease, since the distance d2 (j, S ∪ {i′ }) ≤ d2 (j, S).

                                                 46
    For a client j that was active (A) or indirectly connected (IC) and now moves to DC,
it does so precisely because its updated dual value satisﬁes the condition in Step 1(c): τj ≥
ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, S ∪ {i′ }) (if it was from A) or αj ≥ ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, S ∪ {i′ }) (if it was from IC).
Since ρ > 1 and the function [x]+ is non-negative and non-decreasing, we can strictly bound
its new actual contribution. For a client moving from A:
                                                                      +
                                                       τj                                 +
         [(1 − δ)d2 (j, S ∪ {i′ }) − d2 (j, i0 )]+ ≤      − d2 (j, i0 ) ≤ τj − ρd2 (j, i0 ) .
                                                       ρ

The identical algebraic bound holds for a client moving from IC using αj . In both cases, the
client’s new connection contribution is strictly bounded by its corresponding dual term in the
openability bound.
     Finally, for the active or indirectly connected clients that do not move to DC, their actual
dual values are exactly τj and αj , respectively. Whether an active client remains in A or moves
to IC, its contribution is evaluated using its dual variable, resulting in w(j)[τj − ρd2 (j, i0 )]+ .
This perfectly matches its term in the openability bound. Therefore, every term in the updated
state is less than or equal to its corresponding term in the openability bound, guaranteeing
the total sum remains ≤ fˆ.
     Stage 2: In the second stage, we have the following steps: decreasing the α-values of
clients in IC to (1 − δ)d2 (j, S), and increasing the α-values of clients in A to min{(1 +
ε3 )θ, (1 − δ)d2 (j, S)}.
    The ﬁrst step can only decrease the left-hand side of the claim. Hence, it suﬃces to
show that the continuous increase in the second step does not violate the claim. Assume
towards contradiction that at the end of the phase, if we increase αj of every j ∈ A to
min{(1 + ε3 )θ, (1 − δ)d2 (j, S)}, the claim is violated for a non-empty subset of facilities F ′ .
    We select a “minimal” such counter-example: let τ ′ ≤ (1 + ε3 )θ be the smallest value such
that if we set αj′ := min{τ ′ , (1 − δ)d2 (j, S)}, it satisﬁes
X                                 X                                 X
      w(j)[αj′ − ρd2 (i, j)]+ +          w(j)[αj − ρd2 (i, j)]+ +          w(j)[(1 − δ)d2 (j, S) − d2 (i, j)]+ = fˆ
j∈A                               j∈IC                              j∈DC

for some i ∈ F ′ . We remark that τ ′ ≥ θ since the constraint was strictly satisﬁed at the end of
Phase 1. Furthermore, i ∈  / S is not yet open; if it were open, there would be no active client

already opened, which contradicts i ∈ F ′ .                                                        √

The ﬁrst inequality holds by direct substitution of the upper bound τj ≤ αj′ . The second
