Therefore, for any arbitrary facility i0 , this sum cannot strictly exceed fˆ. Since all four
conditions hold, i is openable, which is a contradiction.

   Next, we ﬁx a facility i and show that the dual-feasibility for i is satisﬁed at the end of
the algorithm. We say that facility i is frozen if there exists some open facility i0 such that
                                                    √
                                       d(i, i0 ) ≤ 2 εθ .

Modiﬁed α-values. For analysis purposes, we will introduce a new modiﬁcation of the α-
values for each client j in terms of the ﬁxed facility i. We will denote this modiﬁed α-value as
αjc . This modiﬁed αc -value will be used to write the analog of the constraints in Lemmas 4.9
and 4.10.
      The modiﬁcations
             √          happen only when we open a facility i0 that does not freeze i, i.e., when
d(i, i0 ) > 2 εθ. More speciﬁcally, at the stage with time θ, if we open such a facility i0 that
does not freeze i, we keep αjc as the value before opening i0 . That is, we don’t increase αjc to
                √
τj if j ∈ B(i0 , εθ) (step 1b) for such i0 . In other steps (step 1b for a facility i0 that freezes
i or step 2), we still modify αjc in the same way as we modify αj .


                                                        48
    In the rest of this subsection, we will consider αc instead of α∗ in writing the constraints.
To ensure that the α∗ -value can ensure dual feasibility, we will show that αjc and αj∗ are close
to each other for every client j.

Lemma 7.6. For any client j ∈ D∗ , we have αj∗ − αjc ≤ ε2 · d2 (j, i).

Proof. According to the deﬁnition of αjc , the only case where αjc 6= αj∗ is if at some time θ,
client j becomes directly connected to a facility i0 that does not freeze i. Suppose that αj
is the α-value of j before opening i0 and τj is the α-value of j after opening i0 . Because we
will no longer change the α-value of a client if it is in DC, we have αjc = αj and αj∗ = τj .
Note that τj − √ αj ≤ ε3 θ according to Deﬁnition 7.1. On the other √        hand, if τj > αj , we
have d(j, i0 ) ≤ εθ. Since i0 does not freeze √      i, we know d(i, i0 ) > 2 εθ. By the triangle
inequality, we have d(j, i) ≥ d(i, i0 ) − d(j, i0 ) > εθ. Squaring both sides implies d2 (j, i) > εθ,
which rearranges to θ < 1ε d2 (j, i). Substituting this into our dual diﬀerence yields αj∗ − αjc =
τj − αj ≤ ε3 θ < ε2 d2 (j, i).

   Now, we assume that

                          D∗ = {j ∈ D : αjc − (ρ − 1)Rj2 > Γd2 (j, i)} .                        (37)

Then, it suﬃces to show that
            X                                              √     X

             j∈D ∗                                                j∈D ∗

For simplicity, let us number the clients in D∗ from 1 to s = |D∗ | in the order they become
inactive, breaking ties by order of increasing αc - value. We will use D∗ and [s] interchangeably.

Establishing the constraints.         Next, for each client t ∈ [s]:

   • If t becomes inactive strictly before i is frozen, we deﬁne DC t as the set of clients in
     [t − 1] that are directly connected at the beginning of the stage t becomes inactive,

     t becomes inactive.

     t becomes inactive, IC t = [t − 1] \ DC t , and S t as the set of opened facilities right before
     t becomes inactive.

Claim 7.7. For any t ∈ D∗ that becomes inactive strictly before i becomes frozen,

     w(j)[(1−δ)d2 (j, S t )−d2 (j, i)]+ +   w(j)[αjc −ρd2 (j, i)]+ +   w(j)[αtc −ρd2 (j, i)]+ ≤ fˆ.

Proof. Let us analyze the situation depending on the stage in which t becomes inactive. Since
the claim assumes t becomes inactive strictly before i becomes frozen, any facility i0 opened
at or before the time t becomes inactive cannot freeze i.




                                                  49
Stage 1. Suppose t becomes inactive because of the opening of some facility i0 . Because

    At the beginning of the stage where i0 is opened, let α denote the current dual values. We

   For any j ∈ IC t , the modiﬁed dual value αjc never exceeds the true dual value αj at the

j∈DC t                                   j∈IC t                       j≥t


Stage 2. Suppose that t becomes inactive due to the continuous update of the α-values in

exactly, meaning αtc = αt ≤ (1 + ε3 )θ.
   By Claim 7.5, the over-bidding constraint is satisﬁed for facility i at the exact moment t
becomes inactive:
           X                                          X

           j∈DC                                        j∈A∪IC

    Notice that Stage 2 does not alter the sets DC and IC ∪ A. Therefore, at this moment,
DC t ⊆ DC and IC t ⊆ IC ∪ A. We compare the contributions of each client in this inequality
to the terms in our claim:

   • For j ∈ DC t ⊆ DC, the contribution is identically [(1 − δ)d2 (j, S t ) − d2 (j, i)]+ .

   • For j ∈ IC t , we have αjc ≤ αj since dual values are non-increasing once a client is in
     IC, and our modiﬁcation αc strictly lowers or matches α. Thus, the term for j is upper
     bounded by the corresponding term in the over-bidding claim.

   • For j > t, these clients either remain in A, or becomes inactive at the same stage as t
     but have αjc ≥ αtc . In the ﬁrst case, we have αt = (1 + ε3 )θ, and thus αtc ≤ αt ≤ αj
     because αc -value is non-increasing after becoming inactive. In the second case, we have
     αtc ≤ αjc ≤ αj . Thus, [αtc − ρd2 (j, i)]+ ≤ [αj − ρd2 (j, i)]+ .

Summing these bounds conﬁrms the inequality holds.

Claim 7.8. If i is frozen by i0 with (τj )j∈A , then, for every j ∈ D∗ ∩ A, j becomes inactive
when opening i0 and αjc ≤ τj .




                                                  50
Proof. Consider j ∈ A. First, note that if τj ≥ (1 − δ)d2 (j, i0 ), then j is removed from A when
i0 is opened (moving to either IC or DC), and so αjc ≤ τj since α-values are non-increasing
after a client becomes inactive.
    In the other case, when αj ≤ τj < (1 − δ)d2 (j, i0 ), where αj is the α-value of j before i0
is open, we show that j ∈  / D∗ . Consider the (future) time right after j is removed from the
active clients. The α-value of j then (which upper bounds the ﬁnal αjc ), cannot be strictly
greater than (1 − δ)d2 (j, i0 ) whether it is increased in stage 1 or in stage 2, since we have
i0 ∈ S at that time. By the metric triangle inequality:
                                                                     √ !
                                                       d(i0 , i)       θ
                  d(i, j) ≥ d(j, i0 ) − d(i0 , i) = 1 − √        ·           d(j, i0 )
                                                           θ       d(j, i0 )
                                                                             √
where θ is the value when i0 was open. As i0 freezes i, we have d(i0 , i) ≤ 2 εθ, and we know
θ = αj < (1 − δ)d2 (j, i0 ). Plugging in those bounds to the above inequality yields:
                                              p        
                                d(i, j) > 1 − 2 ε(1 − δ) d(j, i0 ) .
                                                          p
Because ε ∈ (0, 10−100 ) is suﬃciently small, the term 2 ε(1 − δ) is strictly less than 1,
meaning the distance remains strictly positive. Squaring both sides gives:
                                             p        2
                             d2 (i, j) > 1 − 2 ε(1 − δ) d2 (j, i0 ) .
                              p        2
   Since Γ > 2, we have Γ 1 − 2 ε(1 − δ) > 1 > 1 − δ. Thus:

                                 Γd2 (i, j) > (1 − δ)d2 (j, i0 ) ≥ αjc .

Hence, αjc < Γd2 (i, j), which implies j cannot be in D∗ .

Claim 7.9. For any t ∈ D∗ ,
 X                                           X                          X
     w(j)((1 − δ)d2 (j, S t ) − d2 (j, i)) +   w(j)(αjc − ρd2 (j, i)) +   w(j)(αtc − ρd2 (j, i)) ≤ fˆ.
j∈DC t                                    j∈IC t                           j≥t

Proof. Consider the point in the execution at which t becomes inactive. As established by
Claim 7.8, if a facility i0 freezes i, every active client j ∈ D∗ ∩ A becomes inactive during the
exact step i0 is opened. Therefore, no client in D∗ can become inactive after i is frozen. This
leaves us with exactly two exhaustive cases for t ∈ D∗ :

Case 1: t becomes inactive strictly before i is frozen. By Claim 7.7,
 X                                        X                          X
     w(j)[(1−δ)d2 (j, S t )−d2 (j, i)]+ +   w(j)[αjc −ρd2 (j, i)]+ +   w(j)[αtc −ρd2 (j, i)]+ ≤ fˆ.
j∈DC t                                    j∈IC t                           j≥t


Dropping the [·]+ completes the proof for this case.




                                                   51
Case 2: t becomes inactive exactly when i is frozen. Suppose t becomes inactive due
to the opening of a facility i0 that freezes i. Because all clients in D∗ ∩ A become inactive
at this step (Claim 7.8), and our ordering of 1, . . . , s in D∗ is determined by the time they
become inactive, it must be that all clients j ≥ t in D∗ become inactive simultaneously at this
step. Furthermore, because ties are broken by order of increasing αc -value, we are guaranteed
that αtc ≤ αjc for all j ≥ t.
    Now, consider the fourth bullet of openability (dual feasibility) for i0 evaluated at the
ﬁxed facility i, just before i0 is opened:
 X                                          X                          X
      w(j)[(1 − δ)d2 (j, S) − d2 (j, i)]+ +   w(j)[αj − ρd2 (j, i)]+ +   w(j)[τj − ρd2 (j, i)]+ ≤ fˆ.
j∈DC                                     j∈IC                          j∈A

    Thus, letting Abef ore and ICbef ore be the sets A and IC just before i0 is opened, we have
exactly S = S t , DC t ⊆ DC, and IC t = (IC t ∩ ICbef ore ) ∪ (IC t ∩ Abef ore ). Note also that
t ∈ IC t ∩ Abef ore , and all j > t belong to Abef ore .
    We drop the non-negative [·]+ operators from the openability constraint and restrict the
sums to our subsets of interest to establish a strict lower bound on the left-hand side:

   • For j ∈ DC t ⊆ DC, we drop the positive part. Because S = S t , we exactingly have:
     [(1 − δ)d2 (j, S) − d2 (j, i)]+ ≥ (1 − δ)d2 (j, S t ) − d2 (j, i).

   • For j ∈ IC t ∩ ICbef ore , the modiﬁed dual never exceeds the true dual prior to freezing,
     so αjc ≤ αj . Thus: [αj − ρd2 (j, i)]+ ≥ αj − ρd2 (j, i) ≥ αjc − ρd2 (j, i).

   • For j ∈ IC t ∩ Abef ore , these clients are active and in D∗ . By Claim 7.8, we have
     αjc ≤ τj . Evaluated in the summation over A, we have: [τj −ρd2 (j, i)]+ ≥ τj −ρd2 (j, i) ≥
     αjc −ρd2 (j, i). (Notice that combining this with the previous bullet perfectly reconstructs
     the αjc − ρd2 (j, i) term for all j ∈ IC t ).

   • For j ≥ t, these clients are in D∗ ∩ Abef ore . By Claim 7.8, αjc ≤ τj . Applying our
     tie-breaking rule, αtc ≤ αjc ≤ τj . Evaluated in the A sum, we have: [τj − ρd2 (j, i)]+ ≥
     τj − ρd2 (j, i) ≥ αtc − ρd2 (j, i).

   Because all subsets are disjoint and strictly contained within the original openability sums,
summing these lower bounds yields:
 X                                           X                          X
     w(j)((1 − δ)d2 (j, S t ) − d2 (j, i)) +   w(j)(αjc − ρd2 (j, i)) +   w(j)(αtc − ρd2 (j, i)) ≤ fˆ.
j∈DC t                                    j∈IC t                       j≥t

This matches the required inequality, completely resolving the case.

   For any client t and j ∈ DC t , deﬁne
                                                √
                                       rjt :=       1 − δd(j, S t ).

Next, we will prove Lemma 7.4 for the general metric setting and the Euclidean setting
separately based appropriate choices of ρ and Γ.




                                                     52
General metric
Now, we will show Lemma 7.4 for ρ = 2.5 and Γ = 4.9. To this end, let’s show that
(IC t ), (DC t ), (αjc ), (Rj ), (rjt ) satisfy all the constraints of Lemma 4.12, restated below for
convenience.

Lemma 4.12. Let ρ = 2.5 and Γ = 4.9. Let fˆ be any non-negative real number. Let
([s] ∪ {i}, d) be a metric. Let w : [s] → R>0 be any positive weight function. Let (IC t )t∈[s] and
(DC t )t∈[s] be two families of sets satisfying:
  (a) For each t ∈ [s], DC t ∩ IC t = ∅ and DC t ∪ IC t = [t − 1].
  (b) For each t ∈ [s − 1], DC t ⊆ DC t+1 .
Suppose that the variables (αj∗ )j∈[s] , (Rj )j∈[s] , (rjt )t∈[s],j∈DC t satisfy the following constraints
  (1) For any t ∈ [s], j ∈ DC t , we have rjt ≤ Rj .
  (2) For each t ∈ [s], j ∈ DC t , we have αt∗ ≤ (rjt + d(j, t))2 .
  (3) For each t ∈ [s], we have
       X             X              X                            X                              X
           w(ℓ)αt∗ +      w(ℓ)αℓ∗ +        2
                                      w(j)rjt ≤ fˆ+                          ρw(ℓ)d2 (ℓ, i)+            w(j)d2 (j, i).
      ℓ∈[t:s]         ℓ∈IC t          j∈DC t                 ℓ∈IC t ∪[t:s]                     j∈DC t


Then, the following holds:
                   X                                         X
                       w(j) · (αj∗ − (ρ − 1)Rj2 ) ≤ fˆ + Γ ·   w(j) · d2 (j, i) .                               (9)
                     j∈[s]                                       j∈[s]

    By deﬁnition of (IC t )t and (DC t )t , the two constraints on them hold. Let j ∈ DC t . Since
Rj is the distance to the ﬁrst facility that j directly connects to, and d(j, S t ) is non-increasing
in t, we have rjt ≤ Rj for any t > j. Now, let t ∈ [s], we have:

                               αtc ≤ αt∗
                                   ≤ (1 − δ)d2 (t, S t )
                                      √                  √

   Hence, by Lemma 4.12, we have:
                   X                                         X
                       w(j) · (αjc − (ρ − 1)Rj2 ) ≤ fˆ + Γ ·   w(j) · d2 (j, i).

Euclidean metric
Now, we will show Lemma 7.4 for ρ = 2, Γ = 3 + ln 2, and d a Euclidean metric. Let a be the
index of the last client that becomes inactive strictly before i is frozen.
   First, let’s prove the following claim:



                                                    53
Claim 7.10. For each t ∈ [a], j ∈ DC t and ℓ ∈ IC t ∪ {t}, we have:

                                         αℓc ≤ (rjt + d(j, ℓ))2 .

Proof. Consider any client t ∈ [a]. Let Pt be the phase in which t becomes inactive. Consider
the state of the algorithm at the exact beginning of phase Pt . By deﬁnition, S t is the set of
open facilities at this time.
   Consider any client ℓ ∈ IC t ∪ {t}. As ℓ ≤ t, ℓ becomes inactive in phase Pt or in an earlier
phase. Next, we prove that αℓc ≤ (1 − δ)d2 (ℓ, S t ) by discussing two cases:

   • If ℓ becomes inactive strictly before phase Pt , then ℓ is indirectly connected at the
     beginning of phase Pt . Because of the Stage 2 continuous updates at the end of the
     previous phase, we have αℓc ≤ αℓ = (1 − δ)d2 (ℓ, S t ).

   • Otherwise (i.e., if ℓ becomes inactive during phase Pt ), we can prove αℓc ≤ (1−δ)d2 (ℓ, S t )
     by contradiction. Suppose that αℓc > (1−δ)d2 (ℓ, S t ). Let θ be the active dual parameter
     at the beginning of phase Pt . Since ℓ is active at this time, θ = αℓc > (1 − δ)d2 (ℓ, S t ).
     Then, ℓ would have been moved to IC during the Stage 2 update of the previous phase
     (when the threshold (1 + ε3 )θprev = θ exceeded the connection cost), contradicting that
     ℓ is still active at the beginning of phase Pt . Therefore, αℓc ≤ θ ≤ (1 − δ)d2 (ℓ, S t ).

    Because the triangle inequality holds for the Euclidean metric d, we have d(ℓ, S t ) ≤
d(j, S t ) + d(j, ℓ). Taking the square root of our established bound, we get:
                                  p c √
                                    αℓ ≤ 1 − δd(ℓ, S t )
                                          √
                                       ≤ 1 − δ(d(j, S t ) + d(j, ℓ))
                                               √
                                       = rjt + 1 − δd(j, ℓ)
                          √
   Since δ > 0, we have 1 − δ < 1. Because metric distances are non-negative, replacing
the scaled distance with the full distance preserves the inequality:
                                      p c
                                        αℓ ≤ rjt + d(j, ℓ)

    Squaring both sides yields αℓc ≤ (rjt + d(j, ℓ))2 , completing the proof.

    We will make several cases according to how i gets frozen.

algorithm, its dual value is never increased during Stage 1 updates, strictly ensuring αℓc ≤ θ.
Combining this with our geometric upper bound gives the exact ﬁnal relation:

                                    αℓc ≤ θ ≤ (rjs + d(j, ℓ′ ))2 ,

completing the proof of the fourth bullet.

    Thus, we can apply Lemma 5.20. We get:
             X                                             √     X
                 w(j) · (αjc − (ρ − 1)Rj2 ) ≤ fˆ + (Γ + 200 ε) ·   w(j) · d2 (j, i).
              j∈[s]                                                   j∈[s]

This completes the proof.


8     Approximation with O(log n/ε3 ) Extra Centers
In this section, we present bi-criteria approximations with O(log n/ε3 ) extra centers, as
claimed in Theorem 1.3 (restated in Theorem 8.7). For simplicity, we will assume that ρ ∈ [2, 3]
in the rest of this section.

8.1    Pre-processing
For this section, we will consider the case where the distances between clients and facilities
are lower bounded by 1, thanks to the following variant of Lemma 3.3:

Lemma 8.1. For any given ε > 0 and α > 1, given a polynomial-time α-approximation
algorithm for Euclidean k-Means on instances with distances between 1 and 2n3 /ε3 + 1, there
exists a polynomial-time α(1 + O(ε))-approximation algorithm for Euclidean k-Means on gen-
eral instances.

Proof. Given Lemma 3.3, it suﬃces to prove the following argument:

      For any given ε > 0 and α > 1, given a polynomial-time α-approximation algo-
      rithm for Euclidean k-Means on instances with distances between 1 and 2n3 /ε3 +1,
      there exists a polynomial-time α(1 + O(ε))-approximation algorithm for Euclidean
      k-Means on instances with non-zero distances between 1 and n2 /ε2 .




                                                  59
    Suppose that we have a Euclidean k-Means instance with non-zero distances between 1
and n2 /ε2 (namely, the old instance), in which x : D ∪ F → Rdim denotes the coordinate of
each point. Now, we index D ∪ F by 1, 2, . . . , |D| + |F | arbitrarily. We construct the following
instance (namely, the new instance) by assigning each point at x′ : D t F → Rdim+|D|+|F | ,
where x′ (i) = ( 2n

which has a value of 1 on coordinate i and a value of 0 on all other coordinates. Note that we
will separate two copies for i ∈ D ∩ F in the new instance. Let d and d′ denote the Euclidean
metric induced by the old and new instances, respectively. Consider any i ∈ F, j ∈ D. We
have
                                4n2                                   4n2 2

It is easy to see that d′ (i, j) ∈ [1, 2n

    Now, we assume that we have an α-approximation on the new instance. We ﬁrst run a
constant-factor approximation k-Center on the old instance and decide if the optimal value
for the old instance OP T on k-Means is 0. If it’s not, it is guaranteed that OP T is at least 1.
According to Equation (38), the optimal value of the new instance OP T ′ is 4n2 /ε2 ·OP T +2n ≥
4n2 /ε2 + 2n. Then we run the α-approximation on the new instance, getting a solution with
value ALG′ on the new instance such that ALG′ ≤ α · OP T ′ . We obtain the solution ALG on

Again, because of Equation (38), we have ALG′ = 4n2 /ε2 · ALG + 2n. This implies, on the

   We shall remark here that under this assumption, our log-adaptive Algorithm 2 still runs
in O(log n/ε3 ) phases.

8.2    Walking Between Two Solutions: Setup
This subsection is an adaptation of Subsection 5.2 of [CCG+ 26] to our new bidding strategy.
    Given the description of the log-adaptive algorithm, we present our ﬁnal bicriteria ap-
proximation MergeSolutions. It results in a solution that opens k + O(log n) centers by
maintaining two partial solutions that lead to opening at least k and at most k centers re-
spectively, and gradually merging them into a single solution that opens exactly k + O(log n)
facilities, exploiting the O(log n) adaptivity of the algorithm.
    Before detailing the merging procedure MergeSolutions, it is helpful to observe that we
may generalize the analysis to make it more “robust”. We introduce this more robust analysis
in this subsection, where we also deﬁne the notation that will be used later to describe
MergeSolutions.
    Starting from the two solutions obtained in the standard way whose facility costs diﬀer by
at most η := 2−n , the two solutions we maintain will have an exponentially small diﬀerence η
in one of their parameters. (This parameter will be either the facility cost f , some distance
related to a free facility that will be introduced later, or some probability that a client is in IC
or DC.) For this reason, when we merge the two solutions, it may occur that some facilities
in one solution are almost completely paid for, rather than fully paid for, or that the dual

                                                    60
constraint of some facilities is slightly violated. To address this, we generalize the deﬁnition
of “openable” to “η-openable”, where the only diﬀerence is an O(nη) slack in the third and
fourth conditions.
     Note that, in the algorithm, each inactive client j is always either directly connected
to some facility i∗ or indirectly connected to some facility i∗ , where i∗ is given by i∗ =
arg mini∈S d2 (j, i). In the rest of this section, we will maintain this invariant, but allow some
ﬂexibility to its connection type and α-value. Speciﬁcally, we allow each client j ∈ D to have
a set of copies Dj , with each j̃ ∈ Dj deﬁned by (i) the connection type ct(j̃), i.e., whether
it is in A, IC or DC;  P (ii) the α-value α̃(j̃); (iii) the probability mass µ(j̃). The probability
mass should satisfy j̃∈Dj µ(j̃) = 1. In particular, for each client j, Dj has two possibilities:

   • Dj has only one copy that is of type A and with probability mass 1;

   • Dj has some copies of type DC and at most one copy of type IC.

In short, each Dj contains at most one copy that is of type IC or A. We will say j is active
if the ﬁrst bullet holds, and inactive if the second bullet holds. In this way, we deﬁne the
αI -value of a client as the α-value of its active copy or the indirectly connected copy. If the
client has no such copy, we say its αI -value is 0 (as this value will not be used). We remark
this αI -value is diﬀerent from the α-values of the copies, and will only be used when we merge
two solutions (Subsection 8.3). Moreover, we will treat each copy j̃ as a normal weighted
client located at the same point as j in the metric space. When we compute the total bid, we
average out over their diﬀerent copies.
    Further, we consider D as the collection of (succinct) copies by dropping all the α-values
of directly connected copies from ∪j∈D Dj . This collection will be used to deﬁne the algorithm
that merges two solutions. In this way, we can still deduce αI values for each client and this
set can be independent of the α-values of directly connected copies, which are not necessary
in deﬁning the algorithm.
    Equivalently, for each inactive client j, we consider its connection as a discrete probability
distribution over (i) the connection type ctj , i.e., whether it is in IC or DC; (ii) the α-value of
the client αj . In particular, in the support of this distribution, there can be only one possible
α-value for indirect connects, i.e., there exists at most one value αI such that Pr[(ctj , αj ) =
(IC, αI )] > 0. When we compute the total bid, we average out their contributions. Adapting
to our new dual ﬁtting approach, the expected contribution of client j to facility i now
becomes:
                                                             
                  E (1 − δ)[d2 (j, S) − d2 (j, i)]+ | ctj = DC · Pr[ctj = DC]
                                                                   
                          +E [αj − ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, i)]+ | ctj = IC · Pr[ctj = IC],

where the probability and the expectations are all taken over the discrete distribution. Letting
π(j) = Pr[ctj = DC] and letting αjI be the only possible α-value for indirect connects of j,
we can rewrite the above as:

            π(j) · (1 − δ)[d2 (j, S) − d2 (j, i)]+ + (1 − π(j)) · [αjI − ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, i)]+

For each active client j, the connection distribution is a distribution with one element in the
support having type ctj = A. Therefore, we can deﬁne π(j) = 0 and let αjI be the α-value of
j. In the rest of this section, we will use both points of view interchangeably.
    Given the time θ = (1+ε3 )p−1 , the sequence H = (H1 , . . . , Hp ) of the sequences of facilities
opened by each stage 1 of the algorithm that was executed so far, we easily can obtain the set

                                                  61
of open facilities S. Further given the collection of copies D for the clients at the beginning
of the phase, we can deduce the α-value for each indirectly connected or active copy. Then,
we can use the following process RetrieveNewCopies, which is the third step of stage 1 of
our modiﬁed log-adaptive algorithm, to obtain the current collection of copies:

  RetrieveNewCopies (D, Hp )
  For each copy j ∈ D that is active or indirectly connected:
     • Let αI be its α-value and j be the client it corresponds to.
     • If αI ≥ ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, Hp ), we modify the copy to be directly connected and drop its
       α-value.
     • Else if αI ≥ (1 − δ)d2 (j, Hp ), we modify the copy to be indirectly connected and
       keep its α-value unchanged.

    Given the output of the procedure, we can deduce all current copies for each client, and
their corresponding connection types, probability mass and α-values. Based on these variables,
we have a new deﬁnition of η-openability tailored for our new dual ﬁtting approach.

Deﬁnition 8.2 (η-openable). Consider the time θ = (1 + ε3 )p−1 . Let H = (H1 , . . . , Hp−1 , Hp )
be the sequence of sequences of opened facilities so far and let D be the collection of copies
for all clients at the beginning of the phase p. Then sets S, A, IC, DC can be deduced from H
and RetrieveNewCopies (D, Hp ) deﬁned as above.
     For each copy j, we can also deduce its probability mass µ(j), and its α-value αj if j ∈
IC ∪ A. We say that a facility i ∈ F is η-openable (with respect to θ, H and D) if there are
(τj )j∈A that satisfy the following conditions.

   • Only nearby clients are increased:
                                                                        √
                                  τj = αj for every j ∈ A \ B(i,            εθ).

   • Nearby clients are only increased slightly:
                                                                                            √
               αj ≤ τj ≤ min{(1 − δ)d2 (j, S), (1 + ε3 )θ} for every j ∈ A ∩ B(i,               εθ).

   • Facility i is almost paid for:
                   X                                     X
         (1 − δ)       µ(j) · [d2 (j, S) − d2 (j, i)]+ +   µ(j) · [αj − ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, i)]+
                  j∈DC                                  j∈IC

    Similar as [CGL+ 25; CCG+ 25; CCG+ 26], we introduce the concept of free facilities. When
we merge the two solutions, we introduce free facilities, whose opening costs are not necessarily
paid. We will introduce at most O(1) such free facilities in each phase. However, we do not
restrict the number of free copies a regular facility may have. Let Sfree ⊆ F denote the
multiset of free facilities that have been opened.
    Moreover, we augment the metric space for the free facilities. For each free copy ĩ ∈ Sfree
of a regular facility i ∈ F , we deﬁne a parameter u(ĩ) ≥ 0, which acts as the squared distance
overhead of the free copy. Therefore, for any point x in the metric space (either a facility or
a client), the distance squared is given by d2 (ĩ, j) = u(ĩ) + d2 (i, j).
    We further introduce the following terminology: as mentioned, we refer to the facilities in
Sfree as free facilities, the facilities in F and Sreg = S − Sfree as regular facilities, and when we
simply mention “facilities”, we are referring to their union.                                   q
    Note that if d is Euclidean, we can have one additional dimension, which equals                         u(ĩ)
for any free facility ĩ and equals 0 for any regular facility or client, to maintain the Euclidean
property.
    Finally, we will use the letters i and h to denote facilities. Speciﬁcally, we use i ∈ F to
denote a regular facility and use ĩ to denote a free copy of i. When referring to a facility that
can be either free or regular, we use the letter h.
    We now introduce the deﬁnition of valid sequences, which aims to capture the sequence of
facilities that are opened during the while-loop of stage 1 of the log-adaptive algorithm. Let

                                                  63
us say H′ = (H1′ , . . . , Hp′ ) is a super-sequence of H = (H1 , . . . , Hp ) and denote it H′ ⊇ H if
they have a common preﬁx of length p − 1, i.e., for all i < p, Hi′ = Hi , and Hp′ is a super-
sequence of Hp . Given two sequences H1 = (H1 , . . . , Hr ) and H2 = (Hr+1 , . . . , Hl ), we deﬁne
H1 ⊕ H2 = (H1 , . . . , Hl ) as the concatenation of the two sequences.

Deﬁnition 8.4 (η-valid sequence). Consider the time θ. Let H be the sequence of sequences
of opened facilities and D be the collection of copies at the beginning of the phase. A sequence
hh1 , . . . , hℓ i of facilities is η-valid (with respect to θ, D and H), if the following conditions
hold.

   • Each ht is either free or η-openable with respect to the time θ, a super-sequence H′ ⊇
     H ⊕ (hh1 , . . . , ht−1 i) of the sequence of sequences of opened facilities, where ⊕ is just the
     concatenation, and the collection D.

   • The sequence is maximal in terms of (0-)openability, i.e., there is no (0-)openable facility
     with respect to the time θ, H ⊕ (hh1 , . . . , hℓ i), and the collection D.

    We consider the following procedure CompleteCopies to return the collection of copies
after opening a sequence of facilities Hp during the ﬁrst stage of the phase. This procedure is
identical to executing stage 2 of the log-adaptive algorithm on the collection of copies.

  CompleteCopies (θ, D, H = (H1 , . . . , Hp ))

  Set S = ∪r∈[p] Hr and D′ = RetrieveNewCopies(D, Hp ). For each copy j ∈ D′ :

     • If j is of type IC, set αj ← (1 − δ)d2 (j, S).

     • If j is of type A, set αj ← min{(1 + ε3 )θ, (1 − δ)d2 (j, S)}, and change its connection
       type to IC when αj = (1 − δ)d2 (j, S).

Deﬁnition 8.5 (Valid collection of copies). Consider the time θ = (1 + ε3 )p−1 . Let H =
(H1 , . . . , Hp ) be the sequence of sequences of opened facilities and D be the collection of copies
after the ﬁrst p − 1 phases. A collection of copies D′ is valid (with respect to θ, H, D), if D′
can be obtained by the following procedure.

      Suppose D′′ = CompleteCopies(θ, D, H). Then, we can obtain D′ by the fol-
      lowing process: for each copy j that is indirectly connected or active in D but is
      directly connected in D′′ , if ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, S) ≤ αj ≤ ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, S) + 4η (where αj
      is the α-value of j in D), then it can be split into a directly connected copy with an
      arbitrarily smaller probability mass, while the remaining probability mass is given
      to an indirectly connected copy with α-value αj′ = (1 − δ)d2 (j, S).

In particular, if D′ = D′′ , we say that D′ is perfectly valid.

    We remark that

   • The log-adaptive algorithm generates 0-valid sequences in the ﬁrst stage of each phase.
     Speciﬁcally, it generates sequences H = (H1 , H2 , . . . , HL ) and a history of the collections
     of copies DH = (D0 , D1 , . . . , DL ), where each Hp is 0-valid (with respect to θ = (1 +
     ε3 )p−1 , Dp−1 and H) and contains only regular facilities.


                                                 64
   • The log-adaptive algorithm only maintains a single copy for each inactive client, i.e.,
     each inactive client j is either directly connected to some facility i with probability 1 or
     indirectly connected to some facility i with probability 1. Therefore, each collection of
     copies Dp maintained by the log-adaptive algorithm is perfectly valid (with respect to
     θ = (1 + ε3 )p−1 , H and Dp−1 ).

   • The set S of opened facilities is such that A = ∅ at the ﬁnal time θ.

    For a general sequence of sequences H = (H1 , H2 , . . . , HL ) and a history of collection of
copies DH = (D0 , D1 , . . . , DL ), let us say they consist of η-valid sequences and valid collections
of copies if, for every p ∈ [L],

   • Hp is an η-valid sequence with respect to θ = (1 + ε3 )p−1 , H, Dp−1 ; and

   • Dp is a valid collection of copies with respect to θ = (1 + ε3 )p−1 , H, Dp−1 .

It is easy to see from the deﬁnitions that the following claim holds.

Claim 8.6. Suppose that H, DH consist of η-valid sequences and valid collections of copies.
For each p ∈ [L], letting S = ∪r∈[p] Hr , then we have

   • If j ∈ Dp is indirectly connected, then αj = (1 − δ)d2 (j, S);

   • If j ∈ Dp is active, then αj = (1 + ε3 )p and (1 + ε3 )p < (1 − δ)d2 (j, S).

    Furthermore, we say that H, DH form a solution with open facilities S = ∪r∈[L] Hr if there
are no active copies in DL . Otherwise, we say that H, DH form a partial solution.
    The results in Section 7, namely Lemmas 7.3 and 7.4, can thus be stated as: if H, DH is a
solution of 0-valid sequences consisting of regular facilities, then the set S of opened facilities
satisﬁes
                       X                       Γ
                           w(j) · d2 (j, S) ≤      · (optLP (f ) − f · |S|) .
                                              1−δ
                         j∈D

We now allow an additive 8nη slack in the third bullet (facility paying) and fourth bullet (dual
feasibility) of the openability (Deﬁnition 8.2). In addition, in the second stage of each phase,
we allow a directly connected copy j that satisﬁes ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, S) ≤ αj < ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, S) +
(1 + ρ)η to split into two copies with arbitrary probability mass: one is identical, while the
other is indirectly connected with α-value αj = (1 − δ)d2 (j, S). All these modiﬁcations lead
to the following generalization.

Theorem 8.7. Consider a solution H, DH of η-valid sequences and valid collections of copies.
Let S be the opened facilities. Further, let Sfree and Sreg = S − Sfree be the free copies and
regular facilities, respectively. Then, we have
                    X                     √

                        d (j, S) ≤

where

   • if d is a (general) metric, we choose ρ = 2.5, Γ = 4.9; or

   • if d is a Euclidean metric, we choose ρ = 2, Γ = 3 + ln(2).


                                                  65
   In the next subsection, we will merge two solutions into one with |Sreg | = k and |Sfree | =
O(log n/ε3 ). To our help, we will use two basic routines deﬁned as follows:
   • CompleteSolutionf,u (H, DH) takes a partial solution H, DH as input and returns
     a solution by running the while-loop of the log-adaptive algorithm using f, u and the
     existing copies induced by the last collection in DH.
      In particular, we also consider a slightly diﬀerent input for this routine. If H =
      (H1 , . . . , Hp ) and DH = (D0 , . . . , Dp−1 ), where the ﬁrst p − 1 sequences of H form a
      partial solution with DH, and Hp is η-valid. Then, we will ﬁrst generate Dp by Com-
      pleteCopies (θ = (1 + ε3 )p−1 , Dp−1 , (H1 , . . . , Hp )), append it to the end of DH (so
      that the resulting H, DH form a partial solution), and then start from phase p + 1 of
      the log-adaptive algorithm.
   • CompleteSequencef,u (H = (H1 , . . . , Hp−1 ), Hp = hh1 , . . . , hℓ i, DH = (D0 , . . . , Dp−1 ))
     where H, DH form a partial solution, and Hp is an η-valid sequence except that it may
     not be maximal. CompleteSequence then runs the while-loop of the log-adaptive
     algorithm starting with Hp and the existing copies induced by the last collection DH
     to return a maximal sequence of Hp (with respect to f, u).
We note that all facilities added by CompleteSolution and CompleteSequence are 0-
openable and regular. We also note that, given Dp−1 and H, CompleteSolution generates
Dp by CompleteCopies (θ = (1+ε3 )p−1 , Dp−1 , (H1 , . . . , Hp )). Additionally, for intuition, ob-
serve that, with the above notation, the set of facilities opened by CompleteSolutionf,u (∅, (D0 ))
is equivalent to that opened by the log-adaptive algorithm, where D0 denotes the collection of
copies in which each client has only one copy with (ct = A, µ = 1, α = 1). (There is no free facil-
ity here.) To simplify notation, we only use CompleteSequence when the partial solution H
and DH are clear from the context, so we simply write CompleteSequence(f,u) (hh1 , . . . , hℓ i).
Furthermore, we omit (f, u) when the parameters are clear from the context.

8.3    MergeSolutions
This subsection is almost identical to Subsection 5.3 of [CCG+ 26]. The main diﬀerence is the
proof of Claim 8.8 in Subsection 8.3.1.
    We now present our procedure MergeSolutions that walks between two solutions. We
will maintain two solutions (H, DH, f, u) and (H′ , DH′ , f ′ , u′ ) whose numbers of open regular
facilities sandwich k. (Formally, let us say a and b sandwich k if a < k < b or b < k < a.) We
will gradually make them closer until one of them opens exactly k regular facilities. We start
from the two initial solutions obtained using the standard method. Note that the minimum
nonzero distance is 1 and the maximum squared distance is M = poly(n), which implies that
opt ≤ nM .

Initialization. We binary search on f in the interval [1/n2 , 4nM ] and run the routine
CompleteSolutionf,u (∅, hD0 i) (with zero u). When f = 1/n2 , every client j ∈ D satisﬁes
d(j, S) = 0 if d(j, F) = 0 and d2 (j, S) ≤ d2 (j, F) + 2/n2 otherwise, as each client can open
its closest facility by itself when αj = d2 (j, F) + 2/n2 . If this solution opens at most k
centers, then it is already a (1 + 2/n)-approximation. On the other hand, when f = 4nM , the
algorithm will open exactly one facility, since the ﬁrst facility is open when θ ≥ 2M , so every
client becomes immediately inactive when it is open. Therefore, the binary search yields two
solutions (H, DH, f, u) and (H′ , DH′ , f ′ , u) where (H, DH, f, u) opens less than k facilities
and (H′ , DH′ , f ′ , u) opens more than k facilities where f ≤ f ′ ≤ f + η.

                                                  66
General Step. MergeSolutions works on phase p = 1, 2, . . . iteratively, and ensures
that the two solutions are identical up to the p-th phase. Formally, MergeSolutions takes
two solutions (H, DH, f, u) and (H′ , DH′ , f ′ , u′ ) as well as the current phase p as input. The
following promises on them will be satisﬁed whenever we call MergeSolutions.

  (i) Both consist of η-valid sequences.

 (ii) The numbers of open regular facilities by the two solutions sandwich k.

 (iii) At most one parameter, which we call the diﬀerence parameter, diﬀers by at most η
       between the two solutions, i.e., if f 6= f ′ then |f − f ′ | ≤ η and u(ĩ) = u′ (ĩ) for every
       ĩ ∈ Sfree ; otherwise if f = f ′ there is at most one free facility ĩ ∈ Sfree so that u(ĩ) 6= u′ (ĩ)
       and |u(ĩ) − u′ (ĩ)| ≤ η. (We don’t consider π, π ′ deﬁned in the next bullet as diﬀerence
       parameters.)

 (iv) The probability that each client is directly connected to any facility is almost the same
      in both solutions, i.e., we have that for every client j, |π(j) − π ′ (j)| ≤ η/M , where π, π ′
      are the probabilities of direct connections for the collection of copies Dp−1 and Dp−1    ′    ,
      respectively.

 (v) H = (H1 , H2 , . . . , HL ) and H′ = (H1′ , H2′ , . . . , HL′ ′ ) have a common preﬁx of length
     p − 1, i.e., Hi = Hi′ for i = 1, 2, . . . , p − 1. Furthermore, the remaining sequences
     Hp , . . . , HL and Hp′ , . . . , HL′ ′ only contain regular facilities and are 0-valid with respect
     to the parameters f, u and f ′ , u′ , respectively. (This implies that H and H′ open the
     same set of free facilities.)

Given the promises, the goal of MergeSolutions is to

 (1) either ﬁnd a solution of η-valid sequences that opens exactly k regular facilities and
     O(log n) free facilities, or

 (2) produce two new solutions that satisfy the above promises for phase p + 1.

    Since the maximum squared distance between any two points is M ≤ poly(n) and f ≤
4nM , before θ reaches 6M , at least one facility will open and every client will become inactive.
Therefore, for some threshold p∗ = O(log n/ε3 ), once H and H′ agree on the ﬁrst p∗ phases,
they open exactly the same set of facilities which violates the promise that the numbers of
open regular facilities sandwich k. Therefore, case (1) must happen for some p ≤ p∗ , which
yields a solution of η-valid sequences that opens exactly k regular facilities and O(log n) free
facilities. Combined with Theorem 8.7, this proves our main bicriteria guarantee. Indeed, we
then have a solution consisting of open centers S with |S| = k + O(log n/ε3 ) such that, by
Theorem 8.7,
             X                           Γ
                   w(j) · d2 (j, S) ≤       · (optLP (f ) − (f − ηn) · k) ≤ (Γ + O(ε))opt ,
                                        1−δ
             j∈D

where, for the last inequality, we used that η = 2−n and opt ≥ 1 (since any non-zero distance
is at least 1). The main theorem then follows by scaling ε appropriately.
    In the remainder of this section, we present MergeSolutions for two solutions and
a given phase p. It consists of four subroutines, presented in the subsequent subsections
respectively. Each of the ﬁrst three subroutines achieves (1) or (2) without condition (iv)


                                                     67
above (which ends the whole procedure or lets us move on to the fourth subroutine), or sets
up the stage for the next subroutine. The third subroutine always achieves (1) or (2) without
condition (iv). The fourth subroutine, then achieves (2), for those not achieving (1), by
ensuring condition (iv).

which is a valid call by Claim 8.8. If H′′ opens k regular facilities, we return this solu-
tion. Otherwise, as (H, DH, f, u) and (H′ , DH′ , f ′ , u′ ) sandwich k, we must have that either
(H, DH, f, u) and (H′′ , DH′′ , f, u), or (H′′ , DH′′ , f, u) and (H′ , DH′ , f ′ , u′ ) sandwich k.
     On the one hand, if (H′′ , DH′′ , f, u) and (H′ , DH′ , f ′ , u′ ) sandwich k, then we made
progress in that we now have two solutions with a common preﬁx of length p (instead of
p − 1) and exactly one parameter diﬀers by at most η. However, the new collection of copies
Dp′′ and Dp′ may not satisfy condition (iv) regarding the bounds on the probabilities of direct
connections (π). In this case, we proceed to Subsection 8.3.4 of phase p.
     On the other hand, if (H, DH, f, u) and (H′′ , DH′′ , f, u) sandwich k, then these two solu-
tions have the exact same parameters f and u, have Dr = Dr′′ for every r ≤ p − 1, and their
ﬁrst η-valid sequences that diﬀer are Hp and Hp′ . In this case, we rename H′′ to H′ (and DH′′
to DH′ ) and proceed to the next step.

8.3.2     Maximize Common Preﬁx of Hp and Hp′
We are given two solutions (H, DH, f, u) and (H′ , DH′ , f, u) on the same parameters f, u
that sandwich k. It is guaranteed that ∀r ≤ p − 1, Dr = Dr′ in the history of collections of
copies. Moreover, if we let H = (H1 , . . . , HL ) and H′ = (H1′ , . . . , HL′ ′ ), then Hi = Hi′ for
i = 1, . . . , p − 1 and Hp 6= Hp′ . In addition, Hp and Hp′ only contain regular facilities.
    In this step of MergeSolutions, we increase the common preﬁx of Hp and Hp′ by “slowly”
modifying Hp′ . As Hp only contains regular facilities, we let Hp = hi1 , i2 , . . . , iℓ i. Moreover,
as the parameters (f, u) and D1 , . . . , Dp−1 are clear from the context, we omit them in the
following when, e.g., using the deﬁnition of η-openability, and calling the procedures Com-
pleteSolution and CompleteSequence.7
    This subsection proceeds similarly to the approach in [CCG+ 25].
   7
     When we use CompleteSolution (H1 , . . . , Hp−1 , Hp′′ ), we are actually referring to CompleteSolution
(H′′ = (H1 , . . . , Hp−1 , Hp′′ ), DH′′ = (D0 , . . . , Dp−1 )). When we use CompleteSequence (Hp′′ ), we are actually
referring to CompleteSequence (H′′ = (H1 , . . . , Hp−1 ), Hp′′ , DH′′ = (D0 , . . . , Dp−1 )).

                                        Hp′ = hi1 , i2 , . . . , iq , iq+1 , h′x , . . . , hy′ i .

We have the following claim.

Claim 8.10. Throughout the updates to Hp′ , H1′ , . . . , Hp′ remain η-valid sequences, and Hp′
contains at most one free facility.

Proof. As we never update f or the value u(·) of a free facility in H1′ , . . . , Hp−1
                                                                                   ′   , these sequences
clearly remain η-valid.
    We continue to argue that Hp′ remains η-valid throughout its updates. By Claim 8.8, this
is true before any updates to Hp′ . We now analyze the three types of updates to Hp′ .

   • Updating Hp′ by inserting a free copy of iq+1 maintains that it is η-valid as every regular
     facility stays η-openable in the sequence (they have the same preﬁx as before) and the
     sequence remains maximal.

                                                                72
   • Updating Hp′ by removing h′t and calling CompleteSequence. Removing a h′t leaves
     the new preﬁx as a subsequence of the old preﬁx. Thus, each subsequent regular facility
     is still η-openable with respect to the original sequence padded with h′t (which acts as a
     valid super-sequence H′ ). Finally, the call to CompleteSequence adds facilities until
     no more can be opened, ensuring that the sequence is maximal.
   • Updating Hp′ by replacing ĩq+1 with its regular copy iq+1 . First note that, since u(ĩq+1 ) =
     0, this does not impact whether the following regular facilities are η-openable (with
     respect to a super-sequence), as the distances d2 (ĩq+1 , ·) and d2 (iq+1 , ·) are perfectly
     identical. By the same argument, the sequence stays maximal. Finally, to see that
     iq+1 is η-openable (with respect to a potential super-sequence), notice that the preﬁx
     i1 , . . . , iq is the same as that in Hp , which is already known to be an η-valid sequence.
    We complete the proof of the claim by arguing that Hp′ contains at most one free facility
at any point in time. This follows from the facts that (1) Hp′ initially contains no free facilities,
(2) in one repetition, the single free facility ĩq+1 is inserted into Hp′ , and (3) ĩq+1 is strictly
replaced by its regular copy iq+1 before continuing to the next repetition.

Outcome of this section of MergeSolutions.                                  We distinguish three cases.

Case 1. If at some point Hp′ is such that CompleteSolution(H1′ , H2′ , . . . , Hp′ ) opens ex-
actly k facilities, then we return that solution.

Case 2. Otherwise, suppose there is an update to Hp′ so that if we let Hpbefore and Hpafter de-
note Hp′ before and after this update, respectively, then CompleteSolution(H1 , H2 , . . . , Hpbefore )
and CompleteSolution(H1 , H2 , . . . , Hpafter ) sandwich k. There are three kinds of updates
to Hp′ . However, note that the update where ĩq+1 is replaced by its regular copy iq+1 cannot
satisfy the conditions of this case. Indeed, as u(ĩq+1 ) = 0, we have
        CompleteSolution(H1 , . . . , Hpbefore ) = CompleteSolution(H1 , . . . , Hpafter )
in this case, which contradicts that they sandwich k.
    We proceed to analyze the two other kinds of updates. If the update to Hp′ is to insert a
free facility ĩq+1 with u(ĩq+1 ) = 0, then
                                Hpbefore = hi1 , i2 , . . . , iq , h′q+1 , . . . , h′ℓ1 i
                                  Hpafter = hi1 , i2 , . . . , iq , h′q+1 , . . . , h′ℓ1 , ĩq+1 i

Note that the solution CompleteSolution(H1 , H2 , . . . , Hpbefore ) is equivalent to CompleteSolution(H1 , H2 , .
if we set u(ĩq+1 ) = 10M (except for the free facility ĩq+1 ). We can thus do binary search on
u(ĩq+1 ) to ﬁnd two values u′ (ĩq+1 ) and u(ĩq+1 ) with |u′ (ĩq+1 ) − u(ĩq+1 )| ≤ η so that the solu-
tions CompleteSolution(f,u′ ) (H1 , . . . , Hpafter ) and CompleteSolution(f,u) (H1 , . . . , Hpafter )
sandwich k (where u and u′ are identical except for ĩq+1 ). We can thus proceed to Subsec-
tion 8.3.4 of phase p, since they have the ﬁrst p, instead of p − 1, η-valid sequences in common
and exactly one diﬀerence parameter. Their maximality follows from that of Hpbefore .
     Finally, consider when the update to Hp′ is of the second type, i.e., u(ĩq+1 ) = 0 and

                 Hpbefore = hi1 , . . . , iq , h′t , h′t+1 . . . , h′ℓ1 , ĩq+1 , h′ℓ1 +1 , . . . , h′ℓ2 i
                  Hpafter = hi1 , . . . , iq , h′t+1 , . . . , h′ℓ1 , ĩq+1 , h′ℓ1 +1 , . . . , h′ℓ2 , . . . , h′ℓ3 i


                                                                73
In this case, CompleteSolution(H1 , . . . , Hpbefore ) and CompleteSolution(H1 , . . . , Hpafter )
are two solutions with the same parameters f, u, that sandwich k and we proceed to the next
step with the following properties of Hpbefore and Hpafter :

   • Both sequences contain one free facility, ĩq+1 .

   • Hpbefore and Hpafter are identical except that (1) Hpbefore contains a regular facility h′t not
     present in Hpafter , and (2) Hpafter contains a (potentially empty) suﬃx of regular facilities
     not present in Hpbefore .

Case 3. None of the previous cases apply. Then, completing the solution with the initial
Hp′ and the ﬁnal Hp′ cannot sandwich k. Since then (assuming the ﬁrst case does not apply)
there must be an update to Hp′ that results in two solutions that sandwich k, since the
completion of Hp and the completion of the initial Hp′ sandwich k. The above implies that if
we let Hpbefore = Hp and Hpafter equal the ﬁnal Hp′ , then CompleteSolution(H1 , . . . , Hpbefore )
and CompleteSolution(H1 , . . . , Hpafter ) sandwich k. Further note that since Hpafter equals
Hp′ after the ﬁnal updates, Hpbefore appears as a preﬁx of Hpafter and both sequences only
contain regular facilities. To summarize, in this case, CompleteSolution(H1 , . . . , Hpbefore )
and CompleteSolution(H1 , . . . , Hpafter ) are two solutions with the same parameters f, u,
that sandwich k and we proceed to the next step with the following properties of Hpbefore and
Hpafter :

   • Both sequences contain no free facilities.

   • Hpbefore and Hpafter are identical except that Hpafter contains a suﬃx of regular facilities
     not present in Hpbefore .

8.3.3   Removing Extra Facilities
This subsection proceeds almost identically to that of [CCG+ 25] (except that we will go to
the next subroutine instead of ﬁnishing phase p). As in Subsection 8.3.2, the parameters (f, u)
and D1 , . . . , Dp−1 are clear from the context, and we will omit them when using the deﬁnition
of η-openability and calling the function CompleteSolution.
    The input to this step consists of two solutions CompleteSolution(H1 , . . . , Hpbefore )
and CompleteSolution(H1 , . . . , Hpafter ) that have the same parameters f, u and sandwich
k. Moreover, Hpbefore and Hpafter both contain at most one free facility and they are identical
except for two potential diﬀerences:

   • Hpbefore may contain a facility not present in Hpafter ;

   • Hpafter contains a suﬃx of regular facilities (which may be empty) that is not present in
     Hpbefore .

    To simplify notation in this section, we let i∗ denote the regular facility in Hpbefore that is
not present in Hpafter if it exists (so i∗ corresponds to h′t in the previous section). We further
let h1 , . . . , hℓ denote the suﬃx of Hpafter not present in Hpbefore .
    We now transform Hpafter into Hpbefore one change at a time:




                                                74
  Generation of η-valid sequences from Hpafter to Hpbefore .

     • If Hpbefore contains the regular facility i∗ not present in Hpafter , then let ĩ∗ be a
       free copy of i∗ with u(ĩ∗ ) = 0 and obtain Hp0 from Hpafter by adding ĩ∗ at the end.
       Otherwise, we let Hp0 equal Hpafter .

     • For r = 1, . . . , ℓ, obtain Hpr from Hpr−1 by removing hr .

    We remark that Hpℓ is identical to Hpbefore since we removed the whole suﬃx h1 , . . . , hℓ
present in Hpafter , except for potentially the facility i∗ . That is, the only diﬀerence in Hpbefore
and Hpℓ may be the placement of ĩ∗ and that ĩ∗ is a free copy of the regular facility i∗ present
in Hpbefore . However, since u(ĩ∗ ) = 0, we have that the two sequences Hpbefore and Hpℓ have an
identical impact on CompleteSolution, which is a fact we use in the proof of Claim 8.12.
Claim 8.11. For r = 0, 1 . . . ℓ, Hpr is an η-valid sequence with at most two free facilities.

Proof. The sequence Hpr has at most two free facilities because Hpafter has at most one free
facility, and we potentially add one new free facility ĩ∗ .
    We now verify that Hpr is η-valid. For any regular facility h′ in the sequence, the preﬁx
of Hpr before h′ is a subsequence of the preﬁx before h′ in Hpafter . Thus, as h′ was openable
with respect to a super-sequence H(h′ ) in Hpafter , h′ must be η-openable with respect to the
same super-sequence H(h′ ) in Hpr . This ensures that the ﬁrst property of Deﬁnition 8.4 is
satisﬁed. To verify maximality, notice that since we added ĩ∗ with u(ĩ∗ ) = 0 at the end, we
have that the set of active clients after opening up the facilities in Hpr is a subset of the active
clients after opening up the facilities in Hpbefore . Hence, the maximality of Hpbefore implies the
maximality of Hpr .

Claim 8.12. One of the following is true.
   • For one of the sequences Hpr , CompleteSolution(H1 , H2 , . . . , Hpr ) opens k regular
     facilities.

   • CompleteSolution(H1 , H2 , . . . , Hpafter ) and CompleteSolution(H1 , H2 , . . . , Hp0 ) sand-
     wich k.

   • CompleteSolution(H1 , H2 , . . . , Hpr ) and CompleteSolution(H1 , H2 , . . . , Hpr+1 ) sand-
     wich k for an r ∈ {0, 1, . . . , ℓ − 1}.
Proof. Assume the ﬁrst bullet point does not hold, i.e., the completion of no Hpr leads to the
opening of k regular facilities. Now notice that the second and third bullet points ask whether
consecutive solutions sandwich k, starting with Hpafter and ending with Hpℓ . By deﬁnition, Hpℓ
equals Hpbefore except potentially for the placement of ĩ∗ and that ĩ∗ is the free copy of i∗
present in Hpbefore . However, as u(ĩ∗ ) = 0, the two sequences have an identical impact when
we complete the solution, which implies

        CompleteSolution(H1 , . . . , Hpℓ ) = CompleteSolution(H1 , . . . , Hpbefore ).

Thus, as CompleteSolution(H1 , . . . , Hpafter ) and CompleteSolution(H1 , . . . , Hpbefore ) sand-
wich k, either the second or third bullet point of the statement must be valid (since no solution
opened exactly k regular facilities).

                                                 75
Outcome of this section of MergeSolutions.               We distinguish three cases based on the
ﬁrst bullet point that is true in the above claim.

Case 1. If one of the sequences Hpr is such that CompleteSolution(H1 , H2 , . . . , Hpr ) opens
k regular facilities, then we return that solution.

Case 2. Otherwise, if the two solutions CompleteSolution(H1 , H2 , . . . , Hpafter ) and
CompleteSolution(H1 , H2 , . . . , Hp0 ) sandwich k, then Hp0 is equal to Hpafter except that ĩ∗
was added at the end of Hpafter with u(ĩ∗ ) = 0. Note that the solution CompleteSolution(H1 , H2 , . . . , Hpafter )
is equivalent to CompleteSolution(H1 , H2 , . . . , Hp0 ) if we set u(ĩ∗ ) = 10M (except for the
presence of ĩ∗ ). We can thus do a binary search on u(ĩ∗ ) to ﬁnd two values u′ (ĩ∗ ) and u(ĩ∗ )
with |u′ (ĩ∗ ) − u(ĩ∗ )| ≤ η so that the two solutions CompleteSolution(f,u′ ) (H1 , . . . , Hp0 ) and
CompleteSolution(f,u) (H1 , . . . , Hp0 ) sandwich k (where u and u′ are identical except for
ĩ∗ ). We can thus proceed to Subsection 8.3.4 of phase p, since they have the ﬁrst p, instead
of p − 1, η-valid sequences in common and exactly one diﬀerence parameter.

Case 3. Finally, consider the case when none of the above cases apply and (thus by the
above claim) there is an r ∈ {0, 1, . . . , ℓ − 1} so that CompleteSolution(H1 , H2 , . . . , Hpr )
and CompleteSolution(H1 , H2 , . . . , Hpr+1 ) sandwich k. The only diﬀerence between Hpr
and Hpr+1 is that Hpr contains hr+1 which is not present in Hpr+1 . Let Hp be the sequence
obtained by adding a free copy ĩr+1 of hr+1 at the end of Hpr+1 (which clearly maintains that
it is η-valid), then

CompleteSolution(H1 , H2 , . . . , Hp ) = CompleteSolution(H1 , H2 , . . . , Hpr ) if u(ĩr+1 ) = 0

and

CompleteSolution(H1 , H2 , . . . , Hp ) = CompleteSolution(H1 , H2 , . . . , Hpr+1 ) if u(ĩr+1 ) = 10M .

Hence, doing a binary search on u(ĩr+1 ) as in the previous case can proceed to Subsection 8.3.4
of phase p, since they have the ﬁrst p, instead of p−1, η-valid sequences in common and exactly
one diﬀerence parameter.

Claim 8.13. Consider time θ = (1 + ε3 )p−1 . For any r ∈ [m], D̃r is valid with respect to

Proof. We prove the claim for D̃r ; the proof for D̃r′ is symmetric. We proceed by induction
on r.
     For the base case when r = 0, because D̃0 = Dp , by the deﬁnition of CompleteCopies,
we have that D̃0 is perfectly valid with respect to θ, (H1 , . . . , Hp ), Dp−1 .
     Next, we consider the induction step when r > 0. Suppose D̃r−1 is valid with respect to
θ, (H1 , . . . , Hp ), Dp−1 . In the procedure, we update D̃r−1 to D̃r by changing a copy j from
type DC to type IC. To show that D̃r is valid with respect to θ, (H1 , . . . , Hp ), Dp−1 , we need
to show that its original α-value in Dp−1 satisﬁes the slack condition: ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, S) ≤ αj ≤
ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, S) + 4η.
     First, because j was IC or A in Dp−1 but became DC in Dp , it must have triggered
the DC conversion during RetrieveNewCopies against the newly opened facilities Hp .
Therefore, it strictly satisﬁed αj ≥ ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, Hp ). Since S = Sold ∪ Hp , we trivially have
d2 (j, S) ≤ d2 (j, Hp ), satisfying the lower bound αj ≥ ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, S).
     Second, suppose that j ′ is the corresponding copy of j in Dp−1        ′   (used in the above proce-
                                 ′            ′
dure), with dual value αj ′ . Since j, j are of type IC or A in Dp−1 , Dp−1         ′   respectively, and

u, u diﬀer in at most one parameter by at most η, Claim 8.9 gives |αj − αj′ ′ | ≤ η.
    To bound αj′ ′ , we look at its behavior under d′ . Because j ′ was IC or A in the valid
collection Dp−1   ′   , its value is exactly (1 − δ)d′2 (j, Sold ) or θ (with θ < (1 − δ)d′2 (j, Sold )). In
either case, since ρ > 1, it strictly satisﬁes αj′ ′ ≤ ρ(1 − δ)d′2 (j, Sold ). Furthermore, since it
remains of type IC or A in Dp′ , it did not trigger the RetrieveNewCopies conversion under
d′ against Hp , meaning αj′ ′ < ρ(1 − δ)d′2 (j, Hp ). Combining these bounds against Sold and
Hp , we establish its bound against the full set S = Sold ∪ Hp :
                  αj′ ′ ≤ ρ(1 − δ) min(d′2 (j, Sold ), d′2 (j, Hp )) = ρ(1 − δ)d′2 (j, S) .

Dp−1   respectively. Let j̃ and j̃ ′ be the unique copies of type IC or A for client j in Dp−1 and

Dp−1   respectively (if they exist).
    If one of them does not exist (meaning the client is fully DC in that historical state),
because we have the invariant |π̃(j) − π̃ ′ (j)| ≤ η/M , we must have π̃(j), π̃ ′ (j) ≥ 1 − η/M .
The walking procedure only acts to revert copies that were IC/A in the previous phase; it
does not convert historical DC copies. Thus, the historical DC mass is permanent, meaning
π(j) ≥ π̃(j) and π ′ (j) ≥ π̃ ′ (j). This mathematically restricts both ﬁnal probabilities to the
top of the interval [1 − η/M, 1], strictly ensuring |π(j) − π ′ (j)| ≤ η/M .
    Next, we consider the case when both j̃ and j̃ ′ exist. Because we run the bidirectional
walking procedures until no more possible updates can be made, it is impossible for j̃ and j̃ ′
to end up with divergent types (one DC and one IC/A). Therefore, we have only two possible
atomic outcomes for these speciﬁc copies:

   1. They both evaluate to type DC in D̃m and D̃m  ′ . Because j̃ and j̃ ′ hold the entirety
                                                      ′

      of the non-DC mass (exactly 1 − π̃(j) and 1 − π̃ ′ (j) respectively), converting them to
      DC forces the total DC probability to π̃(j) + (1 − π̃(j)) = 1. Thus, π(j) = π ′ (j) = 1,
      yielding a diﬀerence of 0.

   2. They both evaluate to type IC/A, meaning the procedure reverted any DC conversions
      from this phase. In this case, the DC probabilities remain exactly their historical values:
      π(j) = π̃(j) and π ′ (j) = π̃ ′ (j). By our inductive promise, their diﬀerence is bounded by
      η/M .

In all cases, we strictly maintain the invariant |π(j) − π ′ (j)| ≤ η/M .

has IC type with probability µ′ and the other copy j (2) has DC type with probability

because D̃r and D̃r+1 are valid collections of copies, any interpolation D̃sp (µ′ ) is a valid
collection of copies. Therefore, we can obtain a solution (Hsp (µ′ ), DHsp (µ′ )) by calling

by η/M , these two solutions satisfy condition (iv). We can then proceed to phase p + 1 of
MergeSolutions with these two solutions: they have the ﬁrst p η-valid sequences in com-
mon, utilize identical parameters (f, u), and the newly established parameter between them is
exactly the connection probability mass µ′ (which is not viewed as a diﬀerence parameter).

8.4    Analysis of the Robust Algorithm
In this subsection, we prove Theorem 8.7, restated as follows, showing that a solution (H =
(H1 , . . . , HL ), DH = (D0 , D1 , . . . , DL )) consisting of η-valid sequences and valid collections

                                                    79
of copies yields an almost LMP Γ-approximation. This subsection follows the same logic as
Subsection 5.4 from [CCG+ 26], the main diﬀerence is in establishing the constraints for the
Dual Payment, which are diﬀerent for this new algorithm.

Theorem 8.7. Consider a solution H, DH of η-valid sequences and valid collections of copies.
Let S be the opened facilities. Further, let Sfree and Sreg = S − Sfree be the free copies and
regular facilities, respectively. Then, we have
                    X                     √
                                   Γ + 201 ε + 20nη
                        d (j, S) ≤
                         2
                                                     · (optLP (f ) − |Sreg | · f ) .
                                         1−δ
                   j∈D

where

   • if d is a (general) metric, we choose ρ = 2.5, Γ = 4.9; or

   • if d is a Euclidean metric, we choose ρ = 2, Γ = 3 + ln(2).

    Our analysis closely follows that of LogAdaptiveAlgorithm in Section 7. However,
one key diﬀerence is that in the current setting, each client j ∈ D may have multiple copies
in its collection of copies Dj . Older setting in Section 7 can be seen as a special case where
each client has only one weighted copy. To close the gap between the two settings, for each
copy j̃ ∈ DL in the ﬁnal collection of copies, it induces a corresponding weighted client with
weight µ(j̃), which is equal to the probability mass of the copy j̃ in DL . For simplicity, in the
rest of this subsection, we will call these induced clients by the copies simply as “clients” and
call the clients in D as “original clients”. We will simply use j to denote such a client, and use
oc(j) to denote its corresponding “original client”. Also, we will use the terminology clients
and copies interchangeably later in the analysis.
    We can keep track of how each copy splits in each Dp for p = 1, . . . , L. In this way, for
each copy in DL , we can know which copy it belongs to in any Dp for each p = 1, . . . , L. For
simplicity, we will split each copy j in Dp into multiple copies j1 , . . . , jr in DL which all belong
to it. These j1 , . . . , jr will keep their own probability mass in Dp , but their connection types
(A, DC, IC) and α̃-values (if not of type DC) will be inherited from j in Dp . After processing
this splitting, we can view D1 , . . . , DL as the history of how each copy in DL evolves from
phase 1 to phase L. Further, the split process in the deﬁnition of “valid collection of copies”
(Deﬁnition 8.5) can be regarded as turning some copies in each Dp from DC to IC.
    One more issue to address is that in the collections of copies, we only maintain α-values for
active or indirectly connected copies. We may lose track of the α-values of directly connected
copies. To handle this issue, we deﬁne the α-value for each copy using the following procedure.
We remark that this procedure is only for analysis purposes. The sequence of sequences H
is enough for us to recover a solution. Hence, we do not need to pay attention to the time
complexity of this procedure.
    For RecoverAlpha, we have the following remarks:

   • In the deﬁnition of the process, we will view free facilities and regular facilities equiva-
     lently (especially when we run RetrieveNewCopies), but in the later analysis of dual
     payment and Theorem 8.7, we still distinguish between them.

   • The steps I) and II) in Stage 1 are well-deﬁned since Hp is η-valid with respect to
     θ, (H1 , . . . , Hp−1 ), Dp−1 (Deﬁnition 8.4).



                                                  80
Algorithm 3 (RecoverAlpha).
 Initialization: Set θ = 1, S = ∅, A = DL , DC = ∅, IC = ∅, and αj = θ, Rj = 0 for
every j ∈ DL .

For each p ∈ {1, . . . , L}:

   • Let θ = (1 + ε3 )p−1 .

   • Stage 1. For each facility ht in the sequence Hp , perform the following steps:

         I) If ht is free, add ht to S and jump to step IV). Otherwise, ﬁnd a super-
            sequence H′ ⊇ hH1 , . . . , Hp−1 i ⊕ ({h1 , . . . , ht−1 }) such that ht is η-openable
            with respect to H′ . Let A′ be the set of active clients induced by Dnew =
            RetrieveNewCopies(Dp−1 , H′ − hH1 , . . . , Hp−1 i).
        II) Compute (τj )j∈A′ so that ht is η-openable with (τj )j∈A′ .
                                                               √
       III) Add ht to S. Set αj ← τj for every j ∈ A′ ∩ B(ht , εθ).
       IV) For every j ∈ A ∪ IC:
              – If αj ≥ ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, ht ), move j to DC, and set Rj as follows:
                                                                                   p
                  ∗ if ht is regular and j is directly connected in Dnew , set Rj = αj /ρ;
                                           √
                  ∗ otherwise, set Rj = 1 − δ · d(j, ht ).
              – Otherwise, if j ∈ A and αj ≥ (1 − δ)d2 (j, ht ), move j to IC.

   • Stage 2. Perform the following step and move to the next phase:

         i) For every j ∈ IC, set αj ← (1 − δ)d2 (j, S).
        ii) For every j ∈ A, set αj ← min{(1 + ε3 )θ, (1 − δ)d2 (j, S)}, and move it to IC
            when αj = (1 − δ)d2 (j, S).
       iii) For every j ∈ DC, if j’s type in Dp is indirectly connected, move j to IC and
            set αj ← (1 − δ)d2 (j, S), and Rj ← 0.
        iv) Update θ ← (1 + ε3 )θ.



• In Step IV) in Stage 1, we have two diﬀerent ways to deﬁne Rj variables for newly
  directly connected clients. The reason we need diﬀerent deﬁnitions is that ht is only
  openable with respect to a super-sequence. If a client j is viewed as directly connected
  w.r.t. the super-sequence, we deﬁne Rj maximally, by enforcing Rj2 = αj /ρ, because the
  (ρ − 1)αj /ρ part of αj will no longer be used for opening other
                                                              √    facilities (see, the proof
  Lemma 8.15). Otherwise, we deﬁne Rj as usual, by Rj = 1 − δd(j, ht ). This is a new
  feature for our analysis, compared with [CCG+ 26].

• We can show that RecoverAlpha produces the same solution as (H, DH) by com-
  paring RecoverAlpha with RetrieveNewCopies, CompleteCopies and the deﬁ-
  nition of valid collection of copies (Deﬁnition 8.5):

       – In Stage 1, step III) of RecoverAlpha opens facilities in Hp one by one; and
         then step IV) of RecoverAlpha updates the α-values and connection types of all


                                               81
           clients in exactly the same way as RetrieveNewCopies.
         – In Stage 2, steps i) and ii) of RecoverAlpha update the α-values and connection
           types of all clients in exactly the same way as the second step of CompleteCopies.
           Step iii) of RecoverAlpha moves some directly connected clients to indirectly
           connected, which is exactly the same as the split process in Deﬁnition 8.5 under
           our viewpoint. We shall remark that all copies moved from DC to IC are those in
           IC ∪ A in Dp−1 because of our algorithm in Subsection 8.3.4.

    Throughout the analysis, we let αj∗ be the ﬁnal α-value of client j ∈ DL . With this
RecoverAlpha procedure, we can prove two key lemmas, Lemmas 8.15 and 8.16, that are
analogs of Lemmas 7.3 and 7.4, respectively. The proofs of them are almost identical to that
for LogAdaptiveAlgorithm. There are only three minor changes:

  1. Deﬁnition 8.2 of η-openability allows for a facility to be paid up to an 8nη-additive
     diﬀerence and ensures every facility not to be overbid up to an 8nη-additive diﬀerence.
     The ﬁrst diﬀerence only appears in Lemma 8.15 below, while the second diﬀerence only
     appears in Lemma 8.16. These diﬀerences reﬂect in step I) and II) of stage 1.

  2. Deﬁnition 8.4 of an η-valid sequence allows ht is η-openable with respect to a super
     sequence H′ ⊇ H ⊕({h1 , . . . , ht−1 }). This diﬀerence appears in the proof of Lemma 8.15,
     and (the last part of) the proof of Lemma 8.16, an analog of Lemma 7.4. This diﬀerence
     reﬂects in step I), II) and IV) of stage 1. In particular, a signiﬁcant diﬀerence is in the
     proof of rjt ≤ Rj (Lemma 8.22).

  3. Deﬁnition 8.5 of valid collections of copies allows for some directly connected copies
     to be moved to indirectly connected in Stage 2 of each phase. This diﬀerence only
     appears in (the last part of) the proof of Lemma 8.15 and the (ﬁrst part of) the proof
     of Lemma 8.16. This diﬀerence reﬂects in step iii) of stage 2.

The combination of these two lemmas will imply Theorem 8.7.

Proof. First, let us show that it suﬃces to prove that at the end of RecoverAlpha, we have

Indeed, at the end of RecoverAlpha, A = ∅. For every j ∈ IC, we have αj = (1 − δ)d2 (j, S)
by the construction of the update steps in Stage 2. Because Rj = 0 for j ∈ IC, their
contribution naturally satisﬁes the required bound. Therefore, Equation (43) implies the
lemma.
   To this end, we prove by induction on p that after the p-th phase in RecoverAlpha,
Equation (43) holds. The base case is p = 0, i.e., at the beginning of the RecoverAlpha,
where DC, S = ∅. Next, we show that this invariant is preserved after each phase of the
algorithm.
   The ﬁrst
          P case is that we only open free facilities in Stage 1 of the p-th phase. In this case,
the term i∈Sreg (fˆ − 12nη) does not change. Suppose DC ′ are the clients that are moved
from IC ∪ A to DC after stage 2 of this phase. In an earlier remark, we have argued that
there is no copy moved from DC to IC ∪ A. Consider any j ∈ DC ′ . Due to step IV) of Stage

                                                    84
1, we have αj ≥ ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, h), where h ∈ S is the ﬁrst open√facility satisfying the inequality.
Furthermore, for this newly directly connected client, Rj = 1 − δd(j, h). As established in
Section 7 (see the proof of Lemma 7.3), we have (1−δ)d2 (j, S)+(ρ−1)Rj2 ≤ ρ(1−δ)d2 (j, h) ≤
αj . Thus, j’s contribution to the LHS of Equation (43) is no less than that to the RHS. For
other clients in DC \ DC ′ , their contributions to the LHS do not change while the RHS may
only decrease because S only grows. Therefore, the inequality still holds.
     The second case is that we open at least one regular facility i in Stage 1 of the p-th phase.
The rest of the proof will focus on this case. Let us consider each step of stage 1, where
we open a facility i, i.e., adding it to S. Suppose that (α, S, A, IC, DC, θ) denotes the state
right before opening i. Suppose that (S ′ , A′ , IC ′ , DC ′ ) denote the set of open facility in the
super-sequence H′ considered in the step i is opened, the set of active clients with respect to
Dnew (as in Step I) of the process, deﬁned in terms of H′ ), the set of indirectly connected
clients with respect to Dnew and the set of directly connected clients with respect to Dnew . Let
(τj )j∈A′ be the τ -variables when opening i. Because H′ is a super sequence of open facilities,
we have S ′ ⊇ S, DC ′ ⊇ DC and A′ ⊆ A.
     According to the third bullet of η-openability (Deﬁnition 8.2), we have

    Next, we have a case-by-case discussion on the client’s contribution to both sides of Equa-
tion (43) for each j ∈ DL . Our goal is to show that the contribution to the LHS minus the
contribution to the RHS is at least w(j) · bid(j). Because of the third bullet of η-openability,
we have the total contribution to the LHS is greater than that to the RHS by an additive
factor of 4nη. Throughout the discussion, for simplicity, we can ignore the w(j) coeﬃcient,
because we have such a coeﬃcient on their contributions to both sides.
   • If j ∈ A′ , this means j ∈ A.

         – If j is not DC after opening i, the contributions to both sides are 0 and we have
           τj < ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, i), and thus bid(j) = [τj − ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, i)]+ = 0.
         – If j is DC after opening i, the contributions to√both sides are τj and (1−δ)d2 (j, i)+
           (ρ − 1)Rj2 . Because j ∈/ DC ′ , we have Rj = 1 − δd(j, i) and thus the diﬀerence
           is τj − ρ(1 − δ)d (j, i). Because j becomes DC after opening i, this quantity is
                             2

           greater than 0. We have the diﬀerence equal to bid(j).

   • If j ∈ IC ′ , this means j ∈ A ∪ IC.

         – If j is not DC after opening i, the contributions to both sides are 0 and we have

         – If j is DC after opening i, the contributions to√both sides are αj and (1−δ)d2 (j, i)+
           (ρ − 1)Rj2 . Because j ∈/ DC ′ , we have Rj = 1 − δd(j, i) and thus the diﬀerence

                             2

           greater than 0. We have the diﬀerence equal to bid(j).

                                                  85
  • If j ∈ DC, this means j ∈ DC ′ . Then, the contribution of it to the LHS is 0. Its
    contribution to the RHS is (1 − δ) · (d2 (j, S ∪ {i}) − d2 (j, S)) because Rj is not changed.
    Further, we have d2 (j, S ∪ {i}) − d2 (j, S) = −[d2 (j, S) − d2 (j, i)]+ ≤ −bid(j)/(1 − δ)
    because S ⊆ S ′ and d2 (j, S) ≥ d2 (j, S ′ ). Therefore, the diﬀerence is no less than bid(j).
  • If j ∈ DC ′ \ DC, we discuss two cases.
        – If j is not moved to DC after opening i, the contributions to both sides are 0. Note
          that αj ≥ ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, S ′ ) because j ∈ DC ′ . However, as αj < ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, S ∪
          {i}) ≤ ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, i), we have d2 (j, S ′ ) < d2 (j, i). This implies that bid(j) = 0.
          Therefore, the diﬀerence equals to bid(j).
                                                                  p
        – If j is moved to DC after opening i, we set Rj = αj /ρ at this time. Its contribu-
          tion to the LHS is αj . Its contribution to the RHS is (1 − δ)d2 (j, S ∪ {i}) + ρ−1 ρ αj .
          The diﬀerence is then αj /ρ − (1 − δ)d2 (j, S ∪ {i}). Note that because j ∈        / DC
          but is directly connected after opening i, d2 (j, S ∪ {i}) = d2 (j, i). The diﬀerence
          further equals to αj /ρ − (1 − δ)d2 (j, i), which is greater than 0. Note that α/ρ ≥
          (1−δ)d2 (j, S ′ ) because j ∈ DC ′ , we have the diﬀerence ≥ (1−δ)(d2 (j, S ′ )−d2 (j, i)).
          Combining these two lower bounds on the diﬀerence, we conclude that the diﬀer-
          ence is lower bounded by bid(j).
    Then, in stage 2, we only move some clients from DC to IC (step iii)). For each such
client j, its contribution to the LHS of Equation (43) drops by αj while its contribution
to the RHS drops by (1 − δ)d2 (j, S) + (ρ − 1)Rj2 . Note that our deﬁnition ensures that
Rj2 ≥ α/ρ ≥ (1 − δ)d2 (j, S) or Rj2 = (1 − δ)d2 (j, i) ≥ (1 − δ)d2 (j, S) for some i ∈ S. According
to Deﬁnition 8.5, for each such client j moved from DC to IC, we are guaranteed that αj <
ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, S) + 4η ≤ (1 − δ)d2 (j, S) + (ρ − 1)Rj2 + 4η. Therefore, the decrease of the LHS
is at most the decrease of the RHS
                                P plus an additive 4η per client. Over all such clients, the
total extra drop is at most 4η w(j) = 4nη. Since we accumulated at least 4nη surplus in
stage 1, the overall invariant Equation (43) still safely holds. This completes the induction
and thus the proof.

   The following claim is an analog of Claim 7.5.
Claim 8.17 (No Over-Bidding). At any point in the algorithm, for every facility i0 ∈ F , we

       X                                              X
           w(j) · [(1 − δ)d2 (j, S) − d2 (j, i0 )]+ +   w(j) · [αj − ρd2 (j, i0 )]+ ≤ fˆ.

                                                86
Proof. Stage 1: Consider an arbitrary facility i0 ∈ F. Suppose that a facility i′ is opened
with dual increases (τj )j∈A . The opening of i′ has three potential√eﬀects on the clients: (1) it
may strictly increase the α-values of active clients j ∈ A ∩ B(i′ , εθ) to τj and subsequently
make them directly connected (DC), (2) it may make some existing active or indirectly
connected (IC) clients directly connected to i′ , and (3) it may make some active clients
indirectly connected.
    Let us analyze how the left-hand side of our constraint is impacted by these changes. First,
observe that a client j that was already directly connected before the opening of i′ can only
lower its contribution, since the distance d2 (j, S) can only decrease when i′ is added to S.
    For a client j that was active or indirectly connected and now becomes a direct connect,
its contribution changes fromw(j) · [αj − ρd2 (j, i0 )]+ to w(j) · [(1 − δ)d2 (j, S ∪ {i′ }) − d2 (j, i0 )]+ .
    If j is active and τj > αj = θ, this means that j is directly connected to facility i′ , which
has just been opened in the step, such that d2 (j, i′ ) ≤ εθ. This implies j will be directly
connected to i′ and (1 − δ)ρd2 (j, S ∪ {i′ }) = (1 − δ)ρd2 (j, i′ ) ≤ αj because ε is suﬃciently
small. Hence, we have

[(1 − δ)d2 (j, S ∪ {i′ }) − d2 (j, i0 )]+ ≤ [(1 − δ)ρd2 (j, S ∪ {i′ }) − ρd2 (j, i0 )]+ ≤ [αj − ρd2 (j, i0 )]+ .

    Next, we consider the case j is active or indirectly connected but τj = αj . Because the
client became directly connected, its dual value must have dominated the connection cost,
meaningαj ≥ ρ(1 − δ)d2 (j, i′ ). Since ρ > 1 and [x]+ ≥ 0, we have:
