    With the above discussions, we prove that the invariant holds throughout Stage 1.
    Stage 2: In the second stage, we have three steps: decreasing the α-values of clients in
IC to (1 − δ)d2 (j, S); increasing the α-values of clients in A to min{(1 + ε3 )θ, (1 − δ)d2 (j, S)};
and moving some clients from DC to IC.
    The ﬁrst step can only decrease the left-hand side of the claim. The third step changes
the contribution of a moved client from [(1 − δ)d2 (j, S) − d2 (j, i0 )]+ to [αj − ρd2 (j, i0 )]+ =
[(1 − δ)d2 (j, S) − ρd2 (j, i0 )]+ . Since ρ > 1, this also strictly decreases its contribution. Hence,
it suﬃces to show that the continuous increase in the second step does not violate the claim.
    Assume towards contradiction that at the end of the phase, if we increase αj of every j ∈ A

      The modiﬁcations

Establishing the constraints. Up to this point, our arguments closely mirror the dual
feasibility proof for the log-adaptive algorithm in Section 7. However, the remaining part of
the proof needs some changes from Section 7, due to the fact that a facility may be η-openable
with respect to a super-sequence instead of the sequence that the algorithm actually executes.
     Let i0 be the facility that freezes i, which opens in (stage 1 of) phase p. Let θ = (1 + ε3 )p−1
be the active dual parameter at the beginning of phase p. Let H be the sequence of opened
facilities up to i0 , and let H′ ⊇ H be the super-sequence with respect to which i0 is η-openable.
     As in Section 7, we order the clients in D∗ according to the order they are removed from A
(in the algorithm), breaking ties in the increasing order of their αc -values. The only additional
rule is for the clients who are in A right before i0 is opened in H; let us call this set of active
clients A. By Claim 8.20, they are the last several clients in the above order of D∗ . Let
AF ⊆ A be the clients in D∗ who are still active right before i0 is opened according to the
collection of copies induced by RetrieveNewCopies(Dp−1 , Hp′ ). Let A′ = (D∗ ∩ A) \ AF be
the clients that become inactive during the extra preﬁx in H′ . In our ordering for D∗ ∩ A,
we place A′ ﬁrst and AF after, sorting within each group in the increasing order of their αjc
values.
     Let a be the index of the last client in D∗ \ AF , meaning the clients in [a + 1 : s] are exactly
A . For each client t ∈ [a], we deﬁne S t , DC t , and IC t to be the sets of opened facilities,
   F

directly connected clients in [t − 1], and indirectly connected (or active) clients in [t − 1],
evaluated at the beginning of the exact stage t becomes inactive. For each client t ∈ [a + 1 : s],
we deﬁne S t , DC t , and IC t to be the sets of opened facilities, directly connected clients in
[t − 1], and indirectly connected (or active) clients in [t − 1], evaluated right before t becomes
inactive in H′ . Similarly, let S F , IC F , and DC F be the set of opened facilities, indirectly
connected, and directly connected clients right before i0 is opened evaluated with respect to
the super-sequence H′ .


                                                 89
    We remark that the deﬁnitions of S F , IC F , DC F , and AF here depend on the super-
sequence H′ , while in Section 7, they only depend on the sequence H executed by the algorithm.
To complete the proof, we will now apply the uniﬁed constraint lemmas (Lemmas 4.11, 4.12
and 5.20) evaluated over this super-sequence state.

directly connected” upon “client j becomes active”.

Claim 8.21. For any t ∈ [s],

Proof. Consider the point in the execution evaluated against the super-sequence H′ at which
t becomes inactive. As established by Claim 8.20, if a facility i0 freezes i, every client j ∈ D∗
that is active right before i0 is opened becomes inactive during the exact step i0 is opened.
Therefore, no client in D∗ can become inactive after i0 is opened in H′ . This partitions our
ordered clients t ∈ D∗ into exactly two cases:

Case 1: t ∈ [a]. Client t becomes inactive strictly before i0 is opened in H′ . Because
every facility in the valid super-sequence H′ is η-openable, the No Over-Bidding invariant
(Claim 8.17) rigorously holds at every sequential step of its execution. We evaluate this
depending on the stage in which t becomes inactive:
    Stage 1: Suppose t becomes inactive due to the discrete opening of a facility hk ∈ H′ .
Because hk precedes i0 (the facility that freezes i), this event happens strictly before i is
frozen. Therefore, hk does not freeze i. By our deﬁnition of αc , no client in D∗ increases its
αc -value, meaning the modiﬁed dual αc does not incorporate any Step 1b increases from hk .



                                                90
Thus, αtc is exactly the active dual parameter θ right before hk is opened. We evaluate the
No Over-Bidding constraint at the state right before hk opens:
      X                                               X
             w(j)[(1 − δ)d2 (j, S t ) − d2 (j, i)]+ +   w(j)[αj − ρd2 (j, i)]+ ≤ fˆ + 8nη.
         j∈DC t                                     j∈IC t ∪At

For j ∈ DC t , the term trivially bounds (1 − δ)d2 (j, S t ) − d2 (j, i). For j ∈ IC t , the modiﬁed
dual αc never exceeds the true dual prior to a freezing step, so αj ≥ αjc . For j ≥ t, because
our ordering places clients by the time they become inactive, they are still active in At at this
moment, meaning their actual dual is αj = θ = αtc . Thus, [αj − ρd2 (j, i)]+ ≥ αtc − ρd2 (j, i).
    Stage 2: Suppose t becomes inactive due to the continuous update of α-values in Stage
2. We evaluate the No Over-Bidding constraint at the exact moment t hits its threshold. At
this moment, the modiﬁed dual exactly tracks the true dual, so αtc = αt . For j ∈ IC t , we
again have αj ≥ αjc . For j ≥ t, these clients either remain active or become inactive at the
same moment but are sorted after t (meaning αjc ≥ αtc ). In both cases, their true dual at this
moment satisﬁes αj ≥ αt = αtc . Thus, [αj − ρd2 (j, i)]+ ≥ αtc − ρd2 (j, i).
    In both Stage 1 and Stage 2, the subsets DC t , IC t , and {j ∈ D∗ : j ≥ t} are mutually
disjoint. Restricting the summations to these subsets and dropping the non-negative [·]+
operators rigorously establishes the algebraic lower bound:

j∈DC t                                  j∈IC t                      j≥t

General metric
Now, we will show Lemma 8.16 for ρ = 2.5 and Γ = 4.9. To this end, we show that

side of the capacity constraint replaced by fˆ + 8nη).
    The proof of this is strictly identical to the one in Section 7, with only two minor adap-
tations due to the super-sequence: First, the last constraint (the capacity bound) is now
directly provided by Claim 8.21 with the updated right-hand side fˆ + 8nη. Second, we will
use Lemma 8.22 to argue that rjt ≤ Rj still holds.
    The remaining bounds, including αtc ≤ (rjt + d(j, t))2 , follow exactly the same triangle
inequality logic as in the log-adaptive part because αtc ≤ (1 − δ)d2 (t, S t ) remains valid for all

t (for t ∈ [a + 1 : s], we have αtc ≤ τt ≤ (1 − δ)d2 (t, S F ) = (1 − δ)d2 (t, S t ) by the second bullet
of η-openability).

Euclidean metric
Now, we will show Lemma 8.16 for ρ = 2, Γ = 3 + ln 2, and d a Euclidean metric. Let a be the
index of the last client that becomes inactive strictly before i0 is opened in the super-sequence
H′ .
     The following claim is the analog of Claim 7.10.
Claim 8.23. For each t ∈ [a], j ∈ DC t and ℓ ∈ IC t ∪ {t}, we have:
                                         αℓc ≤ (rjt + d(j, ℓ))2 .
Proof. We ﬁrst prove the bound for t ∈ [a]. Consider the exact state in the execution evaluated
at the beginning of the stage where t becomes inactive. By deﬁnition, S t is the set of open
facilities at this state.
    Consider any client ℓ ∈ IC t ∪ {t}. As ℓ ≤ t, ℓ becomes inactive at this state or earlier. We
prove that αℓc ≤ (1 − δ)d2 (ℓ, S t ) by discussing two cases:
   • If ℓ became inactive strictly before the phase containing t’s inactivation, then ℓ is in-
     directly connected at the beginning of this phase. Because of the Stage 2 continuous
     updates at the end of the previous phase, we have αℓc ≤ αℓ = (1−δ)d2 (ℓ, Sprev ). Because
     the set of open facilities only grows, Sprev ⊆ S t , meaning the distance can only decrease.
     Thus, αℓc ≤ (1 − δ)d2 (ℓ, S t ).
   • Otherwise, ℓ becomes inactive during the same phase as t (this includes the case ℓ = t).
     Let θt be the active dual parameter at the beginning of this phase. Since ℓ is active at
     this time, αℓc ≤ αℓ = θt . We must strictly have θt ≤ (1 − δ)d2 (ℓ, S t ), because if θt > (1 −
     δ)d2 (ℓ, S t ), ℓ would have been moved to IC during the Stage 2 continuous update of the
     previous phase (when the threshold exceeded the connection cost), contradicting that ℓ
     is still active at the beginning of the current phase. Therefore, αℓc ≤ θt ≤ (1 − δ)d2 (ℓ, S t ).
    We have established that αℓc ≤ (1 − δ)d2 (ℓ, S t ). Because the triangle inequality holds for
the Euclidean metric d, we strictly have d(ℓ, S t ) ≤ d(j, S t ) + d(j, ℓ). Taking the square root
of our established bound, we get:
                              p c √
                                 αℓ ≤ 1 − δd(ℓ, S t )
                                      √
                                    ≤ 1 − δ(d(j, S t ) + d(j, ℓ))
                                             √
                                    = rj,t + 1 − δd(j, ℓ).
                           √
    Since δ > 0, we have 1 − δ < 1. Because metric distances are strictly non-negative,
replacing the scaled distance with the full distance preserves the inequality:
                                     p c
                                       αℓ ≤ rj,t + d(j, ℓ).
    Squaring both sides yields αℓc ≤ (rj,t + d(j, ℓ))2 , completing the proof.
    We will make several cases according to how i gets frozen.

9     (4 + O( ε))-Approximation for ( logζ n )-Stable Instances
                                    √
In this section, we give a (4 + O( ε))-approximation algorithm for ( logζ n )-stable metric k-
means instances, hence proving Theorem 1.4. We will reuse most of the notation and proofs
in [CCG+ 25], and only mention the main diﬀerences.
    To simplify notation, we let ε be the minimum of ε and ζ in the above theorem and further
                                                                                         √
assume that ε < 1/12 is suﬃciently small. With this notation, we present a (4 + O( ε)-
approximation algorithm for ε/ log(n)-stable instances. This implies the above theorem as
any ζ/ log(n)-stable instance is also ζ ′ / log(n)-stable with ζ ′ ≤ ζ.
    The algorithm and the proof of the above result follow several steps where we iteratively
simplify the instance by adding more structure. Finally, we obtain enough structure to solve
the problem by maximizing a submodular function subject to a partition matroid constraint.
We have not optimized constants in favor of simplifying the description, and throughout this
section we let Oε (log n) denote O(log(n)/εO(1) ).

Deﬁnitions of leaders. We use the following     P          deﬁnitions throughout this section. Given a
set of centers A ⊆ F , we let cost(A) := p∈D d (p, A) denote the cost of the corresponding
                                                          2

clustering (where each client p is assigned to the closest center in A and pays the corresponding
distance). Sometimes we will consider aP     (possibly suboptimal) assignment µ : D → A of clients
to centers in A, and let cost(A, µ) := p∈D d2 (p, µ(p)) be the corresponding cost. Let OPT
be a ﬁxed optimum solution for the k-means instance, and let opt = cost(OPT) be its cost.
For a client p ∈ D, let OPTp := d2 (p, OPT) be the cost paid by p in OPT.
     Let C ∗ be an OPT cluster with center c∗ ∈ OPT. We let avgC ∗ ,OPT be the squared
distance from c∗ to the ε|C ∗ |th closest client of C ∗ to c∗ . We further let Cavg      ∗  be the set of
             ∗                                                           ∗
clients of C at a squared distance at most avgC ∗ ,OPT from c , we refer to these clients as
leaders for C ∗ . So avgC ∗ ,OPT is the maximum squared distance from a leader to its center c∗ .
Also note that we have, |Cavg  ∗ | ≥ ε|C ∗ |. We thus have that avg
                                                                           C ∗ ,OPT is upper bounded by
  1                             1 P          2 (p, c∗ ), and at the same time C ∗ contains a constant
 1−ε times the  average  cost |C ∗ | p∈C ∗ d                                       avg
fraction of the clients.

Partition matroids. In Subsection 9.10, we formulate a submodular function that we then
maximize over a partition matroid using known results. We deﬁne those concepts and also
state the result that we will use. First, recall the deﬁnition of a matroid and partition matroid.
A matroid is a tuple (E, I) deﬁned on a ground set E with a family of independent sets I
that satisfy: (1) if A ∈ I and A′ ⊆ A, then A′ ∈ I; and (2) if A, B ∈ I and |A| < |B|, then
there exists an element e ∈ B − A such that A ∪ {e} ∈ I. In the special case of a partition
matroid, the ground set E is partitioned into disjoint subsets E1 , E2 , . . . , Ek , and there are
non-negative integers r1 , r2 , . . . , rk such that:

    • A subset I ⊆ E is independent if and only if |I ∩ Ei | ≤ ri for each i = 1, 2, . . . , k.

In other words, each subset Ei has a capacity limit ri , and an independent set I cannot
contain more than ri elements from Ei . To work with matroids (to get a running time that is
polynomial in the size of the ground set E), we often use independence queries, which allow
us to determine if a given subset A ⊆ E is independent (i.e., whether A ∈ I). Speciﬁcally, an
independence query on a subset A returns true if A is independent and false otherwise.




                                                   98
Submodular functions. Having deﬁned matroids, we proceed to deﬁne non-negative mono-
tone submodular functions. For a ﬁnite ground set E, a set function f : 2E → R is submodular
if it satisﬁes the diminishing returns property: for every pair of sets A ⊆ B ⊆ E and any
element e ∈ E − B, it holds that

                            f (A ∪ {e}) − f (A) ≥ f (B ∪ {e}) − f (B).

Intuitively, this means that adding an element e to a smaller set A provides at least as much
additional value as adding e to a larger set B. In addition, f is called monotone if for any
pair of sets A ⊆ B ⊆ E, we have f (A) ≤ f (B). In other words, adding elements to a set does
not decrease the function value. Finally, f is non-negative if f (A) ≥ 0 for all A ⊆ E.
    A celebrated result [CCPV11] gives a polynomial-time (1 − 1/e)-approximation algorithm
for the problem of maximizing a non-negative monotone submodular function over a ma-
troid constraint. While the original result was a randomized algorithm, a recent striking
result [BF24], building upon the work of [FW14], gives a deterministic algorithm with the
same guarantee. We remark that polynomial time here refers to a running time that is poly-
nomial in the size of the ground set E. To summarize, they show the following theorem.


Theorem 9.1. Let f : 2E → R be a non-negative monotone submodular function that we can
evaluate in polynomial time, and let (E, I) be a matroid on the same groundset, for which
we can answer independence queries in polynomial time. Then, for every ζ > 0, there is a
polynomial-time algorithm that outputs a set X ⊆ E with X ∈ I so that

                               f (X) ≥ (1 − 1/e − ζ) · max
                                                        ∗
                                                           f (X ∗ ) .
                                                       X ∈I




9.1    Properties of a Locally Optimal Solution
Let S be a locally optimal solution output by the following standard local search algorithm.
We remark that its running time is polynomial since by Lemma 3.2 the cost of a solution is a
polynomially bounded integer and, at each improving step, the cost decreases by at least one.

  Classic Local Search Procedure for k-Means.

      1. S ← Arbitrary solution for k-Means.

      2. While there exists a solution S ′ such that |S∆S ′ | ≤ 2 and cost(S ′ ) < cost(S):

          (a) S ← S ′ .

      3. Output S.

Local search achieves a 26 approximation for k-Means, see [GT08].

Lemma 9.2. cost(S) ≤ 26 · opt.

    In the remaining part of the algorithm and its analysis we ﬁx S, and recall that we ﬁxed
the reference optimal solution OPT. We distinguish diﬀerent types of clusters of OPT and
analyze their properties in the next two sections. We let Sp := d2 (p, S) be the cost paid by p
in S and recall that OPTp denotes the cost p ∈ D pays in OPT.

                                                99
9.2    Pure Clusters
We say that a cluster C ∗ of OPT is pure (w.r.t. S) if there exists a cluster C ′ of S such that
|C ′ ∆C ∗ | ≤ 3ε min(|C ∗ |, |C ′ |). In which case we also say that C ′ is pure w.r.t. OPT. We say
that C ′ and C ∗ are associated. The following lemma follows from the fact that the input is
β-stable for β := ε/ log n and that the solution S is a 26-approximation by Lemma 9.2. Given
a client or center a and a distance r, we let B(a, r) be the set of clients and centers at distance
at most r from a.

In the previous section, we showed that the cost of pure clusters in OPT is close to their
optimal cost in our local search solution S. Our goal in this section is to “hit” those non-pure

center c∗ is basic-cheap if the total cost in S of the clients in Cavg

procedure to sample clients proportional to their cost in solution S.

9.6    Removal of Expensive Centers of S0
Thus, it remains to show that our algorithm can yield a good approximation to the gain that
the swap (OPT,ˆ S0 ) would provide. Of course, it will not necessarily be optimum, but we
can show it is enough to conclude the proof of our theorem. We focus on the iteration of the
algorithm that considers a valid ball set of balls B. In particular, at this point given that W
is successful and B valid, the number of balls in our ball guess is equal to |S0 | = |OPT|ˆ   =
       log n
|Λ| ≤ ε3 . Our algorithm then makes use of the following Lemma in [CCG 25] that takes
                                                                                 +

as input the local search solution S and the set Λ of dummy centers.
                                                                               O(1)
Lemma 9.11 ([CCG+ 25]). There is a procedure that runs in n1/ε -time and produces a
                                O(1)
collection Lexp of at most n1/ε      subsets of S such that with probability at least 1 − 1/n there
exists Q ∈ Lexp satisfying

   1. Q ⊆ S0 , and

   2. The total cost in the solution S − Q ∪ Λ of the clusters with centers in S0 − Q is at most
      ε · opt.




                                                     103
9.7    Consistently Assigning Clients in Mixed Solutions and the sets U and
       R
While our algorithm tries all possible Q ∈ Lexp , we focus on the execution path of a set Q of
centers satisfying the properties of Lemma 9.11, i.e.,

   1. Q ⊆ S0 , and

   2. the cost in the solution S − Q ∪ Λ of the clusters with centers in S0 − Q is at most ε · opt.

We deﬁne the solution SQ := S − Q ∪ Λ and for a center c ∈ SQ we let SQ (c) be the subset of
clients closest to c (i.e., assigned to c) in the solution SQ . The second property above allows
us to consider each cluster SQ (c), c ∈ S0 − Q, as contracted by paying a small extra cost of
ε · opt. In particular, this allows us to give a “consistent” assignment µO of clients in the
mixed solutions. Here, consistent means that all clients in SQ (c), for c ∈ S0 − Q, are assigned
to the same center in the mixed solutions where all of S0 is removed.

Deﬁnition of µO and µD . In the following we slightly deviate from the approach in
[CCG+ 25]. More speciﬁcally, the constant 4 below is a 5 in [CCG+ 25]. The rest of the
analysis remains however very similar. We modify how he clients are assigned in the solution
MO to obtain the assignment µO . For every c ∈ S0 − Q, all the clients in SQ (c) are reassigned
                                                               ˆ that is the closest to c and
to the same center of MO as follows. Let c1 be the cluster of OPT
                                  ˆ
let c2 be the center of MO − OPT = S − S0 that is the closest to c. The clients of SQ (c) are
all assigned to either c1 or c2 :

   • If d2 (c, c1 ) ≤ d2 (c, c2 )/4, assign SQ (c) to c1 ; SQ (c) is called a type-1 cluster.

   • Otherwise (d2 (c, c1 ) > d2 (c, c2 )/4), assign SQ (c) to c2 ; SQ (c) is called a type-2 cluster.

Let T1 be the set of clients in a type-1 cluster and T2 the set of clients in a type-2 cluster.
                           ∗ − (T ∪ T ) are assigned to their closest centers in OPT,
The remaining clients in Dexp                                                       ˆ and the
                                   1   2
                           ∗
remaining clients in (D−Dexp )−(T1 ∪T2 ) are assigned to their closest centers in S −S0 . Notice
that, even neglecting the extra cost due to the consistent assignment, the above assignment
of clients in T2 is suboptimal when d(c, c2 ) > d(c, c1 ) > d(c, c2 )/4. The motivation for this
reassignment is technical. More speciﬁcally, it will be used in Lemma 9.12 to have a better
upper bound on the cost of clients in C ∗ ∩ T1 for a non-expensive cluster C ∗ (case b2). This
leads to a larger upper bound on the cost of clients in C ∗ ∩ T2 for an expensive cluster C ∗
(case a3), which is however tolerable.
    We further obtain an assignment µD of clients in MD by modifying µO as follows: each
                         ˆ is assigned to the associated dummy center by µD . So for each
client p with µO (p) ∈ OPT
                                                   ˆ in which case µD (p) equals the dummy
client p we have µD (p) = µO (p) unless µO (p) ∈ OPT
center associated with µO (p).

Sets R and U . By the deﬁnition of type-1 and type-2 cluster, we can split the set of centers
of S0 − Q into two groups R and U . Let R be the set of centers of S0 − Q whose set of clients
is completely assigned to a center of OPTˆ in the assignment µO , and let U = S0 − Q − R be
the remaining ones that are assigned to centers in S − S0 .
    We end this section by analyzing the costs of the assignments µO and µD . Recall that we
use the notation cost(MO , µO ) to denote the cost of MO equipped with assignment µO and,
similarly cost(MD , µD ) for the cost of MD equipped with assignment µD . We say that the


                                                  104
selection of (W, B, Q) is successful, if the sample W selected in Subsection 9.3 is successful, the
set of balls B from Subsection 9.4 is valid, and Q selected in this section satisﬁes the properties
of Lemma 9.11. In [CCG+ 25] there is an analogue version of the next lemma, where (besides
the mentioned diﬀerence in the constant 4 versus 5) only the sum cost(MO , µO )+cost(MD , µD )
is upper bounded. For reasons that will become clearer later, we need to bound the two
quantities separately.

9.8   Removal of Cheap Centers in S0
At this stage we have deﬁned a clustering SQ = S − Q ∪ Λ, where Λ is the set of dummy
centers. Recall that the mixed solution MO equals S − S0 ∪ OPT.     ˆ     So SQ − Λ represents
progress compared to S in that we have already removed a subset Q of S0 . Furthermore,
with respect to the assignment of clients µO , we have that the remaining centers S0 − Q,
which we want to remove, are divided into two sets R and U . The clusters with centers in
R are completely assigned (by µO ) to centers in OPT, ˆ and every cluster with center in U is
completely assigned to a center in S − S0 . The task of this section is to make further progress
by guessing the centers in U and their reassignment to centers in S − S0 . This turns out to
be a diﬃcult task, and we will instead “approximately” guess a set Ue and reassignment µ    e of
                                                                                    e
the clients of those clusters. Speciﬁcally, we will output a solution SQ∪Ue = SQ − U (obtained
by removing Ue from SQ ) and an assignment of clients µ   e to centers in SQ − Ue. This is done
through the following lemma. Recall that for c ∈ SQ , SQ (c) is the set of clients assigned to c
in SQ , i.e., closest to c among all centers in SQ .

9.10    Finding S ∗ via Submodular Optimization
In this section, we give a polynomial-time algorithm for ﬁnding the solution S ∗ by reducing
the problem to maximizing a submodular function subject to a partition matroid constraint.
Speciﬁcally, we prove the following lemma:
Lemma 9.16. We can in polynomial-time ﬁnd a clustering S ∗ with k centers of cost at most
       √
(4 + O( ε)) · opt.
    Recall that at this point the algorithm calculated the local search solution S, sampled
W , guessed B, Q, and Ue with the assignment µ      e of the clustering SQ∪Ue . We assume that
all these choices were successful guesses, i.e., that (W, B, Q, Ue, µ
                                                                    e) is successful. Further recall
the notation that we partition the clients into the clusters {Cc }c∈SQ∪Ue where Cc = {p ∈ D :
e(p) = c}.
µ
    Our goal is to give a polynomial-time algorithm that ﬁnds approximations of the sets OPT    ˆ
and R that only have slightly worse cost. We start by deﬁning the feasible set of candidates
      ˆ as a partition matroid. Recall that OPT
for OPT                                            ˆ contains one center from each ball in B.

Deﬁnition of partition matroid MB . We deﬁne the partition matroid that captures the
constraint that we wish to open a center in each ball in B. Let FB be the (multi) subset of
facility/center locations containing B ∩ F for each ball B ∈ B. If a center c is in multiple
balls in B, then FB contains one distinct copy of c for each ball. We let FB (B) ⊆ FB denote
the centers associated with B ∈ B. These sets satisfy the following two properties:
   • The sets FB (B) partition FB .
   • For B ∈ B, FB (B) contains (a copy of) every center in F ∩ B.
The ﬁrst property holds since we took a unique copy of each center for each ball, and they
are thus disjoint: FB (B) ∩ FB (B ′ ) = ∅ for distinct B, B ′ ∈ B. Indeed, while it is not better
for the cost to open multiple copies of a center, we make the copies to ensure the above two
properties. This allows us to deﬁne the partition matroid MB = (FB , I) where
                     I = {X ⊆ FB : |X ∩ FB (B)| ≤ 1 for every B ∈ B} .
Moreover, since there is exactly one ball in B for each center in OPT,ˆ            ˆ ∈ I
                                                                          we have OPT
                                                                        ∗   ˆ that belongs
(where we slightly abuse notation as we should take the copy of center c ∈ OPT
to its associated ball).

                                                    112
Core, concentrated and hit clusters. Our goal is now to deﬁne a submodular function f
so that we obtain a good approximation to OPT ˆ by maximizing f over the matroid constraint
MB . In particular, the domain of f is every subset of FB . However, we need some additional
steps before deﬁning f . In particular, we introduce the concept of the core of a cluster Cc
and the notions of concentrated and hit clusters, which allow us to simplify the structure of
centers in R. For a cluster Cc , we deﬁne the core of Cc as
                                                                    
                                                          cost(S)
                         Cc = p ∈ Cc : d (p, c) ≤ ε ·
                           core              2
                                                                       .
                                                         |R| · |Cc |
We further say that cluster Cc is concentrated if
                                      |Cccore | ≥ (1 − ε)|Cc |
and it is hit by a set X of centers if there is a point p ∈ Cccore such that
                                       d2 (p, X) < d2 (p, c) .
For shortness we will sometimes say that a center is concentrated (resp., hit), if the corre-
sponding cluster is so.

Guessing the centers of R that are not concentrated. We further simplify the task of
ﬁnding the set of centers R by guessing the centers of R that are not concentrated. Speciﬁcally,
partition the set R into R0 and R1 , where R1 contains those centers of R that are concentrated
and R0 contains those that are not. We can correctly guess R0 in polynomial time since the
following simple claim shows that it is a subset of the centers whose cluster costs at least
ε2 · opt/|R|, of which there are only O(|R|/ε2 ) many, and so guessing R0 can thus be done in
              2
time 2O(|R|/ε ) .
Claim 9.17 ([CCG+ 25]). Suppose that Cc is not concentrated. Then the cost of Cc is at least
ε2 · opt/|R|.
    Now let P be the potential centers that can be in R. Speciﬁcally, we let P be the set that
contains a center c in S − Q − Ue if Cc equals the set of clients in SQ (c), where we recall that
SQ (c) is the set of clients that are closest to c in the clustering SQ . Notice that the algorithm
has all the information S, B, Q, Ue and µ   e to calculate P. Furthermore, by Lemma 9.14, we
                               e
have that no clients from U were reassigned by µ       e to a center in R. So for c ∈ R we have
Cc = SQ (c). Hence, R ⊆ P and we can guess R0 as follows:

  Guessing R0

     1. Let C be the clusters of P whose cost is at least ε2 · opt/|R|.

     2. Output each subset of C.
By the above claim and the deﬁnition of P, one of the outputs is R0 . Furthermore, the
above guessing procedure outputs a family of polynomial many subsets. Indeed, we have
|R| ≤ log(n)/ε3 by Lemma 9.3. Moreover, the total cost of the clusters in P is O(opt). To
see this notice that the clusters corresponding to P is a subset of the clusters of SQ and the
cost of SQ is at most cost(S − S0 ∪ Λ) (since SQ ⊇ S − S0 ∪ Λ), which has cost at most
O(opt) by Lemma 9.9. This implies that |C| = O(log(n)/ε5 ), so the total number of subsets is
   −O(1)
nε       . The algorithm proceeds by trying all possible subsets in the output, and we analyze
the algorithm when it takes the correct guess of R0 .

9.11    Putting Everything Together – Proof of Theorem 1.4
We now turn to proving Theorem 1.4. We ﬁrst describe the algorithm we analyse.



                                                     115
         √
  A (4+O( ε))-Approximation for k-Means on ε/ log n- Stable Instances k, D, F, d.


     1. S ← Local Search on (k, D, F, d), see Subsection 9.1

     2. Let W be the set produced by the s∗ -D2 -Sample process (Subsection 9.3) on S.

     3. For each B∈ Lbal produced as in Lemma 9.8 on W (Subsection 9.4)

         (a) Compute the set of dummy centers Λ.
         (b) For each Q ∈ Lexp produced as in Lemma 9.11 (Subsection 9.6):
              i. For each U∈e Lcheap , with the associated µ   e, produced as in Lemma 9.14
                 (Subsection 9.8):
                 solve the Submodular Instance associated with (W, B, Q, Ue, µ   e) and com-
                 pute the resulting solution S(W,B,Q,Ue,eµ) for the k-Means problem (Subsec-
                 tion 9.10)

     4. Output the solution S(W,B,Q,Ue,eµ) of smallest cost.

    Our approach consists of running the above algorithm log n times and take the minimum
cost solution output. In the remaining, we argue that the above algorithm yields a (4 +
   √
O( ε))-approximation with probability at least (1 − ε)(1 − 1/n). Theorem 1.4 then follows
as the probability that all log n executions of the above algorithm would fail is at most
                                                                                              √
(1 − (1 − ε)(1 − 1/n))log n < 1/n, and so with high probability we output a (4 + O( ε))-
approximation.
    We ﬁrst discuss the algorithm’s running time. The local search algorithm runs in polyno-
mial time. The submodular optimization step also runs in polynomial time by Lemma 9.16.
The output of the s∗ -D2 -Sample process (which is clearly a polynomial time procedure) is a
                                                                                              −O(1)
subset of size s∗ . By Lemma 9.8, we construct Lbal in polynomial time, and |Lbal | ≤ nε            .
                                                                                    −O(1)
Similarly, by Lemma 9.11, we construct Lexp in polynomial time and |Lexp | ≤ nε           . Finally,
                                                            candidate pairs Ue, µ
                                                      −O(1)
by Lemma 9.14, we construct in polynomial-time nε                               e. It follows that
           ε −O(1)
we solve n         submodular function optimization problems, each in polynomial time, and
thus the total running time is polynomial.
    We turn to proving the approximation guarantee. We aim to show that there exists
a (W, B, Q, Ue, µ
                e) that is successful with probability at least (1 − ε/2). Assuming this, the
        √
(4 + O( ε))-approximation follows by Lemma 9.16.
    Our algorithm runs the local search algorithm and thus provides us with a 26 approxima-
tion S that satisﬁes Lemma 9.4. Our algorithm next applies the s∗ -D2 -Sample process and
by Lemma 9.5 ﬁnds a successful W with probability at least 1 − ε. Condition on having a
successful W , we then have that Lemma 9.6 holds, and so does Lemma 9.7 by combining
Lemma 9.4 and Lemma 9.6.
    Next, Lemma 9.8 implies that one set of balls B is a valid set of balls. This set of balls
induces a set of dummy centers Λ. From there, by Lemma 9.11 we have that with probability
at least 1 − 1/n, the procedure from Lemma 9.11 produces a set of centers Q such that

   1. Q ⊆ S0 , and

   2. The total cost in solution S−Q ∪ Λ of the clusters in S0 −Q is at most ε · opt.


                                                116
and so we have obtained a successful (W, B, Q) and Lemma 9.12 applies. Condition on the
event that we obtain a successful (W, B, Q), then, the procedure from Lemma 9.14 outputs a
pair (Ue, µ
          e) satisfying the properties in the claim of the lemma. We thus have a (W, B, Q, Ue, µ  e)
such that W is successful, the set of balls B from Subsection 9.4 is valid, Q selected in
Subsection 9.6 satisﬁes the properties of Lemma 9.11, and Ue, µ  e selected in this section satisﬁes
the properties of Lemma 9.14 and so (W, B, Q, Ue, µ   e) is successful. Finally, note that the only
probabilistic steps were the sampling of W and of Q, and both steps are successful with
probability at least (1 − ε) · (1 − 1/n), as required.

              √
10     (2 + O( ε))-Approximation for ( logζ n )-Stable Euclidean In-
       stances
                                                                                   √
In this section, we prove Theorem 1.5. Recall that our goal is to compute a 2+O( ε) approxi-
                                                                            √
mate solution for O(ζ/ log n) stable instances. This improves on the 4 + O( ε) approximation
in [CCG+ 26], that however works in the more general metric setting, under the only restric-
tion that the client locations are valid facility locations (while our improvement only works
for the Euclidean case). Our algorithm is the same as in [CCG+ 26], and our analysis is very
similar too: in the following we will only focus on the diﬀerences in the analysis.

Notation and Preliminaries. To simplify the formulas, we let ε be the minimum of ε and
ζ in the claim of Theorem 1.5 and further assume that ε < 1/12 is suﬃciently small. With
                                  √
this notation, we present a (2+O( ε))-approximation algorithm for ε/ log(n)-stable instances.
This implies the above theorem as any ζ/ log(n)-stable instance is also ζ ′ / log(n)-stable with
ζ ′ ≤ ζ.
     Recall that, by known reductions, we can assume that we are given a set F of tentative
centers, with D ⊆ F , and we have to choose centers from that set. Recall also that non-zero
distances are between 1 and n2 /ε2 according to Lemma 3.3. We also assume w.l.o.g. that the
optimal cost is positive (hence at least 1).
     Given a cluster C ∗ with center c∗ ∈ F , in
                                               Pthe analysis  it is convenient
                                                                      P          to consider the
actual center of mass µ(C ) of C . Obviously p∈C ∗ d (µ(C ), p) ≤ p∈C ∗ d (c∗ , p). We will
                           ∗     ∗                    2      ∗                  2

also need the following standard fact.

Lemma 10.1. For any C ∗ ⊆ D and for any c, c∗ ∈ <t ,
   X                                   X                                          X
      d2 (c, p) ≤ |C ∗ |d2 (c, µ(C)) +   d2 (µ(C ∗ ), p)≤ |C ∗ |d2 (c, µ(C ∗ )) +   d2 (c∗ , p).
     p∈C ∗                              p∈C ∗                                    p∈C ∗

    Given a set of centers A ⊆ F (not necessarily of cardinality k), we let ApP= d2 (p, A)
denote the cost associated to p in the clustering induced by A, and cost(A) := p∈D Ap be
the total cost of A. The cluster in A associated with c ∈ A is denoted by A(c). Sometimes
we will consider aP(possibly suboptimal) assignment µ : D → A of clients to centers in A, and
let cost(A, µ) := p∈D d2 (p, µ(p)) be the corresponding cost. Let OPT be a ﬁxed optimum
solution under the restriction OPT ⊆ F . Let also opt = cost(OPT) be its cost.

Overview of our approach. Our algorithm consists of a few steps which are discussed in
the following subsections. The ﬁrst step (see Section 10.1) is to compute a candidate solution
S via standard local search. We will show that the points in almost all the clusters in OPT
(excluding a logarithmic size set) pay in S roughly the same cost as in OPT. We call the

                                                117
centers OP Tpure of such clusters pure. More precisely, we will show that all the points in
OP T (c∗ ) with c∗ ∈ OPTpure can be reassigned to the same center c′ ∈ S without increasing
too much their cost.
     For each remaining impure center c, we identify a core set of clients in OPT(c), the leaders,
which are suﬃciently close to c. We distinguish the impure clusters depending on whether
the cost in S of the leaders is small (core-cheap) or large (core-expensive). The next step (see
Section 10.2) is to identify one leader in most of the core-expensive clusters of OPT. Let Λ
be the set of such leaders, and OPTexp be the core-expensive centers for which one leader was
discovered. One can reassign all the clients OPT(c∗ ) with c∗ ∈ OPTexp to the corresponding
leader while increasing their cost at most by a factor (roughly) 2. Let OPTcheap be the
remaining impure centers of OPT. We show that we can reassign all the clients OP T (c∗ )
with c∗ ∈ OPTcheap to the same center in S while increasing their cost, again, by at most a
factor (roughly) 2. The latter two mentioned factors 2 are replaced by 4 in the analysis in
[CCG+ 26].
     Consider next the solution that contains the leaders Λ plus the centers in S to which the
points in the clusters of OPTpure ∪ OPTcheap are reassigned (which are at most |OPTpure | +
|OPTcheap | many). Notice that this is a feasible solution since |Λ| = |OPTexp |. Furthermore,
it is (roughly) 2-approximate by the previous discussion. Our ﬁnal goal is to identify such a
solution, or some good enough approximation of it. More precisely, we start with the solution
                                                  ′
Λ ∪ S (which is infeasible), and remove a set Sdisc   ⊆ S of |Λ| many carefully chosen centers.
This removal is done in two steps. First (see Section 10.3), we remove a set Q ⊆ S of centers
which are expensive in S. Then (see Section 10.4), we remove other Ue centers from S, with
 e = |Λ| − |Q|.
|U|

10.1     Locally Optimal Solution and Pure Clusters
Let S be a locally optimal solution output by the following standard local search algorithm.
Here, for technical reasons and diﬀerently from [CCGG98], we use a truncated version of local
search: this way we do not need to assume that distances are integers.

  localSearch()
   1: S ← Arbitrary solution for k-Means
   2: while There exists a solution S ′ such that |S∆S ′ | ≤ 2 and cost(S ′ ) ≤ (1 − ε)cost(S)
        do
   3:    S ← S′
   4: return S

We remark that the running time of the above procedure is polynomial. It is standard fact
that local search achieves a 26 approximation for k-Means, see [GT08] (as we will see, the
speciﬁc constant in not very relevant for us).

Lemma 10.2 ([GT08]). The procedure localSearch() runs in polynomial time and returns a
feasible solution S with cost(S) ≤ 26 · opt.

    We next ﬁx S, and recall that we also ﬁxed a reference optimal solution OPT. We identify
in OPT a set of pure clusters as follows. We say that a cluster C ∗ of OPT is pure (w.r.t. S)
if there exists a cluster C ′ of S such that |C ′ ∆C ∗ | ≤ 3ε min(|C ∗ |, |C ′ |). In which case we also
say that C ′ is pure w.r.t. OPT. We say that C ′ and C ∗ are associated. By OPTpure ⊆ OPT
we denote the set of the centers of pure clusters (that we also call pure). The centers (resp,


                                                 118
clusters) which are not pure are impure. The impure centers of OPT are denoted by OPTimp .
We use a similar notation for the centers and clusters of S. The following lemma follows
from the fact that the input is β-stable for β := ε/ log n and that the solution S is a 26-
approximation by Lemma 10.2. Given a client or center a and a distance r, we let B(a, r) be
the set of clients and centers at distance at most r from a. We will use the following Lemma
from [CCG+ 25] (for technical reasons the original proof uses 25 as the approximation ratio of
the local search solution, however their proof works exactly in the same way with 26).

Lemma 10.3 ([CCG+ 25]). |OPTimp | ≤ log
                                      ε3
                                         n
                                           .

     We next deﬁne a replacement cost r(p) for each client p ∈ D. Let C ∗ be a pure cluster of
OPT and C ′ be the associated cluster of S. Let c∗ be the center of C ∗ and c′ be the center of
C ′ . We deﬁne t(c∗ ) := c′ . For any client p ∈ C ∗ , let r(p) := d2 (p, c′ ) = d2 (p, t(c∗ )). For any
impure cluster C ∗ with center c∗ , we let t(c∗ ) be the center of S closest to µ(C ∗ ), and deﬁne
r(p) = d2 (p, t(c∗ )) for each p ∈ C ∗ .
     The following lemma is the only one that requires that the solution S is obtained via local
search (the proof of the previous lemma only used that it was a constant-factor approximation).
In words, it says that S approximates the connection cost of clients belonging to pure clusters
of the optimal solution almost perfectly. We will use the following Lemma from [CCG+ 25].
                                P           P                     P               P
Lemma 10.4 ([CCG+ 25]). c∗ ∈OPTpure p∈OPT(c∗ ) r(p) ≤ c∗ ∈OPTpure p∈OPT(c∗ ) OPTp +
    √
O( ε) · opt .

10.2    Leaders’ Sampling of Leader-Expensive Clusters of OPT
Let OPTimp = OPT − OPTpure be the impure centers of OPT. We next partition OPTimp
according to the following deﬁnition. Consider any c∗ ∈ OPTimp and set C ∗ = OPT(c∗ ). We
let avg(c∗ ) be the squared distance from µ(C ∗ ) to the ε|C ∗ |-th closest client of C ∗ to µ(C ∗ ).
We further let OPTlead (c∗ ) be the set of clients of C ∗ at a squared distance at most avg(c∗ )
from µ(C ∗ ): we call such clients leaders of the cluster. So avg(c∗ ) is the maximum squared
distance from a leader to µ(C ∗ ). Also note that we have, |OPTlead (c∗ )| ≥ ε|C ∗ |. We thus
have that avg(c∗ ) is roughly upper bounded by the average optimal cost of C ∗ , namely

    Our next goal is to identify one leader for each leader-expensive center. We will actually
miss each one of the latter centers with some small probability, however this is not a problem
as we will see. The idea is to sample each client p with probability proportional to its cost Sp
for a suﬃciently large number of times: this way it is unlikely to not hit each set OPTlead (c∗ )
of a leader-expensive center c∗ . Then one extracts a proper set of leaders by brute force. Here
we critically exploit the fact that there is only a logarithmic number of impure centers (by
Lemma 10.3), hence of leader-expensive centers. We will use the following result in [CCG+ 26]
that works when S is O(1) approximate.
Lemma 10.6. [CCG+ 26] There is a polynomial-time procedure to compute a collection Llead
of subsets of clients such that, with probability at least 1 − ε, at least one Λ ∈ Llead is valid,
i.e., it satisﬁes the following property. Let OPTexp ⊆ OPTlexp be the centers with exactly one
leader in Λ and OPTmiss = OPTlexp − OPTexp . Then

10.3    Removal of Expensive Centers of Sdisc
Let us start with the tentative solution S ∪ Λ (which is infeasible since it contains more than
k centers). Our goal is to remove a proper subset Sdisc  ′  of |Λ| many centers from S, so that
            ′
S ∪ Λ − Sdisc , which is a feasible solution, is roughly 2-approximate. Ideally we would like to
          ′
choose Sdisc  = Sdisc , so that Lemma 10.8 directly gives what we need. We will rather ﬁnd
        ′
some Sdisc which is comparably good. For a subset A ⊆ S, let SA := S ∪ Λ − A. As usual, we
let SA (c) be the cluster in SA associated with the center c ∈ SA .

                                                           121
    Consider any cluster C of S ∪ Λ with center c ∈ Sdisc . For reasons that will become clearer
                                               ′
later, if we remove c (which is then added to Sdisc ), we would like to assign all the clients in C
                     ′
to the same center c in S ∪ Λ − {c}. Similarly for the later steps. However, this might be too
expensive when the cost of C in S ∪ Λ is large. Our next goal is to identify a subset Q ⊆ Sdisc
of centers such that, after removing Q from S ∪Λ, we enforce in some sense the above property.
More precisely, we desire that the cost of the clusters with center in Sdisc − Q in the solution
SQ := S ∪ Λ − Q is very small compared with opt. This is done via the following lemma in
[CCGG98] that is based on the fact that cost(Λ ∪ S − Sdisc ) ≤ O(1) · opt.
                                                                              O(1)
Lemma 10.9 ([CCG+ 26]). There is a procedure that runs in time n1/ε                and produces a
                                O(1)
collection Lexp of at most n1/ε      subsets of S such that with probability at least 1 − 1/n there
exists Q ∈ Lexp satisfying
   1. Q ⊆ Sdisc , and
   2. The total    in SQ of the clusters with centers in U := Sdisc − Q is at most ε · opt,
             Pcost P
      namely c∈U p∈SQ (c) d2 (p, c) ≤ ε · opt.

10.4    Removal of Cheap Centers from S
Our algorithm considers each choice of Q ∈ Lexp . We next assume that there exists at
least one such Q that satisﬁes the properties in Lemma 10.9 (event which happens with
high probability), and focus on the execution of the algorithm corresponding to the latter Q.
Consider the (possibly infeasible) solution SQ . Our target reference solution is SSdisc , which is
roughly 2-approximate. Let U := Sdisc −Q. Ideally we would like to remove U from SQ , hence
getting the desired solution SQ∪U = SSdisc . We will rather remove a subset U ′ of the same
cardinality, whose removal leads to a solution of cost only marginally larger than the cost of
SSdisc . In order to simplify our argument, for each candidate set of centers U ′ ⊆ S − Q with
|U ′ | = |U|, we will restrict our attention to suboptimal assignments µU ′ of points to centers
in SQ∪U ′ such that, for each c ∈ U ′ , all the points p ∈ SQ (c) are assigned to the closest center
t(c, U ′ ) ∈ SQ∪U ′ to c. The remaining points are assigned by µU ′ to the same center as in SQ .
We call consistent an assignment µU ′ of this type, and we denote the consistent cost of the
corresponding solution by
                                                    X
                                  cost′ (SQ∪U ′ ) =    d2 (p, µU ′ (p)).
                                                p∈D

Obviously cost′ (SQ∪U ′ ) is an upper bound on the actual cost cost(SQ∪U ′ ) of the solution
SQ∪U ′ . The next lemma shows that, for U ′ = U , the consistent cost is small enough. The
ﬁrst inequality in the following lemma follows directly from an analogous lemma in [CCG+ 26],
while the second from Lemma 10.8.3.
                                                     √                √
Lemma 10.10. cost′ (SQ∪U ) ≤ cost(SQ∪U ) + O( ε)opt ≤ (2 + O( ε))opt.
    Our next goal is to compute a set Ue of cardinality |U| such that
                                                               √
                            cost′ (SQ∪Ue ) ≤ cost′ (SQ∪U ) + O( ε)opt.
This is done via the following lemma from [CCG+ 26].
Lemma 10.11 ([CCG+ 26]). Assume that Λ and Q satisfy the properties mentioned before.
Then there is a polynomial-time procedure that computes a polynomial size collection Lcheap of
subsets of S−Q, each of size |Λ|−|Q|, such that at least one Ue ∈ Lcheap satisﬁes cost′ (SQ∪Ue ) ≤
                  √
cost′ (SQ∪U ) + O( ε)opt.

                                               122
10.5    Putting Everything Together – Proof of Theorem 1.5
We next summarize the overall algorithm:

  apxStableEuclideankM eans()
   1: Lapx ← ∅
   2: S ← localSearch() (see Subsection 10.1)
          1
   3: for 2ε ln n many times do
   4:    Compute Llead according to Lemma 10.6 (see Subsection 10.2)
   5:    for all Λ ∈ Llead do
   6:       Compute Lexp according to Lemma 10.9 (see Subsection 10.3)
   7:       for all Q ∈ Lexp do
   8:            Compute Lcheap according to Lemma 10.11 (see Subsection 10.4)
   9:            for all Ue ∈ Lcheap do
  10:                                          e
                    Lapx ← Laxp ∪ {S ∪ Λ − Q − U}
  11: return the cheapest solution AP X ∈ Lapx

   We are now ready to prove Theorem 1.5.

Proof of Theorem 1.5. Consider the algorithm apxStableEuclideankM eans(). Its running
time is polynomial since the called procedures run in polynomial time (see Lemmas 10.2,
10.6, 10.9, and 10.11), which also implies that each considered set Llead , Lexp and Lcheap has
polynomial size.
    Notice that all the solutions in Lapx (including AP X) contain at most k centers, hence
they are deterministically feasible. Consider one execution of the outer for loop. Suppose
that the event from Lemma 10.6 holds for some Λ ∈ Llead , which happens with probability at
least 1 − ε, and focus on that choice of Λ in Line 5. Notice that under this condition Lemmas
10.7 and 10.8 hold. Suppose also that the event from Lemma 10.9 holds for some Q ∈ Lexp ,
which happens with probability at least 1 − 1/n, and focus on that choice of Q in Line 7.
Under these assumptions, which hold with probability at least 1 − 2ε altogether, there exists
Ue ∈ Lcheap which satisﬁes the conditions of Lemma 10.11: let us focus on the execution of
Line 9 for this value of Ue, and consider the corresponding solution AP X = S ∪ Λ − Q − Ue.
Observe that, under the above assumptions, Lemma 10.10 also holds. Thus
                               Lem.10.11                     √      Lem.10.10        √
   cost(AP X) ≤ cost′ (AP X)      ≤        cost′ (SQ∪U ) + O( ε)opt    ≤      (2 + O( ε))opt.
                                     1
Since the outer for loop is executed 2ε ln n many times, the probability that no AP X ∈ Lapx
         √
is 2 + O( ε) approximate is at most 1/n. The claim follows.


Acknowledgement
The authors developed the new algorithm, found evidence that it might improve upon the
previous work, and established the (3 + ln 2 + ε)-approximation for the Euclidean case without
any help of AI.
   For the metric case, the authors developed a (5 + ε)-analysis without any help of AI. The
better (4.9 + ε)-analysis was obtained by GPT 5.2/5.4 Pro/Thinking after several rounds of
communication between the authors and it. The writeup for the (4.9 + ε)-analysis has been


                                                123
veriﬁed and revised by the authors. The two analyses follow quite diﬀerent routes within the
spectral analysis framework and were found by diﬀerent authors simultaneously.
    Aditya Anand, Euiwoong Lee, and Amatya Sharma were supported in part by NSF grant
2236669. Ruiquan Gao was supported by NSF CCF-2112824 and Aviad Rubinstein’s David
and Lucile Packard Fellowship. Fabrizio Grandoni was partially supported by the SNF Grants
200021-200731 and 200021-236706. Ernest van Wijland was supported in part by the French
PEPR integrated projects EPIQ (ANR-22-PETQ-0007).

A     Proof of Theorems 1.1 and 1.2
Theorem 1.1. For any ε > 0, there is a polynomial-time algorithm that yields a (3+ln 2+ ε)-
approximation for Euclidean k-Means.
Theorem 1.2. For any ε > 0, there is a polynomial-time algorithm that yields a (4.9 + ε)-
approximation for Metric k-Means.
Proofs of Theorems 1.1 and 1.2. We compute a set of feasible solutions, and return the cheap-
est one. Let ∆ be the maximum extra number of centers computed by the algorithm from
Theorem 1.3 (this number is independent from k). Let k ′ = max{1, k − ∆}. Obtain a solution
by computing the optimum solution with one center if k ′ = 1, and otherwise by running the
algorithm from Theorem 1.3 with target number of centers being k ′ (notice that it returns a
solution with at most k centers, and is hence feasible).
    We run the algorithm from either of Theorems 1.4 and 1.5 for every integer k ′′ ∈ (k ′ , k]
(thus obtaining solutions with k ′′ ≤ k centers, hence feasible).
    If OPTk′ ≤ (1 + ε)OPTk , the ﬁrst solution is (1 + ε)Γ approximate. Otherwise, OPTk′ >
(1 + ε)OPTk . Thus there exists an integer k ′′ ∈ (k ′ , k] such that OPTk′′ ≤ (1 + ε)OPTk
and OPTk′′ −1 ≥ (1 + β)OPTk′′ for β = Ω(ε/∆) = Ω(ε4 / log n). For that value of k ′′ the
corresponding instance of Metric k-Means is β-stable, hence we can compute a solution with
                     √                             √
cost at most (4 + O( ε))OPTk′′ ≤ (1 + ε)(4 + O( ε))OPTk using Theorem 1.4, which yields
             √                                                                           √
a (1 + ε)(4 + ε) approximation. Similarly, in the Euclidean case, we get a (1 + ε)(2 + O( ε))
approximation using Theorem 1.5. The proof is then complete after suitably rescaling ε.

                          X                        X
                            w(j)Pjℓ (xj − xℓ )2 ≤     w(j)x2j .

Our proof works for the more general case where xi ∈ Rdim for some dim ≥ 1.

B.1.1    Preliminaries on weighted k-means
We deﬁne the weighted versions of Euclidean k-Means that will be useful later in the proof.

Deﬁnition B.1 (weighted discrete Euclidean k-Means). The input consists of a set of clients
D, a set of facilities F and their locations x : D ∪ F → Rdim . In addition there is a weight
function w : D → R>0 mapping each client to a positive weight. The goal of the discrete
Euclidean k-Means problem is to ﬁnd a set of k centers S ⊆ F such that |S| = k and the
following objective is minimized:
                                   X
                                       w(j) min kxi − xj k22 .
                                               i∈S
                                   j∈D

Deﬁnition B.2 (weighted continuous Euclidean k-Means). The input consists of a set of
clients D and their locations x : D → Rdim . In addition there is a weight function w : D → R>0
mapping each client to a positive weight. The goal of the weighted continuous Euclidean k-
Means problem is to ﬁnd a set of k centers S ⊆ Rdim such that |S| = k and the following
objective is minimized:
                                    X
                                       w(j) · min kc − xj k22 .
                                                c∈S
                                   j∈D

In particular, an instance is unweighted if w(j) = 1 for any client j ∈ D.

   One technical lemma we will use later is a characterization of the optimal cost of a cluster
consisting of a set of clients among all possible choices of the center.

B.2     Proof of Lemma 5.20
In the rest of this subsection, we will prove the above lemma. We will use the same set of
non-negative parameters ptjℓ and βt as in the proof of Lemma 4.11 (in terms of (w(t))t∈[s] ,
(DC t )t∈[s] and (IC t )t∈[s] ). One property we will use about the β-parameters is the following:


Lemma B.6. For any t ∈ [a + 1 : s − 1], we have βt = 0.
Proof. For any t ∈ [a + 1 : s − 1], we have t ∈ IC s . Therefore, according to our deﬁnition
of πt (ℓ), we have πt (ℓ) = πt (s) for any ℓ ∈ [a + 1 : s]. Further, ηsℓ = w(ℓ)πt (s) for any
ℓ ∈ [a + 1 : s]. Note that βs is deﬁned by βs ηss = w(s). We have βs = 1/πt (s). Therefore, for
any ℓ ∈ [a + 1 : s − 1], we have βs ηsℓ = w(ℓ). Using the recurrence relation in Equation (15),
we have βt ηtℓ = 0 for any ℓ ∈ [a + 1 : s − 1]. Because ηtt > 0, we have βt = 0 for any
t ∈ [a + 1 : s − 1].

Lemma 4.11, with a (1 + 2ε) factor before each (rj t + d(j, ℓ))2 .

C.2     Weighted: Full Proof
In this subsection, we prove Lemma 4.12. Deﬁne

                 def                          def

    As brieﬂy discussed in the overview in Subsection 2.1, the proof has four steps. First, from
the inequalities at time αt − ε given in condition (3) of Lemma 4.12, we derive a family of con-
straints (Ptw ) by using the triangle inequality to lower bound the terms involving rjt . Second,
we choose coeﬃcients (β)t∈[s] so that, in the weighted combination of these constraints, the
coeﬃcient of each αt∗ is exactly w(t). Third, after collecting
                                                       p       terms, we obtain a quadratic form
in the distances to the facility i. After rescaling by w(t), the quadratic form is represented
by an ordinary symmetric nonnegative matrix. Finally, we bound the top eigenvalue of the
corresponding matrix by the Collatz–Wielandt Formula, which yields the claimed constant.

Step 1: From (Itw ) to (Ptw ). For each t ∈ [s], condition (3) gives
